Burklund–Lin–Wang–Xu Methods in the
Cofiber-of-Tau Formalism

Abstract
We reinvestigate the theory of spectral sequences by studying the ( ∞ , 1 ) (\infty,1) -category of filtered spectra 𝖥𝗂𝗅𝖲𝗉 \mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp} through the cofiber-of- τ \tau formalism of Burklund–Isaksen–Pstragowski–Wang–Xu. In this framework, we define and analyze hidden extensions along arbitrary maps of filtered spectra, establishing computational principles that extend the generalized Leibniz rule and the generalized Mahowald trick of Lin–Wang–Xu [ Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire ] , as well as Burklund’s Leibniz rule for total differentials [ Burklund-cookware ] , from the Adams spectral sequence to this broader setup. Our formulation uses a more refined, layered notion of extension, which slightly sharpens these statements even for the Adams spectral sequence. As an application, we study equivariant slice spectral sequences and obtain new families of “exotic transfer” differentials in the C 4 C_{4} -slice spectral sequences for the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel theories BP ( ( C 4 ) ) ​ ⟨ m ⟩ \mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle for every m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 .



Yuchen Wu

1   Introduction

1.1   Background

Spectral sequences (SS) are ubiquitous computational devices across mathematics, and they play a particularly central role in algebraic topology. A striking illustration is the Kervaire invariant problem: Geometrically, this is about whether there exists a smooth closed framed manifold of dimension
2^{j+1}-2
that cannot be converted to a homotopy sphere via framed surgery, while the works of Kervaire–Milnor
[
KM63
]
and Browder
[
Bro69
]
translate this into deciding whether the Kervaire class
h_{j}^{2}
in the
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-Adams spectral sequence of the sphere spectrum is a permanent cycle. For
j\leqslant 5
, these classes are permanent cycles due to the computation of Mahowald–Tangora
[
MT67
]
and Barratt–Jones–Mahowald
[
BJM84
]
, while the remaining cases were open for decades, until two major breakthroughs in the next century.

In
[
HHR-paper
]
, Hill, Hopkins and Ravenel solve the Kervaire invariant problem for
j\geqslant 7
:

Theorem 1.1

(Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel)
.

For
j\geqslant 7
,
h_{j}^{2}
is not a permanent cycle. Thus, every closed framed manifold of dimension
2^{j+1}-2
can be converted to a homotopy sphere via framed surgery.

The resolution of Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel is based on equivariant homotopy theory. They carry out an extensive study of the
equivariant slice spectral sequences
, and they show that the Kervaire classes are detected in the slice spectral sequence of a certain genuine equivariant spectrum, namely the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel theory
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(G)\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
with
G=C_{8},m=1
. The slice spectral sequence analysis further shows that a suitable localization of this spectrum has a gap and a
256
-periodicity in its equivariant homotopy groups, from which they conclude that
h_{j}^{2}
does not survive for each
j\geqslant 7
.

The last open case, i.e. the case
j=6
, is solved by Lin, Wang and Xu in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
:

Theorem 1.2

(Lin–Wang–Xu)
.

For
j=6
,
h_{6}^{2}
is a permanent cycle. Thus, there exist closed framed manifolds of dimension
126
that cannot be converted to a homotopy sphere via framed surgery.

The resolution of Lin–Wang–Xu stays in the realm of Adams SS. Their key technique lies in the systematic study of
(hidden) extensions
in the Adams SS, which record information about a map
f\colon X\to Y
between finite spectra that is not detected by the induced map
f\colon E_{2,\mathrm{ASS}}^{*,*}(X)\to E_{2,\mathrm{ASS}}^{*,*}(Y)
. More precisely, for such a map
f\colon X\to Y
, they established

•

The
Generalized Mahowald trick (GMT)
, which yields a correspondence between extensions along the map
f
and the Adams differentials of
\cofib(f)
.

•

The
Generalized Leibniz rule (GLR)
, which combines short Adams differentials for
X
and extensions along
f
to produce long Adams differentials for
Y
.

Given as input the Adams
E_{2}
-pages and
d_{2}
differentials of many finite spectra (computed by Lin’s program using
secondary Steenrod operations, cf.
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-machine
]
), these two techniques generate lots of longer differentials, which clarify the pattern of Adams differentials of
\mathbb{S}^{0}
around stem
126
.

1.2   Summary of the results

This paper extends the study of hidden extensions from the Adams SS to the standard SS associated to arbitrary filtered spectra, and applies this framework to equivariant slice SS computations.

In
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
, hidden extensions in the Adams SS are characterized using
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-synthetic spectra. The underlying strategy goes back to the cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism in the stable
\mathbb{C}
-motivic homotopy category by Gheorghe, Isaksen, Wang and Xu in
[
Isaksen-stable-stems
]
and
[
GWX
]
, while it has been further developed in a series of works by, among others, Burklund, Isaksen, Pstragowski, Wang and Xu in
[
Pst23
]
,
[
GIKR
]
,
[
BHS1
]
,
[
Burklund-Xu
]
,
[
BIX
]
and
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
. Throughout this literature, the common formal input is a stable presentably (symmetric) monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a (symmetric) monoidal functor from the poset
(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)
to
\mathcal{C}
. In this paper, we work in the universal such setting, namely the
\infty
-category of filtered spectra
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fun}((\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Sp})
together with the spectral Yoneda embedding
\gamma\colon(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
.

Many key constructions in synthetic spectra admit a universal, and often simpler, formulation in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. There is a bigraded family of invertible objects

\mathbb{S}^{n,w}=\Sigma^{n}\gamma(w),

which induces bigraded suspensions and bigraded homotopy groups.
The
\gamma
-image of the map
(-1)\to 0
defines a ‘‘deformation parameter’’
1
1

1

We choose to write
\tau
because of its origination from the map
\tau
in the stable motivic homotopy category over
\mathbb{C}
.

\tau\colon\mathbb{S}^{0,-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0},
and hence natural transformations
\tau^{k}\colon\Sigma^{0,-k}\to\mathrm{id}
for
k\geq 1
.
Moreover, from the composable pair

\gamma((-r)\to(-k)\to 0)=\mathbb{S}^{0,-r}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,-k}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0}

one extracts a fundamental distinguished triangle

\Sigma^{0,-k}X/\tau^{\,r-k}\xrightarrow{\tau^{k}}X/\tau^{\,r}\xrightarrow{\rho_{k}^{r}}X/\tau^{\,k}\xrightarrow{\delta_{k}^{\,r-k}}\Sigma^{1,-k}X/\tau^{\,r-k},

which defines the “projection”
\rho=\rho_{k}^{r}
and the “total differential”
\delta_{k}=\delta_{k}^{\,r-k}
.
On the other hand, each
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
has an associated “standard spectral sequence”
\{E_{r}^{*,*}(X)\}_{r\geq 2}
, and this construction encompasses most spectral sequences encountered in practice; see Example
2.54
, Example
2.65
, and Definition
5.4
.
The
cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism
in the sense of Burklund–Isaksen–Pstragowski–Wang–Xu (BIPWX)
is the study of the standard SS through the bigraded homotopy groups of
X/\tau^{\,r}
for
1\leq r\leq\infty
, together with the operators
\tau^{k}
,
\rho
, and
\delta_{k}
. In Theorem
2.26
we construct the standard SS from these data. Conversely, the bigraded homotopy groups and these operators can be read off from the standard SS due to

Theorem A

(Bockstein dictionary; Precise formulation in Theorems
2.32
,
2.34
,
2.38
; See also Summary
2.36
)
.

Suppose
1\leqslant r\leqslant\infty,1\leqslant k<\infty
and
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
(subject to some mild assumptions if
r=\infty
). Then

1.

We have
E_{2}^{s,t}(X)\cong\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau)
.

2.

Each
d_{r}
-cycle
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}\subset E_{2}^{s,t}
admits a lift
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
along the projection
\rho\colon X/\tau^{r}\to X/\tau
. Conversely, each bigraded stem
\alpha\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
is of the form
\tau^{a}[y]
for some
y\in Z_{r-a}^{s+a,t+a}
.

3.

The operator
\tau^{k}\colon X/\tau^{r}\to X/\tau^{r+k}
kills the lifts of
(k+1)
-boundaries
B_{k+1}^{s,t}\subset E_{2}^{s,t}
(up to some
\tau
-divisible stems), while the projection
\rho=\rho^{r}_{k}\colon X/\tau^{r}\to X/\tau^{k}
removes the
\tau^{k}
-divisible stems.

4.

The total differential
\delta_{k}=\delta_{k}^{r}\colon X/\tau^{k}\to\Sigma^{1,-k}X/\tau^{r}
repackages the data of
d_{2},\ldots,d_{k+r}
, in the sense that
\delta_{k}^{r}\tau^{a}[x]=\tau^{b}[y]
if and only if
d_{k-a+b+1}(x)=y
in the standard SS.

Remark 1.3
.

The Bockstein dictionary results are closely related to
rigidity
results for trigraded SS. For a more detailed account of the relevant history, including work of Hu–Kriz–Ormsby, Isaksen, Burklund–Hahn–Senger, Burklund–Xu, and Carrick–Davies–van Nigtevecht, see Remark
2.42
.

The functoriality of these bigraded homotopy groups allows us to make sense of “extensions of standard SS” along any map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
: there is a
j
-th layer extension along
f
on the
E_{r+1}
-page of filtration jump
k
from
x\in E_{2}^{*,*}(X)
to
y\in E_{2}^{*,*}(Y)
if there exist lifts
[x]\in\pi_{**}(X/\tau^{r}),[y]\in\pi_{**}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
so that
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y]
. In terms of this, we have the following

Theorem B

(Generalized Leibniz rule; Precise formulation in Theorems
3.24
and
3.27
)
.

Suppose
f\colon X\to Y
is a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
,
x,x_{\infty}\in E_{2}^{*,*}(X)
and
y,y_{\infty}\in E_{2}^{*,*}(Y)
, such that

•

d_{r+1}(x)=x_{\infty}
in the standard SS of
X
.

•

There is a finite page
f
-extension
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y
of filtration jump
m
.

Then we have the following:

1.

If there is an
E_{\infty}
-page
f
-extension
x_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y_{\infty}
of filtration jump
l
, and certain no crossing conditions are satisfied, then
d_{r-m+l+1}(y)=y_{\infty}
in the standard SS of
Y
.

2.

Conversely, if
d_{r-m+l+1}(y)=y_{\infty}
in the standard SS of
Y
, and some other no crossing conditions are satisfied, then there is an
E_{\infty}
-page
f
-extension
x_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y_{\infty}
of filtration jump
l
.

Theorem C

(Generalized Mahowald trick; Precise formulation in Theorems
4.23
and
4.26
)
.

Suppose
f\colon X\to Y
is a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
with cofiber
Z
,
x\in E_{2}^{*,*}(X)
,
y\in E_{2}^{*,*}(Y)
,
\overline{x},\overline{y}\in E_{2}^{*,*}(Z)
, so that

•

There is a finite page extension along
g\colon Y\to Z
from
y
to
\overline{y}
of filtration jump
m
.

•

There is a finite page extension along
h\colon Z\to\Sigma^{1,0}X
from
\overline{x}
to
x
of filtration jump
l
.

Write
r=n+m+l
for some
n\geqslant 0
. Then we have the following:

1.

If
d_{r+1}(\overline{x})=\overline{y}
in the standard SS of
Z
, and certain no crossing conditions are satisfied, then there is a finite page
f
-extension
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y
of filtration jump
n
.

2.

Conversely, if there is a finite page
f
-extension
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y
of filtration jump
n
, and some other no crossing conditions are satisfied, then
d_{r+1}(\overline{x})=\overline{y}
in the standard SS of
Z
.

Here “no crossing conditions” serve as necessary inputs to eliminate the potential discrepancy (nonuniqueness in the choice of lift) along the Bockstein dictionary (Theorem
A
) translation.

Remark 1.4
.

These results generalize Lin–Wang–Xu’s Adams SS versions
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Theorems 6.1 and 6.12]
. For historical attributions concerning Theorem
B
, including works of Chua, Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel, Burklund–Hahn–Senger, Burklund–Xu, Marek, Isaksen–Kong–Li–Ruan–Zou, and Carrick–Davies–van Nigtevecht, see Remark
3.30
. For the corresponding discussion of Theorem
C
, including works of Mahowald, Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel, Behrens, and Ma, see Remark
4.29
.

Even for the Adams SS, Theorems
B
and
C
are slightly sharper than the corresponding results of
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
, since they are formulated in terms of
layered extensions
, which keep track of the relevant
\tau
-power in a hidden extension. This refinement distinguishes some configurations that count as crossings in the sense of
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
but not in the layered framework; see the discussion on crossings at the end of Example
3.5
. Consequently, the same conclusions can hold under weaker hypotheses, as demonstrated in Example
3.29
.

Remark 1.5
.

The mechanism underlying this layered refinement already appears implicitly in concrete computations: multiplying by a suitable power of
\tau
can sometimes remove a crossing obstruction at the relevant bidegree. Instances of this idea occur in
[
Burklund-hidden-extension
]
,
[
BIX
]
and
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, § 7]
. The role of layered extensions is to isolate this mechanism systematically.

Before moving on to the remaining main results, we pause to highlight two technical inputs that underlie the theorems, which are developed in two appendices.

(A).

We compare our approach to hidden extensions with the approach in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
in Remark
3.3
. This relies on the identification of the synthetic Adams SS of
\nu X/\tau^{r}
(
1\leqslant r\leqslant\infty
) with the corresponding trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS that is compatible with the abutments, and we prove such a result in Theorem
A.9
. In Appendix
A
we also establish the “rigidity” of trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS in general,
and we apply this to reprove the fact that the synthetic Adams SS of
\nu X
(and also
\nu X/\tau^{r}
) determines, and is determined by the classical Adams SS of
X
.

(B).

Theorem
B
and Theorem
C
both come from a careful Bockstein dictionary translation applied to the (more intuitive) blueprint results in terms of bigraded stems. The Blueprint
3.20
for Theorem
B
follows from the naturality of the total differential operator
\delta
; on the other hand, the Blueprint
4.20
for Theorem
C
is technically more subtle.

Indeed, to identify the bigraded homotopy groups lifting the extensions and/or differentials, one would need to construct a delicate homotopy between different internal isomorphisms in a
2
-dimensional coherent enhancement of distinguished triangles, which we refer to as the “coherent Mahowald trick”.

The paper
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
resolves this challenge by restricting to the special situation
where the “
2
d triangle” comes from the smash product of two classical distinguished triangles, for which a model categorical argument is sketched in
[
May-additivity
, Proof of (TC3) in § 6]
.
In this paper, we achieve further generality: we make sense of the concept “
2
d triangle” in an arbitrary stable
\infty
-category in §
4.1
, state the coherent Mahowald trick (cf. Theorem
4.17
) in this context, and provide a model-independent proof in Appendix
B
.

Besides the generalized Leibniz rule and the generalized Mahowald trick, Burklund–Isaksen–Pstragowski–Wang–Xu’s cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism also allows us to generalize the classical Leibniz rule along another direction in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, extending the
Leibniz rule for total differentials
proposed by Burklund in
[
Burklund-cookware
, Claim 3.3.3]
:

Theorem D

(Leibniz rule for total differentials; Precise formulation in Theorem
2.50
)
.

Suppose
F\colon X\otimes Y\to T
is a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. For each
r\geqslant 1
,
the induced pairing

F=F/\tau^{r}\colon\pi_{**}(X/\tau^{r})\times\pi_{**}(Y/\tau^{r})\to\pi_{**}(T/\tau^{r})

satisfies the Leibniz rule for total differentials
\delta_{r}=\delta_{r}^{r}
, in the sense that

\delta_{r}F(\alpha,\beta)=F(\delta_{r}(\alpha),\beta)+(-1)^{|\alpha|}F(\alpha,\delta_{r}(\beta)).

In particular, the induced pairing on the
E_{r+1}
-page satisfies the classical Leibniz rule.

Remark 1.6
.

A special case of Theorem
2.50
, where
F
is the multiplication for a (
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-)synthetic ring spectrum, is proved by Carrick–Davies–van Nigtevecht in
[
CDvN2
, Theorem 2.34]
.

In Appendix
, we recast the Leibniz rule for ordinary/total differentials as a categorical statement, expressed in terms of the lax
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-monoidality of certain functors.

Theorem E

(Lax monoidality for the standard SS functor and its total-differential refinements; Precise formulation in Theorems
,
and
)
.

Take
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}\cup\{\infty\}
. If
\mathcal{E}
is a presentably
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-monoidal
t
-
\infty
-category, then the assignments

\displaystyle A_{r+1}^{*,*}\colon\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit})^{E_{r+1}},

\displaystyle\quad X\mapsto\{(A_{r+1}^{s,t}(X)=\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r}),\delta_{r}^{r})\}_{s,t\in\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle E_{r+1}^{*,*}\colon\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit})^{E_{r+1}},

\displaystyle\quad X\mapsto\{(E_{r+1}^{s,t}(X),d_{r+1})\}_{s,t\in\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle E_{*}^{*,*}\colon\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathsf{SS}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}),

\displaystyle\quad X\mapsto\{(E_{r+1}^{s,t}(X),d_{r+1})\}_{r\geqslant 1,s,t\in\mathbb{Z}}

define lax
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-monoidal functors for each
r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
. Here the target
\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit})^{E_{r+1}}
is the
1
-category of graded chain complexes
\{(M^{s,t},d_{r+1})\}
valued in
\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}
,
and
\mathsf{SS}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit})
is the
1
-truncated
\infty
-operad over
\mathbb{E}_{n}
of spectral sequences valued in
\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}
.

Suppose moreover
\mathcal{E}
is
\mathbb{E}_{n+1}
-monoidal and it is equipped with an
\mathbb{E}_{n+1}
-monoidal retract
V\mapsto|V|
of the canonical map
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathbb{S})\to\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})
. Then the assignments

\displaystyle A_{r+1}^{*,\filledstar}\colon\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})}^{E_{r+1}}\mathsf{Ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}),

\displaystyle\quad X\mapsto\{(A_{r+1}^{s,V}(X)=\pi_{V-s,|V|}(X/\tau^{r}),\delta_{r}^{r})\}_{s\in\mathbb{Z},V\in\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})}

\displaystyle E_{r+1}^{*,\filledstar}\colon\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})}^{E_{r+1}}\mathsf{Ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}),

\displaystyle\quad X\mapsto\{(E_{r+1}^{s,V}(X)=E_{r+1}^{s,0}(\hom_{\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})}(\mathbb{S}^{V,|V|},X)),d_{r+1})\}_{s\in\mathbb{Z},V\in\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})}

\displaystyle E_{*}^{*,\filledstar}\colon\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathsf{SS}_{\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}),

\displaystyle\quad X\mapsto\{(E_{r+1}^{s,V}(X),d_{r+1})\}_{r\geqslant 1,s\in\mathbb{Z},V\in\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})}

define lax
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-monoidal functors for each
r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
. Here the target
\mathsf{Gr}_{\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})}^{E_{r+1}}\mathsf{Ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit})
is the
1
-category of
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})
-graded chain complexes
\{(M^{s,V},d_{r+1})\}
valued in
\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}
for which the sign
C_{2}
-action on
V\in\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})
induces the sign
C_{2}
-action on each
M^{s,V}\in\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}
, and
\mathsf{SS}_{\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})}(\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit})
is the
1
-truncated
{\infty}
-operad over
\mathbb{E}_{n}
of
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{E})
-graded spectral sequences valued in
\mathcal{E}^{\heartsuit}
.

Remark 1.7
.

The lax symmetric monoidality of
E_{*}^{*,*}\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{SS}
was first proved by Hedenlund
[
Hed20
, Theorem II.3.5]
, and subsequently revisited by Antieau
[
Ant24
, Theorem 8.9]
. Their proofs are based on the streamlined décalage functor
\textrm{D\'{e}c}\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, while our proof, based on total differentials, is different in nature. The décalage construction is well suited for extracting the standard SS itself; however, since the
r
-fold décalage of a filtered spectrum
X
does not retain the earliest
r
pages of the standard SS, hidden extensions and total differentials are not visible from décalage alone.

From a different perspective, in §
2.4
we demonstrate that our results in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
apply equally well to
the study of an arbitrary presentable
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-module, namely any presentable stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a cocontinuous monad
T\in\mathsf{Alg}(\mathsf{Fun}^{L}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}))
such that
\fib(\eta\colon\mathrm{id}\to T)
is an invertible functor (cf. Theorem
2.67
and Corollary
2.68
).

Having all these computational methods at hand, we test them in
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{Sp}^{G})
with equivariant slice spectral sequences for the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel theories
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(G)\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
, which are genuine
G
-spectra (for any
2
-group
G
equipped with a fixed inclusion
C_{2}\subset G
) introduced in
[
HHR-paper
]
. Such computations have received significant attention for two reasons:

•

Hill, Hopkins and Ravenel resolve the Kervaire invariant problem in the negative for
j\geqslant 7
through a partial computation for the
C_{8}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{8})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 1\rangle
.

•

For each
m,n\geqslant 1
,
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{2^{n}})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
is a decompleted model for the Lubin–Tate theory
E_{h}
(here
h=2^{n-1}\cdot m
) with
C_{2^{n}}
-action, and the
C_{2^{n}}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{2^{n}})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
contains the information about all differentials in the
C_{2^{n}}
-homotopy fixed point SS of
E_{h}
, cf.
[
BHSZ
]
.

The first equivariant slice SS computation for
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(G)\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
appears in Dugger’s thesis
[
Dug05
]
, which corresponds to the case
G=C_{2}
(so
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(G)\mkern-3.5mu)}=\mathrm{BP}_{\mathbb{R}}
) and
m=1
; subsequently, Hu and Kriz
[
Hu-Kriz
]
compute the
C_{2}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}_{\mathbb{R}}\langle m\rangle
for all
1\leq m\leq\infty
, Hill, Hopkins and Ravenel
[
HHR-BPC4<1>
]
compute the
C_{4}
-slice SS for
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 1\rangle
, and Hill, Shi, Wang and Xu
[
HSWX-BPC4<2>
]
compute the
C_{4}
-slice SS for
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 2\rangle
. Beyond these cases, we do not currently have a complete computation of the
G
-slice SS for
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(G)\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
for any other
G
or
m
. Nevertheless, a substantial body of structural results on the equivariant slice filtration and HHR theories—height stratifications, transchromatic shearing isomorphisms, vanishing lines, periodicities, and more—has been developed by Hill, Meier, Shi, Li, Duan, Zeng, Wang, Xu, et al. in works such as
[
MSZ-localized-slice-SS
,
MSZ-stratification
,
MSZ-transchromatic
,
DLS-vanishing-lines
,
DHLLSWX-periodicity
]
, among others. BIPWX’s cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism provides a natural framework for organizing these results and extracting further computational information from them.

In this paper we focus on the computation for
G=C_{4}
. There are three known families of differentials in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
:

•

the “norm differentials”
d_{5},d_{13},d_{29},d_{61},\ldots,d_{2^{2m+2}-3}
constructed from applying the HHR norm functor to the (completely known)
C_{2}
-slice SS, as in
[
HHR-BPC4<1>
, Theorem 4.7]
.

•

the “transfer differentials”
d_{3},d_{7},d_{15},d_{31},\ldots,d_{2^{2m+1}-1}
constructed from applying the transfer functor to the
C_{2}
-slice SS, as in
[
DLS-vanishing-lines
, Theorem 2.3]
.

•

the “sheared differentials”
d_{5},d_{13},\ldots,d_{2^{m+2}-3}
constructed from the full knowledge of the
C_{4}
-slice SS above the line of slope
1
, as in
[
MSZ-transchromatic
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Apart from these families, the other differentials in existing computations (e.g.
[
HSWX-BPC4<2>
]
) mostly come from ad-hoc case-by-case arguments, which admit no apparent generalization for
m\geqslant 3
. However, using Theorem
B
and Theorem
C
, we are able to produce new families of differentials in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
for all
m
:

Theorem F

(Exotic transfer differentials; Precise formulation in Theorem
5.29
)
.

For
1\leqslant m<\infty
, consider the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
. Suppose for
1\leqslant h\leqslant 2m
we have

•

a homogeneous polynomial
P\in B_{m}=\mathbb{Z}[T_{1},T_{3},\ldots,T_{2^{m}-1}]
of degree
p

•

two homogeneous classes
x,c\in R_{m}=\mathbb{Z}[\bar{t}_{1},\gamma\bar{t}_{1},\bar{t}_{3},\gamma\bar{t}_{3},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2^{m}-1},\gamma\bar{t}_{2^{m}-1}]

subject to the algebraic identity

x\cdot\bar{v}_{h}=P(\bar{t}_{1}\gamma\bar{t}_{1},\bar{t}_{3}\gamma\bar{t}_{3},\ldots,\bar{t}_{2^{m}-1}\gamma\bar{t}_{2^{m}-1})+c+\gamma(c)\mod I_{h}=(2,\bar{v}_{1},\ldots,\bar{v}_{h-1})

for the Araki generators
\bar{v}_{k}\in R_{m}

(1\leqslant k\leqslant 2m)
in Theorem
5.23
. Then for each
q\in\{1,\ldots,m\},j\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a differential in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle

d_{2^{h+1}+2^{q+2}-5}(\mathrm{tr}(xa_{\sigma_{2}})u_{2^{h-1}\lambda}u_{(2^{q+1}\!j+2^{q}-p-1)\sigma})=P(\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{1},\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{3},\ldots,\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{2^{m}-1})\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{2^{q}-1}u_{2^{q+1}\!j\sigma}a_{(2^{h}+2^{q}-1)\lambda}a_{(2^{q+1}-2)\sigma}

constructed from “splicing together” the
h
-th transfer differential and the
q
-th sheared differential.

With appropriately chosen inputs (cf. Examples
5.31
and
5.33
), this recovers

•

all
d_{11}
differentials in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 1\rangle

•

all
d_{43}
differentials in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 2\rangle

•

all transchromatic
2
2

2

i.e. crossing the stratification line
\mathcal{L}_{1}
of slope
1
in
[
MSZ-stratification
, Figures 4 and 5]

d_{19}
differentials in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 2\rangle

and extends them to families of differentials in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
for
m\geqslant 3
.

Finally, in §
5.6
, we apply Theorems
D
and
F
to obtain a satisfactory interpretation for all differentials in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 1\rangle
: each differential in the SS comes from one of the seven generating differentials under the classical Leibniz rule, while each generating differential is established by a systematic (i.e. non-ad-hoc) construction. This is a promising first step towards a systematic description for all slice differentials of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(G)\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
.

1.3   Organization and conventions

In §
2
we set up BIPWX’s cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism in the realm of filtered spectra. In particular, we prove the Bockstein dictionary (Theorem
A
) and the Leibniz rule for total differentials (Theorem
D
). In §
3
we define hidden extensions and their no crossing conditions in terms of the deformation parameter
\tau
, and prove the generalized Leibniz rule (Theorem
B
). In §
4
we prove the generalized Mahowald trick (Theorem
C
) up to the delicate homotopy referred to as the “coherent Mahowald trick”. In §
5
we apply these results to equivariant slice SS to construct the exotic transfer differentials in Theorem
F
. In Appendix
A
we investigate the notion of trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS and its rigidity (Theorem
A.6
and Remark
A.8
), and identify the synthetic Adams SS as a special case in Theorem
A.9
. In Appendix
B
we prove the coherent Mahowald trick stated in Theorem
4.17
. Lastly, in Appendix
we prove the categorical refinements of Leibniz rules (Theorem
E
).

We summarize our convention throughout this paper below:

Convention A
.

Conventions for categories in general.

•

Throughout the paper, we work with the theory of
(\infty,1)
-categories as developed in
[
HTT
,
HA
]
. All definitions, constructions and arguments in this paper are supposed to be model-independent, and may equally be carried out in the axiomatic framework of
[
CCNW26
,
Cno26
]
.

•

We suppress most size considerations of categories throughout the paper; all such issues are standard and will be made explicit only when relevant.

•

We denote by
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}
or
(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)
the poset of integers, and we write
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}
for its underlying set. Similar conventions apply to the set of nonnegative integers
\mathbb{N}
.

•

We write categories, or
1
-categories, for
(1,1)
-categories.

•

We treat posets as special instances of
1
-categories. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
[n]
or
\Delta^{n}
the totally ordered set
\{0\leqslant 1\leqslant\cdots\leqslant n\}
. We write
\Delta
for the
1
-category of all finite totally ordered sets.

•

We write
\infty
-categories for
(\infty,1)
-categories, and we treat
1
-categories as special instances of
\infty
-categories. For any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we denote by
h\mathcal{C}
its homotopy
1
-category.

•

If
\mathcal{K},\mathcal{C}
are
\infty
-categories, we write
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{K}}
or
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{C})
for the functor
\infty
-category.

•

For any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we write
\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{Fun}({\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}},\mathcal{C})
and
\mathsf{Gr}(\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{Fun}((\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{C})
.

•

We write
\mathsf{Spc}
for the
\infty
-category of spaces (i.e.
\infty
-groupoids, or
(\infty,0)
-categories). For any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we write
\mathcal{C}^{\simeq}
for the
core groupoid
(i.e. the maximal sub-
\infty
-groupoid) of
\mathcal{C}
.

•

We write
\mathsf{Spc}_{*}
for the
\infty
-category of pointed spaces.

•

We write
\mathsf{Set}
for the
1
-category of sets, and
\mathsf{Set}_{*}
for its pointed variant.

•

We denote by
\mathsf{Sp}
the
\infty
-category of spectra. We write
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}
for mapping spaces and
\mathrm{map}_{\mathcal{C}}
for mapping spectra. Furthermore, we write
[X,Y]
or
[X,Y]_{\mathcal{C}}
for
\pi_{0}\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
.

•

We denote by
\mathsf{Ab}
the
1
-category of abelian groups. For each
A\in\mathsf{Ab}
, we write
\mathrm{H}A\in\mathsf{Sp}
for its Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum.

•

For any pointed
1
-category
\mathcal{A}
, we write
\mathsf{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
for the
1
-category of (cohomological) chain complexes, i.e. the category of pairs
(\{M^{s}\in\mathcal{A}\}_{s\in\mathbb{Z}},d\colon M^{s}\to M^{s+1})
such that
dd=0
.

•

A
t
-
\infty
-category
is a stable
\infty
-category with a
t
-structure.

•

A
homological functor
is a functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{A}
whose source
\mathcal{C}
is a stable
\infty
-category, whose target
\mathcal{A}
is an abelian
1
-category, such that for any fiber sequence
A\to B\to C
in
\mathcal{C}
, the image
F(A)\to F(B)\to F(C)
is exact in
\mathcal{A}
.

•

We write
\mathsf{Cat}
(resp.
\mathsf{Cat}_{\mathrm{st}}
) for the
\infty
-category of (stable)
\infty
-categories.

•

We denote by
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathsf{Cat})^{\mathrm{lax}}
the
\infty
-category of monoidal
\infty
-categories with lax monoidal functors in between, and by
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathsf{Cat})
the wide subcategory spanned by monoidal functors. Similar conventions apply to the
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-monoidal variants
\mathsf{Alg}_{\mathbb{E}_{n}}(\mathsf{Cat})\subset\mathsf{Alg}_{\mathbb{E}_{n}}(\mathsf{Cat})^{\mathrm{lax}}
, in particular (for
n=\infty
) the symmetric monoidal variants
\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Cat})\subset\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Cat})^{\mathrm{lax}}
.

•

For
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\mathsf{Alg}(\mathsf{Cat})
, we write
\mathsf{Fun}^{\otimes\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
for the
\infty
-category of lax monoidal functors, and we write
\mathsf{Fun}^{\otimes\mathrm{oplax}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\coloneq\mathsf{Fun}^{\otimes\mathrm{lax}}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}
for the
\infty
-category of oplax monoidal functors. Similar conventions apply to the
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-monoidal variants.

•

For each
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-monoidal
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we write
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathcal{C})
for the
picard
\infty
-groupoid
of
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. the sub-
\infty
-groupoid of
\mathcal{C}^{\simeq}
spanned by invertible objects. In the case
n=\infty
, we also write
\mathrm{pic}(\mathcal{C})
for the corresponding connective spectra. For
R\in\mathsf{Alg}_{\mathbb{E}_{n+1}}(\mathsf{Sp})
, we write
\mathsf{Pic}(R)
for
\mathsf{Pic}(\mathsf{Mod}_{R})
; if
n=\infty
we also write
\mathrm{pic}(R)
for
\mathrm{pic}(\mathsf{Mod}_{R})
.

•

We write
\mathsf{Pr}
(resp.
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}
) for the
\infty
-category of presentable (resp. presentable stable)
\infty
-categories and left adjoints in between, equipped with the Lurie tensor product
\otimes
.

•

We write
X\times_{f,Z,g}Y
or
X\times_{Z}Y
for the pullback of
X\xrightarrow{f}Z\xleftarrow{g}Y
. Similarly for pushouts.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{K}
is a small
\infty
-category and
\mathcal{C}
admits
\mathcal{K}
-indexed colimits. Write
\mathcal{K}^{\triangleright}=\mathcal{K}\filledstar[0]\coloneq(\mathcal{K}\times[1])\sqcup_{\mathcal{K}\times\{1\}}[0]
. We can construct a colimit comparison functor

\mathrm{cp}=\mathrm{cp}_{\mathcal{K}}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{K}^{\triangleright}}\to\mathcal{C}^{[1]},\qquad T\mapsto(\colim T|_{\mathcal{K}}\to T(\text{cone point})).

Concretely,
\mathrm{cp}
is the composite
\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{K}^{\triangleright}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{K}\times[1]}=\mathsf{Fun}([1],\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{K}})\xrightarrow{\colim_{\mathcal{K}}}\mathsf{Fun}([1],\mathcal{C})
.

•

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a stable
\infty
-category. Then
h\mathcal{C}
admits a triangulated structure, whose shift endomorphism is the suspension functor
\Sigma
, and in which
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma X
is a
distinguished triangle (DT)
if it extends to a diagram
[2]\times[1]\to\mathcal{C}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma X}

so that both squares are cocartesian in
\mathcal{C}
, and the outer rectangle corresponds to
\mathrm{id}\colon\Sigma X\to\Sigma X
under the pushout comparison functor, cf.
[
HA
, § 1.1.2]
. We refer to such an enhancement (if it exists) as an
extended cofiber sequence
lifting the DT.

•

In a triangulated category, we write
(h,g,f)
for a triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

We say it is an
anti-distinguished triangle (ADT)
if
(-h,-g,-f)
is a distinguished triangle.

Convention B
.

Conventions on equivariant homotopy theory.

•

We write
C_{n}
for the cyclic group of order
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Suppose
G
is a finite group. We write
\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
for the
\infty
-category of
genuine
G
-spectra
, and we denote by
\mathbb{1}\in\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
its tensor unit. For
X\in\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
and
K\subset G
, we write
X^{K}
(resp.
X^{hK},\Phi^{K}X
) for its
categorical
(resp.
homotopic
,
geometric
)
K
-fixed point
.

•

We write
X\mapsto X^{e}
for the forgetful functor
\mathsf{Sp}^{G}\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathrm{B}G,\mathsf{Sp})
, and refer to
X^{e}
as the
underlying (Borel
G
-)spectrum
of
X
. This functor admits a fully faithful right adjoint, whose image consists of the
Borel complete
G
-spectra
.

•

Due to
[
GM11
]
(see also
[
CMNN20
, Theorem A.1]
),
\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
can be identified with the
\infty
-category of
spectral
G
-Mackey functors

\mathsf{Mack}_{G}(\mathsf{Sp})=\mathsf{Fun}^{\times}(\mathsf{Span}(\mathsf{Fin}_{G}),\mathsf{Sp})
, i.e. finite product preserving functors from the
\infty
-category of
spans of finite
G
-sets
in
[
BH21
, Appendix C]
to
\mathsf{Sp}
. Therefore, we have a
pointwise
t
-structure
on
\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
whose heart is equivalent to
\mathsf{Mack}_{G}(\mathsf{Ab})=\mathsf{Fun}^{\times}(\mathsf{Span}(\mathsf{Fin}_{G}),\mathsf{Ab})
, the
1
-category of ordinary
G
-Mackey functors. For
M\in\mathsf{Mack}_{G}(\mathsf{Ab})
, we refer to its image
\mathrm{H}M\in\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
as the
Eilenberg–MacLane
G
-spectrum
of
M
.

•

For any group homomorphism
\rho\colon K\to G
, the forgetful functor
\mathsf{Fin}_{G}\to\mathsf{Fin}_{K}
induces a unique symmetric monoidal left adjoint
\rho^{*}\colon\mathsf{Sp}^{G}\to\mathsf{Sp}^{K}
. If
\rho
is surjective with
G=K/A
, then
\rho^{*}=\mathrm{Infl}^{K}_{K/A}
is the
inflation
whose right adjoint is taking categorical
A
-fixed points. If
\rho
is injective, then
\rho^{*}=\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{K}
is the
restriction
whose right adjoint is the
induction

\mathrm{Ind}_{K}^{G}
. By the Wirthmüller isomorphism (cf.
[
MS06
,
Cno23
]
),
\mathrm{Ind}^{G}_{K}
is also left adjoint to
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{K}
.

•

We denote by
\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}\colon\mathsf{Span}(\mathsf{Fin}_{G})\cong\mathsf{Span}(\mathsf{Fin}_{G})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
the spectral Yoneda embedding, and for each
X\in\mathsf{Sp}^{G},P\in\mathsf{Fin}_{G}
we write
X[P]=X\otimes\Sigma_{+}^{\infty}P
. Note that
X[G/H]\cong\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}X
.

•

We write
\mathrm{RO}(G)
for the real representation ring of
G
. For any (virtual)
G
-representation
V
, we write
|V|=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}V
, and we denote by
\mathbb{S}^{V}\in\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
the
representation sphere
associated to
V
. If
V
is an actual
G
-representation, we denote by
a_{V}\colon\mathbb{1}=\mathbb{S}^{0}\to\mathbb{S}^{V}
the
Euler class
of
V
(induced by the inclusion
0\subset V
).

•

For
K\subset G,X\in\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
and
V\in\mathrm{RO}(K)
, we write
\pi_{V}^{K}(X)=[\mathbb{S}^{V},\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{K}X]_{\mathsf{Sp}^{K}}
and refer to these as the
\mathrm{RO}(K)
-graded homotopy groups
.

•

Suppose
X\in\mathsf{Sp}^{G}
. For any subgroup
H\subset G
, there is a
restriction map

\mathrm{res}=\mathrm{res}^{G}_{H}\colon\pi^{G}_{V}(X)\to\pi^{H}_{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}V}(X)=\pi^{G}_{V}(X[G/H])

induced by the unit
\mathrm{res}\colon X\to X[G/H]
of the adjunction
\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}\dashv\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}
, and a
transfer map

\mathrm{tr}=\mathrm{tr}_{H}^{G}\colon\pi^{H}_{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}V}(X)=\pi_{V}^{G}(X[G/H])\to\pi^{G}_{V}(X)

induced by the counit
\mathrm{tr}\colon X[G/H]\to X
of the adjunction
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}\dashv\mathrm{Ind}_{H}^{G}
. Note that
\mathrm{res}_{X}\cong X\otimes\mathrm{res}_{\mathbb{1}}
,
\mathrm{tr}_{X}\cong X\otimes\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbb{1}}
, and under the Spanier–Whitehead self-duality of
\mathbb{1},\mathbb{1}[G/H]
the two maps
\mathrm{res}_{\mathbb{1}},\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbb{1}}
are dual to each other. As for the maps on homotopy groups, both of them are group homomorphisms, while for products (say
X=R
is an
\mathbb{A}_{2}
-ring) we have
\mathrm{res}(a\cdot b)=\mathrm{res}(a)\cdot\mathrm{res}(b)
and the
Frobenius relations

\mathrm{tr}(a)\cdot b=\mathrm{tr}(a\cdot\mathrm{res}(b)),b\cdot\mathrm{tr}(a)=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{res}(b)\cdot a)
.

Convention C
.

Grading conventions in this paper, to be detailed in §
2
.

•

For bigraded spheres in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, our convention is determined by three principles:

–

The tensor unit
\mathbb{1}\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is
\mathbb{S}^{0,0}
.

–

The homological suspension
\Sigma\mathbb{S}^{0,0}=\cofib(0\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0})
is
\mathbb{S}^{1,0}
.

–

The “deformation parameter” (cf. Definition
2.7
) is
\tau\colon\mathbb{S}^{0,-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0}
.

•

For spectral sequences, our convention is determined by two principles:

–

Every SS starts from the
E_{2}
-page.

–

We use cohomological Adams convention for differentials, namely
d_{r}\colon E_{r}^{s,t}\to E_{r}^{s+r,t+r-1}
.

•

From any filtered spectrum
X
we can extract a “standard spectral sequence” (cf. Theorem
2.26
) whose grading convention is determined by
E_{2}^{s,t}(X)\cong\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau)
.

To compare this with some other conventions in the literature, we use the table below: its first row lists the objects (bigraded homotopy groups and spectral sequence components) in this paper and the following rows describe the same objects in the language of the cited works.

Grading conventions in the literature.

As an example, in
[
HZ25
, § 4]
the authors adopt the same convention for bigraded spheres, while their spectral sequences start from the
E_{1}
-page and follow the Atiyah–Hirzebruch convention for differentials (namely
d_{r}\colon E_{r}^{n,s}\to E_{r}^{n-1,s+r}
). According to the table, their grading convention for the standard spectral sequences is determined by
E_{1}^{n,s}(X)\cong\pi_{n,s}(X/\tau)
, and in their convention Theorem
2.34
becomes the statement that each
y\in B_{k+1}^{n,s}(X)\setminus B_{k}^{n,s}(X)
admits a lift
[y]\in\pi_{n,s}(X)
so that
\tau^{k}[y]\neq 0
and
\tau^{k+1}[y]=0
, etc.

1.4   Acknowledgment

The author thanks Weinan Lin, Guozhen Wang, and Zhouli Xu for discussions related to their work on hidden extensions in the Adams spectral sequence; Zhipeng Duan, Guchuan Li, and Xiaolin Danny Shi for discussions related to equivariant slice spectral sequence computations; and Eva Belmont, Yueshi Hou, Hana Jia Kong, Cheng Li, Yuxuan Li, Tongtong Liang, Yunze Lu, and Shangjie Zhang for helpful conversations during the preparation of this paper. The author also thanks Dan Isaksen, Xiaolin Danny Shi, Zhouli Xu and Shangjie Zhang for comments on earlier drafts.

2   Filtered spectra and BIPWX’s cofiber-of-tau formalism

In this section we set up BIPWX’s cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism in the realm of filtered spectra. More precisely, in §
2.1
we introduce the
\infty
-category of filtered spectra
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, the bigraded spheres, the fundamental operators
\tau^{k},\rho,\delta_{k}
and their basic relations; in §
2.2
we construct the standard spectral sequence of a filtered spectrum and prove the Bockstein dictionary (Theorems
2.32
,
2.34
and
2.38
) that translates between standard SS and the bigraded homotopy groups; in §
2.3
we deal with the multiplicativity of standard SS and prove the Leibniz rule for total differentials in Theorem
2.50
; finally, in §
2.4
we demonstrate how our results facilitate the study of any presentable
\infty
-category with a cocontinuous
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-action, and construct numerous examples.

Remark 2.1
.

Although we work solely with
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
in the first three subsections, most of the discussions there hold true for
\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})
where
\mathcal{E}
is a presentably
\mathbb{E}_{n}
(
n\geqslant 1
) monoidal
t
-
\infty
-category satisfying some mild assumptions, cf. the construction in Corollary
2.27
, the Bockstein dictionary results in Corollary
2.41
and the multiplicativity results in Appendix
.

2.1   Filtered spectra

Definition 2.2
.

The
\infty
-category of
filtered spectra
is
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Sp})
.

Concretely, an object in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is a sequence of maps between spectra

while a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
amounts to a family of squares

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y(-1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{-1}}

Remark 2.3
.

For
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
treated as a diagram
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathsf{Sp}
, we write
X(-{\infty})
(resp.
X(\infty)
) for its colimit (resp. limit) in
\mathsf{Sp}
. We say
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is
complete
if
X(\infty)=0
.

Fact 2.4
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is presentable and stable. Furthermore, it acquires a presentably symmetric monoidal structure via Day convolution (cf.
[
HA
, Example 2.2.6.17]
).

Concretely, the Day convolution tensor unit
\mathbb{1}\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is given as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}(2)=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}(1)=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}(0)=\mathbb{S}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{1}(-1)=\mathbb{S}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

Moreover, the tensor product
X\otimes Y
is computed by
(X\otimes Y)(n)=\colim_{a+b\geqslant n}X(a)\otimes Y(b)
.

Remark 2.5
.

The above discussions also work for the
\infty
-category of
graded spectra

\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fun}((\mathbb{Z}^{\delta})^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Sp})
. Concretely, the Day convolution tensor unit of
\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}
is a single
\mathbb{S}^{0}
sitting in stage zero, and for
X,Y\in\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}
their tensor product
X\otimes Y
is given as
(X\otimes Y)(n)=\bigoplus_{a+b=n}X(a)\otimes Y(b)
.

Definition 2.6

(Bigraded spheres)
.

There are two families of invertible objects in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
.

•

By construction, the spectral Yoneda embedding functor

\gamma\colon\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp},\quad w\mapsto\gamma_{w}=\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\mathrm{Map}(-,w)

is symmetric monoidal. Thus, each
\gamma_{w}
is invertible in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
with inverse
\gamma_{-w}
. We write
\mathbb{S}^{0,w}=\gamma_{w}
. Concretely, the image of
k\in\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}
under
\mathbb{S}^{0,w}
is
\mathbb{S}^{0}
if
k\leqslant w
or
0
otherwise, and the transition maps are either
0
or
\mathrm{id}
. In particular,
\mathbb{S}^{0,0}=\mathbb{1}
.

•

On the other hand, each suspension
\Sigma^{n}\mathbb{1}
is invertible with inverse
\Sigma^{-n}\mathbb{1}
. We write
\mathbb{S}^{n,0}=\Sigma^{n}\mathbb{1}
.

In general, we set
\mathbb{S}^{n,w}=\mathbb{S}^{n,0}\otimes\mathbb{S}^{0,w}
, and we write
\Sigma^{n,w}X=X\otimes\mathbb{S}^{n,w}
for each
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
.

For
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
we write
\pi_{n,w}(X)
for
\pi_{0}(\mathrm{Map}(\mathbb{S}^{n,w},X))
. Concretely,
\pi_{n,w}(X)=\pi_{n}(X(w))
.

Definition 2.7

(Deformation parameter)
.

We write
\tau\colon\mathbb{S}^{0,-1}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0}
for the image of
-1\to 0
under the spectral Yoneda embedding
\gamma\colon\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Concretely, this corresponds to the ladder diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{0,-1}=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{0,0}\ \ =}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

For
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, we write
\tau
for
X\otimes\tau\colon\Sigma^{0,-1}X\to X
. We also write
\tau^{k}\colon\Sigma^{0,-k}X\to X
for the
k
-fold composite of
\tau
, i.e. the image of
-k\to 0
under
X\otimes\gamma\colon\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
.

For technical convenience, we will adopt the following conventions: We write
\tau^{0}
for the map
\mathrm{id}_{X}\colon X\to X
, and we write
\tau^{\infty}
for the map
0\colon 0\to X
. We can therefore make sense of
X/\tau^{r}
for each
r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, with
X/\tau^{0}=0
and
X/\tau^{\infty}=X
.

Proposition 2.8

(Generic fiber and special fiber)
.

•

The “
\tau
-generic fiber” of
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is
\mathsf{Sp}
. More precisely,
\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]=\colim_{w\to\infty}\mathbb{S}^{0,w}
is an idempotent
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
algebra in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, and the functor
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Sp},X\mapsto X(-\infty)
induces a symmetric monoidal equivalence
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]}\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\cong\mathsf{Sp}
.

•

The “
\tau
-special fiber” of
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is
\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}
. More precisely, the cofiber
\mathbb{1}/\tau
admits an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
algebra structure in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, and the functor
\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp},X\mapsto\{\mathrm{gr}_{w}(X)=X(w)/X(w+1)\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
induces a symmetric monoidal equivalence
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}/\tau}\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\cong\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}
.

Proof.

We first construct the two symmetric monoidal left adjoints
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Sp}
and
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}
. For the first one, as
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is the free stable cocompletion of
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}
, it suffices to construct a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\to\mathsf{Sp}
sending each
w
to
\mathbb{S}^{0}
. This comes from the composite
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\to*\to\mathsf{Sp}
where the second map hits the tensor unit
\mathbb{S}^{0}\in\mathsf{Sp}
. For the second one, it suffices to construct a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\to\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}
sending each
w
to
\mathbb{S}^{0,w}
. This comes from pre-composing
\mathsf{Gr}(\Sigma^{\infty})\colon\mathsf{Gr}(\mathsf{Spc}_{*})\to\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}
with the symmetric monoidal functor
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\to\mathsf{Gr}(\mathsf{Set}_{*})\subset\mathsf{Gr}(\mathsf{Spc}_{*})
, where the latter functor is the unique enhancement of the pointed Yoneda embedding
\mathbb{Z}^{\delta}\rightarrow\mathsf{Gr}(\mathsf{Set})\to\mathsf{Gr}(\mathsf{Set}_{*})
that assigns each non-identity map
z<w
in
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}
to the zero map in the codomain.

From the construction it is immediate that the right adjoint
\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
sends
\mathbb{S}^{0}
to the constant filtration with value
\mathbb{S}^{0}
, which is the same as
\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]
. Similarly, the right adjoint
\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
sends
\mathbb{S}^{0,0}
to the filtration
\cdots\to 0\to\mathbb{S}^{0}\to 0\to\cdots
with
\mathbb{S}^{0}
concentrated in degree
0
, which is the same as
\mathbb{1}/\tau
. These right adjoints are lax symmetric monoidal (since their left adjoints are symmetric monoidal), so they equip
\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]
and
\mathbb{1}/\tau
with
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra structures in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Furthermore,
\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]\otimes\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]\cong\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]
since
\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]=\colim_{w\to\infty}\mathbb{S}^{0,w}
where the index category
\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}
is filtered, i.e.
\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]
is idempotent, so the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra structure on
\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]
is unique (
[
HA
, Proposition 4.8.2.9]
). It remains to prove the two induced functors
\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]}\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
and
\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}/\tau}\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
are equivalences. These equivalences are recorded in
[
BHS2
, Examples A.5 and A.6]
, as straightforward applications of the criterion
[
BHS2
, Proposition A.4]
.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

See Example
2.65
item 4 for a justification of the names “generic fiber” and “special fiber” from the perspective of algebraic geometry.

Remark 2.10
.

Since
\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]
is idempotent, the forgetful functor
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]}\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is fully faithful. For
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, we will not distinguish the constant filtration
X[\tau^{-1}]=X\otimes\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]
and its image
X[\tau^{-1}](-\infty)=X(-\infty)\in\mathsf{Sp}
. In particular, we write
\pi_{n}(X[\tau^{-1}])
for
\pi_{n}(X(-\infty))
, which is also isomorphic to
\pi_{n,w}(X[\tau^{-1}])
for each
w\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 2.11
.

For
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, the limit
\lim_{w\to\infty}\Sigma^{0,-w}X
along multiplication by
\tau
is the constant filtration on
X({\infty})
, so
X
is complete iff
\lim_{w\to\infty}\Sigma^{0,-w}X=0
. The inclusion of complete filtered spectra
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\hat{\tau}}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
admits a left adjoint that sends
X
to its
completion

X_{\hat{\tau}}
, here

X_{\hat{\tau}}=\cofib(\lim_{w\to\infty}\Sigma^{0,-w}X\to X)=\lim_{w\to\infty}X/\tau^{w}.

It follows that
X\to X_{\hat{\tau}}
induces an isomorphism
X/\tau^{w}\cong X_{\hat{\tau}}/\tau^{w}
for each
0\leqslant w<\infty
.

Remark 2.12
.

We record some categorical facts for
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
:

•

The two inclusions
\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}[\tau^{-1}]\hookrightarrow\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\hookleftarrow\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\hat{\tau}}
exhibit
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
as a
recollement
of
\mathsf{Sp}
and
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\hat{\tau}}
in the sense of
[
HA
, Definition A.8.1]
due to
[
HA
, Theorem A.8.20]
. Furthermore, we can equip
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\hat{\tau}}
with the complete tensor product
(X,Y)\to(X\otimes Y)_{\hat{\tau}}
, which upgrades this to a
symmetric monoidal recollement
in the sense of
[
Sha21
, Definition 2.20]
.

•

If a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
induces an isomorphism
X/\tau\cong Y/\tau
, then it also induces an isomorphism
X/\tau^{w}\cong Y/\tau^{w}
for each
w\geqslant 1
and an isomorphism of completions
X_{\hat{\tau}}\cong Y_{\hat{\tau}}
. This is because each
X/\tau^{w}
is an iterated extension of
X/\tau
, and
X_{\hat{\tau}}=\lim_{w\to\infty}X/\tau^{w}
.

Construction 2.13

(Fundamental distinguished triangles)
.

For any pair of arrows
A\to B\to C
in a stable
\infty
-category, there is an “octahedral” distinguished triangle
B/A\to C/A\to C/B\to\Sigma(B/A)
, cf.
[
HA
, Proof of (TR4) in Theorem 1.1.2.14]
. Applying this to the pair

\Sigma^{0,-r}\mathbb{1}\xrightarrow{\tau^{r-k}}\Sigma^{0,-k}\mathbb{1}\xrightarrow{\tau^{k}}\mathbb{1}

for
\mathbb{1}\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
,
0\leqslant k<r\leqslant\infty
and tensoring this with
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
leads to the distinguished triangle

\Sigma^{0,-k}X/\tau^{r-k}\xrightarrow{\tau^{k}}X/\tau^{r}\xrightarrow{\rho_{k}^{r}}X/\tau^{k}\xrightarrow{\delta_{k}^{r-k}}\Sigma^{1,-k}X/\tau^{r-k}.

In practice, we usually suppress some of the indices by writing
\rho=\rho_{k}^{r}
and
\delta_{k}=\delta_{k}^{r-k}
.

In some sense,
\tau
,
\rho
and
\delta
are the “gist” of
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. In the next section, we will study their effect in terms of spectral sequences. We end this section by collecting some relations on their composites.

Proposition 2.14
.

For
a,b\geqslant 1
, the composite
\Sigma^{0,-b}X/\tau^{a}\xrightarrow{\tau^{b}}X/\tau^{a+b}\xrightarrow{\rho}X/\tau^{a}
is homotopic to the image of
\tau^{b}\colon\mathbb{S}^{0,-b}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0}
under
(X/\tau^{a})\otimes-\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
.

Remark 2.15
.

It is for this reason that we reserve the name
\tau^{b}
for the map
\Sigma^{0,-b}X/\tau^{a}\to X/\tau^{a+b}
despite potential ambiguity. When we have to distinguish this with
(X/\tau^{a})\otimes(\tau^{b}\colon\mathbb{S}^{0,-b}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0})
, we choose to write
\cdot\tau^{b}
or
\tau^{b}\cdot
for the latter map as it is the actual “multiplication by
\tau^{b}
”.

Proof.

It suffices to prove this for
X=\mathbb{1}
. The map
(\mathbb{1}/\tau^{a})\otimes\tau^{b}
is the vertical cofiber of the square

\tau^{a}\otimes\tau^{b}\colon\hbox to152.4pt{\vbox to57.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 76.20013pt\lower-27.40666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
