leads to another proof for the classical Leibniz rule.

In Appendix
, we will recast Theorem
2.50
as a categorical statement, expressed in terms of the lax symmetric monoidality of certain functors.

2.4   The stages beyond filtered spectra

In this subsection we record a vastly general setup for BIPWX’s cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism. We start by showing there are plenty of methods to construct filtrations that recover known spectral sequences.

Example 2.54
.

1.

Suppose
X
is a pointed CW complex. Then the standard SS of the filtration
\Sigma^{\infty}\mathrm{sk}_{*}X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is a reindexed version
9
9

9

In the standard convention, this is the Atiyah–Hirzebruch SS of
X
that starts from its
E_{1}
page
of the Atiyah–Hirzebruch SS of
X
.

2.

Suppose
R\to S
is a map of
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-ring spectra and
M
is an
R
-module, such that
M
is
S
-nilpotent complete
in the sense that
M\cong\lim_{[n]\in\Delta}M\otimes_{R}S^{\otimes_{R}(n+1)}
. Then the totalization of Whitehead towers
\lim_{[n]\in\Delta}\tau_{\geqslant*}(M\otimes_{R}S^{\otimes_{R}(n+1)})
is a filtration whose standard SS is the
descent SS
of
M
. In particular, if
R=\mathbb{S}^{0}
and
S=E
is an Adams type ring spectrum (cf.
[
Pst23
, Definition 3.14]
), then this recovers the
E
-Adams SS of
M\in\mathsf{Sp}
starting from its
E_{2}
-page.

3.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a
t
-
\infty
-category and
F\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Sp}
is an exact functor. For
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the standard SS of
F(\tau_{\geqslant*}X)
recovers many familiar constructions:

•

If
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathrm{B}G,\mathsf{Sp})
for some compact Lie group
G
, then for
F=(-)_{hG}
(resp.
F=(-)^{hG}
, or
F=(-)^{tG}
), this recovers the homotopy orbit SS (resp. homotopy fixed point SS, or Tate SS) of the Borel
G
-spectrum
X
.

•

If
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Sh}(\mathcal{E};\mathsf{Sp})
is the
\infty
-category of spectral sheaves over some
\infty
-topos
\mathcal{E}
and
F=\Gamma
is the global section functor, then this recovers (a generalization of) the Leray SS computing the hypercohomology of
X
.

4.

Suppose
\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{F}\mathcal{B}\xrightarrow{G}\mathcal{C}
is a composable pair of left exact functors between Grothendieck abelian
1
-categories, which induces a pair of derived functors
\mathsf{D}^{+}(\mathcal{A})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{R}F}\mathsf{D}^{+}(\mathcal{B})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{R}G}\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{C})
, cf.
[
HA
, § 1.3.3]
. For
X\in\mathsf{D}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
,
\mathrm{R}G(\tau_{\geqslant*}\mathrm{R}F(X))\in\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{C}))
yields a
\mathcal{C}
-valued standard SS (cf. Corollary
2.27
), which recovers the Grothendieck SS computing
\pi_{*}\mathrm{R}(G\circ F)(X)
.

To fully make use of BIPWX’s cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism, it is not enough to produce filtrations only. In particular, the computational methods based on hidden extensions (in the next two sections) require more structured inputs such as cofiber sequences of filtrations. We shall introduce the “minimal” coherent structure that generates such functoriality data. Recall that
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is a stable presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, in other words
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\in\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
.

Definition 2.55
.

A
presentable
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-module
is a
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-module in
10
10

10

here the definition will not change if we replace
\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}
by
\mathsf{Pr}
due to idempotency of
\mathsf{Sp}\in\mathsf{Pr}

\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}
. Concretely, this is a presentable stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a functor
\mathcal{C}\times\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathcal{C}
that commutes with colimits on each side, subject to associativity and unitality coherences. For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
, we write
\mathcal{C}[\tau^{-1}]=\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}}\mathsf{Sp}\in\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}
for the
\tau
-generic fiber
of
\mathcal{C}
, and we write
\mathcal{C}/\tau=\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}/\tau}(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}}\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
for the
\tau
-special fiber
of
\mathcal{C}
. We denote by
\tau^{-1}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}[\tau^{-1}]
,
-/\tau\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}/\tau
the two basechange comparison functors.

Remark 2.56
.

Here we invoke the equivalence of
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathcal{V}\!}\mathsf{Mod}_{A}(\mathcal{V})\cong\mathsf{Mod}_{A}(\mathcal{C})
following
[
HA
, Theorem 4.8.4.6]
.

Remark 2.57
.

Alternatively, a presentable
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-module is a
locally filtered stable
\infty
-category
in the sense of
[
Rotational-Invariance
, Definition 3.1.10]
whose underlying
\infty
-category is presentable. This follows from comparing the characterization in
[
Rotational-Invariance
, Remark 3.1.12]
and in Corollary
2.68
.

Construction 2.58
.

For
\mathcal{C}\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
and
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we set
\Sigma^{n,w}X=X\otimes\mathbb{S}^{n,w}
and set
\tau^{k}=X\otimes\tau^{k}_{\mathbb{1}}\colon\Sigma^{0,-k}X\to X
. We also construct the fundamental distinguished triangles (cf. Construction
2.13
) for
X
by tensoring
X
with the fundamental distinguished triangles of
\mathbb{1}\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Clearly, all results we proved in §
2.1
also hold true in this generality.

This construction imports the basic language of
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
into
\mathcal{C}
. On the other hand, from
\mathcal{C}
we can also extract information into
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, using the standard fact that a
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-action on a presentable
\infty
-category amounts to a
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-enrichment in the sense of
[
GH15
]
.

Theorem 2.59
.

Suppose
\mathcal{V}\in\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
, and
\mathcal{M}\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
. Then for each
m\in\mathcal{M}
, the functor
\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{M},v\mapsto m\otimes v
admits a right adjoint
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{V},m^{\prime}\mapsto\hom_{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathcal{V}}(m,m^{\prime})
, and these assemble to a
\mathcal{V}\!
-enriched
\infty
-category structure on
\mathcal{M}
. Furthermore, this renders an equivalence between
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{V}\!}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
and the subcategory of large
\mathcal{V}\!
-enriched
\infty
-categories spanned by those
\mathcal{M}
admitting
\mathcal{V}\!
-tensors whose underlying
\infty
-categories are presentable stable, and left adjoint
\mathcal{V}\!
-enriched functors in between.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Hei23
, Theorem 1.2]
.
∎

Remark 2.60
.

For
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
we shall write
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}
for
\hom^{\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}}
. Similarly for
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp}
.

By construction,
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}
satisfies the following standard properties:

Fact 2.61
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a presentable
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-module,
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

The filtered mapping spectrum
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is concretely given by
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)(w)\cong\mathrm{map}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma^{0,w}X,Y)\cong\mathrm{map}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,\Sigma^{0,-w}Y)
.

•

The functor
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(-,-)\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
preserves limits and finite colimits for each variable.

•

The functor
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(-,-)
is compatible with bigraded suspensions, in the sense that

\Sigma^{n,w}\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\cong\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Sigma^{-n,-w}X,Y)\cong\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,\Sigma^{n,w}Y).

•

The
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(X,-)
image of
\tau^{k}\colon\Sigma^{0,-k}Y\to Y
is
\tau^{k}\colon\Sigma^{0,-k}\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(X,Y)\to\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(X,Y)
. Combining this with its limit preservation, it follows that the
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(X,-)
image of any fundamental distinguished triangle (cf. Construction
2.58
) of
Y
is the corresponding fundamental distinguished triangle for
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(X,Y)
. Similar results hold true for
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(-,Y)
as well.

We further relate the generic/special fiber constructions with filtered mapping spectra.

Lemma 2.62
.

Suppose
F\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}
is a map in
\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
,
\mathcal{M}\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}),\mathcal{N}\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{W}}(\mathsf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}})
and
F_{1}\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
is a
\mathcal{V}\!
-linear left adjoint. Write
F^{R},F_{1}^{R}
for the right adjoints of
F
and
F_{1}
.

•

For
x\in\mathcal{M},z\in\mathcal{N}
, there is a natural isomorphism
F^{R}(\hom^{\mathcal{W}}\!\!(F_{1}(x),z))\cong\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!(x,F_{1}^{R}(z))
.

•

For
x,y\in\mathcal{M}
, there is a natural map
F(\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,y))\to\hom^{\mathcal{W}}\!\!(F_{1}(x),F_{1}(y))
.

•

For
x,y\in\mathcal{M}
, there is a natural map
F^{R}F(\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,y))\to\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,F_{1}^{R}F_{1}(y))
. If
F^{R}
is conservative, then this is invertible iff
F(\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,y))\to\hom^{\mathcal{W}}\!\!(F_{1}(x),F_{1}(y))
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For the first item, take any
v\in\mathcal{V}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{V}}(v,F^{R}(\hom^{\mathcal{W}}\!\!(F_{1}(x),z)))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{W}}(F(v),\hom^{\mathcal{W}}\!\!(F_{1}(x),z))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{N}}(F_{1}(x)\otimes F(v),z)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{N}}(F_{1}(x\otimes v),z)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{M}}(x\otimes v,F_{1}^{R}(z))

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{V}}(v,\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,F_{1}^{R}(z))).

This induces the isomorphism
F^{R}(\hom^{\mathcal{W}}\!\!(F_{1}(x),z))\cong\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,F_{1}^{R}(z))
. The second map is constructed as the transpose of the map
F_{1}(x)\otimes F(\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,y))\cong F_{1}(x\otimes\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,y))\to F_{1}(y)
, and the third map is constructed as the composite

F^{R}F(\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,y))\to F^{R}(\hom^{\mathcal{W}}\!\!(F_{1}(x),F_{1}(y)))\cong\hom^{\mathcal{V}}\!\!(x,F_{1}^{R}F_{1}(y))

of the second map and the first isomorphism. Therefore, the third map is an isomorphism iff the second map becomes an isomorphism after taking
F^{R}
image, which is further equivalent to the second map itself being invertible if
F^{R}
is conservative.
∎

Theorem 2.63
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a presentable
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-module, and
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
.

•

For the
\tau
-special fiber, we have
\mathrm{map}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(X,Y)/\tau\cong\mathrm{map}_{\mathcal{C}/\tau}^{\mathsf{Gr}}(X/\tau,Y/\tau)
.

•

For the
\tau
-generic fiber, if
X
is sequentially compact in
\mathcal{C}
, then

\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)[\tau^{-1}]\cong\mathrm{map}_{\mathcal{C}[\tau^{-1}]}(X[\tau^{-1}],Y[\tau^{-1}]).

•

Also,
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
is complete if
Y
is
\tau
-complete (i.e.
\lim_{w\to\infty}\Sigma^{0,-w}Y=0
), and for completions
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)_{\hat{\tau}}\cong\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y_{\hat{\tau}})\cong\lim_{w\to\infty}\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y/\tau^{w})
.

Proof.

To establish the first identification, by Lemma
2.62
it suffices to show
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)/\tau\to\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y/\tau)
is invertible. This is true as both of them are the cofiber of
\tau\colon\Sigma^{0,-1}\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\to\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
. For the second part, one can work with
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)[\tau^{-1}]\to\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y[\tau^{-1}])
. As inverting
\tau
is essentially taking a sequential colimit, this is an isomorphism if
\mathrm{map}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,-)
preserves all sequential colimits, i.e. if
X
is sequentially compact in
\mathcal{C}
. Lastly, the results on completeness follow from the fact that
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,-)
preserves limits and cofiber sequences.
∎

Remark 2.64
.

Write
\mathcal{C}_{\hat{\tau}}
for the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
spanned by
\tau
-complete objects. It follows from
[
CSY21
, Proposition 5.2.3]
that
\mathcal{C}_{\hat{\tau}}=\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}}\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\hat{\tau}}
, and
the inclusions
\mathcal{C}[\tau^{-1}]\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}\hookleftarrow\mathcal{C}_{\hat{\tau}}
exhibit
\mathcal{C}
as a recollement of
\mathcal{C}[\tau^{-1}]
and
\mathcal{C}_{\hat{\tau}}
. Note that the proof of Theorem
2.63
also implies that
\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)_{\hat{\tau}}\cong\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}_{\mathcal{C}_{\hat{\tau}}}(X_{\hat{\tau}},Y_{\hat{\tau}})
.

Theorem
2.63
, together with Fact
2.61
, builds the bridge between
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
and an arbitrary presentable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
with cocontinuous
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-action. It remains to find examples for such
\mathcal{C}
.

Example 2.65
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a stable presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, and
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathcal{C}
is a symmetric monoidal left adjoint, then
\mathcal{C}
admits a cocontinuous
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-action. For instance:

1.

If
\mathcal{E}
is a stable presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, then the unique symmetric monoidal left adjoint
\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathcal{E}
yields a symmetric monoidal left adjoint
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Fil}(\mathcal{E})
by postcomposition. In this case,
\mathcal{C}[\tau^{-1}]\cong\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{C}/\tau\cong\mathsf{Gr}(\mathcal{E})
.

2.

If
R
is an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
algebra in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, then there is a symmetric monoidal left adjoint
-\otimes R\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Mod}_{R}(\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp})
. In this case,
\mathcal{C}[\tau^{-1}]=\mathsf{Mod}_{R[\tau^{-1}]}(\mathsf{Sp})
and
\mathcal{C}/\tau\cong\mathsf{Mod}_{R/\tau}(\mathsf{Gr}\mathsf{Sp})
. By
[
BHS2
, Proposition C.22]
this covers the case of
(cellular)
E
-synthetic spectra
in
[
Pst23
]
since
\mathsf{Syn}_{E}^{\mathrm{cell}}\cong\mathsf{Mod}_{R_{E}}(\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp})
, where
R_{E}=\lim_{[n]\in\Delta}\tau_{\geqslant*}(E^{\otimes(n+1)})
.
In this case, write
\nu\colon\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Syn}_{E}
for the
synthetic analogue
functor in
[
Pst23
, Definition 4.3]
, then for each
E
-nilpotent complete spectrum
X
this equivalence of categories sends
\nu X
to the
E
-Adams filtration of
X
in Example
2.54
item 2, cf. Theorem
A.9
.

3.

More generally, if
(\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{def}},\mathcal{E})
is a
deformation pair
in the sense of
[
BHS2
, Definition C.14]
, then there is a symmetric monoidal left adjoint
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{def}}
due to
[
BHS2
, Construction C.18]
. In this case,
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{def}}[\tau^{-1}]\cong\mathcal{E}
.

4.

On the other hand, in terms of spectral algebraic geometry, we have
\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{Mod}_{R}(\mathsf{Sp}))\cong\mathsf{QCoh}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{R}/\mathbb{G}_{m,R})
for each
R\in\mathsf{CAlg}(\mathsf{Sp})
, cf.
[
Mou21
, Theorem 1.1]
. Thus, every spectral
11
11

11

It is easy to construct maps of classical stacks
f\colon X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
over a classical commutative ring
R
(or maps of
derived
stacks
f\colon X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
over an
animated
commutative ring
R
): such a map is determined by the data of a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
X
(which corresponds to
f^{*}(\Sigma^{0,1}R)
) together with a section
\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{L}
(which corresponds to the map
f^{*}\tau
), cf.
[
Khan-Rydh
, Proposition 3.2.6]
. In general, the spectral stack
\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
classifies
strict
line bundles with
strict
sections, whose construction would require more homotopy coherence data.
stack
X
over
\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}
gives rise to a symmetric monoidal left adjoint
f^{*}\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{QCoh}(X)
. In this case,
\mathsf{QCoh}(X)[\tau^{-1}]\cong\mathsf{QCoh}(X\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}}*)
and
\mathsf{QCoh}(X)/\tau\cong\mathsf{QCoh}(X\times_{\mathbb{A}^{1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}}\mathrm{B}\mathbb{G}_{m})
. As a concrete example, if
R=\mathbb{C}
and
X=Y^{\mathrm{dR},+}
is Simpson’s filtered de Rham stack (as in
[
FGauge
, Definition 2.3.5]
) of a smooth complex variety
Y
, then the right adjoint
f_{*}\colon\mathsf{QCoh}(X)\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
sends the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
to the filtration whose standard SS is the Hodge-to-de Rham SS of
Y
, cf.
[
FGauge
, Theorem 2.3.6]
.

Remark 2.66

(Comparison with synthetic spectra)
.

Consider the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{Syn}_{E}
of
E
-synthetic spectra for an Adams type ring spectrum
E
. Write
U=\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(\mathbb{S}^{0,0}_{E},-)\colon\mathsf{Syn}_{E}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
for the forgetful functor, which is right adjoint to the functor
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Syn}^{\mathrm{cell}}_{E}\subset\mathsf{Syn}_{E}
above. By Fact
2.61
, the operators
\tau^{k}
(or
\lambda^{k}
),
\rho
, and
\delta_{k}
in
[
BHS1
]
,
[
Burklund-Xu
, § 7]
, and
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
map under
U
to the operators
\tau^{k}
,
\rho
, and
\delta_{k}
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
discussed above. Consequently, our Bockstein dictionary (i.e. Theorem
2.34
) recovers the “omnibus theorem” in
\mathsf{Syn}_{E}
, namely
[
BHS1
, Theorem 9.19]
.

In general it requires a lot of coherence data to construct
symmetric monoidal
left adjoints from
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
to a certain abstract
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
. On the other hand, to produce cocontinuous
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-actions it suffices to construct
monoidal
left adjoints
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathcal{C}
, which is much simpler.

Theorem 2.67
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a stable presentably monoidal
\infty
-category. Then the assignment
F\mapsto F(\mathbb{1}/\tau)
defines an equivalence from the
\infty
-category of monoidal left adjoint functors
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathcal{C}
to the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Alg}(\mathcal{C})
spanned by those
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras
R
for which
V=\fib(\mathbb{1}\to R)
is invertible.

Proof.

This follows from
[
GHMG
, Proposition 1.5]
.
∎

Corollary 2.68
.

According to
[
HA
, Corollary 4.7.1.41]
, a presentable
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-module structure on a presentable stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
amounts to a monoidal left adjoint from
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
to the (stable presentably monoidal)
\infty
-category of left adjoint endofunctors
\mathsf{Fun}^{L}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
. Thus, by Theorem
2.67
, the data of a presentable
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
-module structure on
\mathcal{C}
is the same as the data of a cocontinuous monad
T\in\mathsf{Alg}(\mathsf{Fun}^{L}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}))
whose fiber of the unit transformation
V=\fib(\eta\colon\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{C}}\to T)
is an invertible functor. There is an abundant source of such monads from
spherical adjunctions
, cf.
[
DKSS
,
GHMG
]
.

3   Hidden extensions and the generalized Leibniz rule

In this section we begin our study of the hidden extensions using BIPWX’s cofiber-of-
\tau
formalism. More precisely, in §
3.1
we define extensions on the
E_{r}
-page for
2\leqslant r\leqslant\infty
and the corresponding no crossing conditions, demonstrate how to translate different no-crossing conditions in terms of bigraded homotopy groups, and establish compatibility of extensions across different pages; in §
3.2
we prove the generalized Leibniz rule (Theorems
3.24
and
3.27
).

3.1   Hidden extensions and crossings

We first introduce the notion of
(hidden) extensions
, which captures the data of a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
—especially the part invisible after passing to the induced map on graded pieces—in terms of the standard spectral sequences of
X
and
Y
. We also introduce
crossings
, which quantify a certain subtlety in the study of extensions; their role will become precise after Lemma
3.8
.

Definition 3.1

(Extensions and crossings)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Take
r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
k<r
.

•

There is a
j
-th layer extension along
f
on the
E_{r+1}
-page of filtration jump
k
from
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
to
y\in Z_{r-k}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)/B_{1+k+j}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
if there exist
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r}),[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+k}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
lifting
x,y
so that

\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y].

In this case we write
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
, and we refer to the pair
([x],[y])
as a
witness
of the
j
-th layer extension. If the index
j
is clear from the context, we just refer to this as an
extension
, and if furthermore
k\geqslant 1
we say the extension is
hidden
. In the case
j=0
, we write
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1}}(x)=y
as an abbreviation for
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},0}(x)=y
.

•

Such a
j
-th layer extension is
trivial
if
y=0
in
Z_{r-k}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)/B_{1+k+j}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
, in other words the witness
\tau^{k+j}[y]\in\IIm(\tau^{k+j+1})
. It is
nontrivial
if it is not trivial.

•

Such a
j
-th layer extension is
inessential
if it is trivial or there exists
x^{\prime}\in Z^{s+a,t+a}_{r-a}(X)
with
0<a\leqslant k
so that
d_{k-a}^{f,E_{r+1-a},j+a}(x^{\prime})=y
. It is
essential
if it is not inessential.

•

A
precrossing
for such a
j
-th layer extension is a pair

x^{\prime}\in Z_{r-a}^{s+a,t+a}(X),y^{\prime}\neq 0\in Z_{r-k+b}^{s+k-b,t+k-b}(Y)/B^{s+k-b,t+k-b}_{1+k+j-b}(Y)

with
0<a\leqslant k,0\leqslant b\leqslant k-a
so that
d_{k-a-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a},j+a}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}
. Such a precrossing is a
crossing
if it is furthermore an essential extension.

•

The extension has
(full) no crossing
if no such crossing extension exists. More precisely, for
S\subset\{1,2,\ldots,k\},T\subset\{0,1,\ldots,k-1\}
, the extension has
(partial) no crossing with source range
S
, target range
T
if there is no such crossing extension with
a\in S,b\in T
.

Remark 3.2

(Classical hidden extensions)
.

There is also a notion of “extensions on the abutment” which matches the usage of the term “(hidden) extensions” in common literature such as
[
IWX
]
:

•

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, fix
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
x\in Z_{\infty}^{s,t}(X)
,
y\in Z_{\infty}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
be two surviving permanent cycles. There is an
extension on the abutment
along
f
of filtration jump
k
from
x
to
y
if there exists
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X)
and
[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+k}(Y)
lifting
x,y
, so that
f\iota[x]=\iota[y]
in
\pi_{t-s}(Y[\tau^{-1}])
. Here
\iota
is the
\tau
-inversion map in Construction
2.40
.

As
\iota\colon Y\to Y[\tau^{-1}]
is the colimit of all maps
\tau^{w}\colon Y\to\Sigma^{0,w}Y
, the information of an
f
-extension on the abutment is equivalent to that of an
f
-extension on the
E_{\infty}
-page in a sufficiently high layer.

Remark 3.3
.

The paper
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
develops the theory of hidden extensions from a slightly different perspective, using the language of
extension SS
. We compare the two approaches below:

•

For each map between strictly filtered abelian groups
f\colon F^{*}A\to F^{*}B
, one can consider the length
1
chain complex
[A\to B]
equipped with the induced filtration (i.e.
x
is of filtration
\geqslant s
if
x\in F^{s}B
or
x\in F^{s+1}A
). The
extension SS
is the standard SS for this filtration, suitably reindexed so that it starts from the
E_{0}
-page (which is the direct sum of
\mathrm{gr}^{*}(FA)
and
\mathrm{gr}^{*}(FB)
).

•

Suppose
X\to Y
is a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. For
1\leqslant r<\infty
, take
f\colon F^{*}_{\tau}\pi_{**}(X/\tau^{r})\to F^{*}_{\tau}\pi_{**}(Y/\tau^{r})
to be the map between
\IIm(\tau^{a})
-filtrations in the sense of Theorem
2.32
. Then a
j
-th layer extension along
f
on the
E_{r+1}
-page from
x
to
y
of filtration jump
k
is a differential
d_{k}(\tau^{j}x)=\tau^{k+j}y
in the extension SS.

•

Similarly, for
r=\infty
, take
f\colon F^{*}_{\tau}\pi_{**}(X)\to F^{*}_{\tau}\pi_{**}(Y)
to be the map between
\IIm(\tau^{a})
-filtrations in the sense of Theorem
2.34
. Then a
j
-th layer extension along
f
on the
E_{\infty}
-page from
x
to
y
of filtration jump
k
is a differential
d_{k}(\tau^{j}x)=\tau^{k+j}y
in the extension SS.

•

This characterization of
f
-extensions is equivalent to Definition
3.1
above. To prove the identification, note that we can construct from
f\colon F^{*}A\to F^{*}B
a distinguished triangle in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}

\Sigma^{0,-1}F^{*}A\xrightarrow{\tau f}F^{*}B\xrightarrow{g}F^{*}[A\to B]\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma^{1,-1}F^{*}A.

From this, the translation between Definition
3.1
and the extension differential approach follows from the generalized Mahowald trick (GMT), i.e. Theorem
4.23
and Theorem
4.26
. Here

–

Assumption item 4 in each case holds true for degree reasons.

–

Assumption item 5 in Theorem
4.26
is also automatic since there is no nontrivial boundary term for the strict filtration
F^{*}B
.

•

To finish the comparison with
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
there is one remaining point: the
\IIm(\tau^{a})
-filtrations on
\pi_{**}(X)
and
\pi_{**}(X/\tau^{r})
come from the abutment of the trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS (cf. Construction
A.3
and Theorem
A.6
), while in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Definition 5.4]
the extension SS are constructed from the abutment of the synthetic
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-Adams SS. In fact, these are also equivalent due to Theorem
A.9
.

•

The extension SS approach explains the terminology
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
we use. Also, an extension is essential iff it is an essential (cf. Remark
2.39
) extension differential, and crossings for extensions are realized as diagrammatic crossings of extension differentials.

Remark 3.4
.

Modulo the translation between bigraded stem functoriality and extension differentials as discussed in Remark
3.3
, there are some further differences in describing hidden extensions and crossings between
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, § 5]
and this section.

•

The
extensions

d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1}}
in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, § 5]
correspond to the
0
-th extensions

d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1}}=d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},0}
in Definition
3.1
. The
j
-th layer extensions for
j\geqslant 1
in the current paper briefly appear in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, § 4]
as certain extension differentials between the
E_{\infty}
pages of synthetic Adams SS, which are not the focus of their study.

•

A
crossing
for
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},0}(x)=y
in the sense of
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, § 5]
is a pair

x^{\prime}\in Z_{r-a}^{s+a,t+a}(X),y^{\prime}\neq 0\in Z_{r-k+b}^{s+k-b,t+k-b}(Y)/B^{s+k-b,t+k-b}_{1+k-a-b}(Y)

with
0<a\leqslant k,0\leqslant b\leqslant k-a
so that we have an essential
12
12

12

here
essential
in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, § 5]
means there is no
z\in Z_{r-a_{1}}^{s+a_{1},t+a_{1}}(X)
with
a<a_{1}\leqslant k-b
so that
d_{k-a_{1}-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a_{1}},0}(z)=y^{\prime}
extension
d_{k-a-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a},0}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}
, and the original extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},0}(x)=y
has no crossing if no such crossing extension exists. It follows from the same discussion as in Fact
3.7
below that if
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},0}(x)=y
has no crossing in the sense of
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, § 5]
, then it has no crossing in the sense of Definition
3.1
. The converse does not hold true in general.

Example 3.5
.

We now present an example illustrating most of the possible behaviors of extensions and crossings. Its conclusions may serve as a first guide to these phenomena, while its arguments are best appreciated after the general results developed in the remainder of this section and the next have been established. To formulate this example, we import data from
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-synthetic spectra via the functor
U\colon\mathsf{Syn}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, according to Remark
2.66
(here everything is
2
-local).

•

Write
\mathbb{S}^{0,0}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}=U(\nu_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}\mathbb{S}^{0})
, and let
\mathbb{S}^{n,w}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}=\Sigma^{n,w}\mathbb{S}^{0,0}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
.

•

Since the stem
\nu\in\pi_{3}(\mathbb{S})
has Adams filtration
1
, by
[
BHS1
, Lemma 9.15]
it lifts to a map
[h_{2}]\colon\mathbb{S}^{3,4}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
whose induced map on graded pieces, equivalently whose extension of filtration jump
0
, recovers multiplication by
h_{2}
on the
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-Adams
E_{2}
-page.

•

By
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Proposition 3.20]
, this map fits into a distinguished triangle

\mathbb{S}^{3,4}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}\xrightarrow{[h_{2}]}\mathbb{S}^{0,0}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}\xrightarrow{i_{\blacktriangle}}\cofib(\nu)_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}\xrightarrow{p_{\blacktriangle}}\mathbb{S}^{4,4}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}

(
\mkern 0.5mu\blacktriangle\mkern-0.5mu
)

in which
\cofib(\nu)_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}=U(\nu_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}\cofib(\nu\colon\mathbb{S}^{3}\to\mathbb{S}^{0}))
.

We will compute all extensions along the (shifted) map

f=[h_{2}]\colon X=\mathbb{S}^{0,0}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}\to Y=\mathbb{S}^{-3,-4}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}

in stem
45
, and record the result in Figure
1
.

•

Here
\{E_{r}^{s,t}(X)\}_{r\geqslant 2}
is the
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-Adams spectral sequence of
\mathbb{S}^{0}
, while
E_{r}^{s,t}(Y)=E_{r,{\mathrm{ASS}}}^{s+1,t+4}(\mathbb{S}^{0})
.

•

We draw our figures with horizontal coordinate
t-s
and vertical coordinate
s
, and refer to
(t-s,s)
as the bidegree in the picture. We also label both copies according to the indexing on the Adams spectral sequence of
\mathbb{S}^{0}
. Thus, for example, a class marked as having bidegree
(48,7)
in the right-hand column actually lies in
E_{2}^{6,45+6}(Y)=E_{2,{\mathrm{ASS}}}^{7,48+7}(\mathbb{S}^{0})
.

In the discussion of this example, we will freely use the generalized Mahowald trick (more precisely, Theorem
4.23
) to construct extensions, together with Theorem
3.12
to adjust their pages and/or layers. We will also appeal to the
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-Adams spectral sequence computations in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-machine
]
, which are available on Lin’s
website
.

The class
d_{0}h_{5}
of bidegree

Similarly, the class

of bidegree
(t-s,s)=(45,6)
is a permanent cycle, and it supports an essential extension

makes sense for all

with

.

To prove this, by Theorem

The class

of bidegree

To prove this, note that the

\IIm(f)
. Since

h_{0}^{2}d_{0}h_{5}
, as expected.

There is an essential extension

To prove these claims, note that there is an essential differential
d_{4}(d_{0}m[0])=-d_{0}Pj[0]
in
E_{*,\mathrm{ASS}}^{*,*}(\cofib(\nu))
.
It follows that

Thus,

,
in other words

\IIm(f)
.

-divisible.

.

These exhaust all essential extensions along the map
f=[h_{2}]
at stem
45
. As for crossings:

•

A crossing of the extension
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{d_{3}^{f,E_{\infty},0}(h_{3}^{2}h_{5})=Mh_{2}}
is an essential extension
d_{3-a-b}^{f,E_{\infty},a}(z)=w
, where

,

, and

. From the figure, there are five candidate classes in the possible bidegrees of

,

,

,

. Among these, only

and

support essential extensions whose targets lie in filtration at most
7
of
E_{*,\mathrm{ASS}}^{*,*}(\mathbb{S}^{0})
.

The essential extension
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}d_{0}^{f,E_{\infty},2}(d_{0}h_{5})=h_{0}e_{0}h_{5}
is a crossing for the original extension. On the other hand, the essential extension
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}d_{0}^{f,E_{\infty},0}(h_{0}d_{0}h_{5})=h_{0}^{2}e_{0}h_{5}
seems
13
13

13

It is indeed classified as a crossing in the sense of
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Definition 5.9]
.
to contribute to a crossing as well. However,
d_{0}^{f,E_{\infty},3}(h_{0}d_{0}h_{5})=h_{0}^{2}e_{0}h_{5}
is not essential anymore, so the class
h_{0}d_{0}h_{5}
cannot support any crossing for the original extension in the sense of Definition
3.1
.

Therefore,
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}d_{0}^{f,E_{\infty},2}(h_{0}d_{0}h_{5})=h_{0}^{2}e_{0}h_{5}
is the only crossing extension for
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{d_{3}^{f,E_{\infty},0}(h_{3}^{2}h_{5})=Mh_{2}}
. In general, for each

, the extension
d_{3}^{f,E_{r+1},u}(h_{3}^{2}h_{5})=Mh_{2}
has only one crossing

.

•

Similarly,
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}d_{0}^{f,E_{\infty},1}(h_{0}d_{0}h_{5})=h_{0}^{2}e_{0}h_{5}
is the only crossing extension for
\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}{d_{3}^{f,E_{\infty},0}(h_{0}h_{3}^{2}h_{5})=h_{0}Mh_{2}}
, and in general for each
4\leqslant r\leqslant\infty,u\in\mathbb{N}
, the extension

has only one crossing
d_{0}^{f,E_{r},u+1}(h_{0}d_{0}h_{5})=h_{0}^{2}e_{0}h_{5}
. Here the plausible interference
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}d_{0}^{f,E_{\infty},0}(h_{0}d_{0}h_{5})=h_{0}^{2}e_{0}h_{5}
again acts as a “fake crossing” since
d_{0}^{f,E_{\infty},2}(h_{0}d_{0}h_{5})=h_{0}^{2}e_{0}h_{5}
is not essential anymore.

•

All the other essential extensions have full no crossing for degree reasons.

Fact 3.6
.

For any map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, there is an abundant supply of extensions.

•

In the case
r<\infty
, it follows from Theorem
2.26
that every class
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
admits a lift
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
. Furthermore, for any such lift
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
and any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, if
\tau^{j}f[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t-j}(Y/\tau^{r+j})
is nonzero, then it lies in
\IIm(\tau^{k+j})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{k+j+1})
for a unique integer
0\leqslant k<r
. Thus, by Theorem
2.32
, there exists a unique class
y\in Z_{r-k}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)/B_{1+k+j}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
together with a lift
[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+k}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
so that
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y]
, and this witnesses the extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
.

•

In the case
r=\infty
, if
x\in Z_{\infty}^{s,t}(X)
lies in the image of
\rho_{1}^{\infty}\colon\pi_{t-s,t}(X)\to Z_{\infty}^{s,t}(X)
(which is always the case if
X
is complete and all weak obstructions in
E_{*}^{*,*}(X)
vanish; cf. Theorem
2.34
), then tautologically there is some lift
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X)
. Furthermore, for any such lift
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X)
and any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, either
\tau^{j}f[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t-j}(Y)
lies in
\bigcap_{a\in\mathbb{N}}\IIm(\tau^{a})
, or it lies in
\IIm(\tau^{k+j})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{k+j+1})
for a unique integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In the latter case (which is always the case if
Y
is complete and all weak obstructions in
E_{*}^{*,*}(Y)
vanish, provided that
\tau^{j}f[x]\neq 0
), Theorem
2.34
further yields a unique class
y\in Z_{\infty}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)/B_{1+k+j}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
together with a lift
[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+k}(Y)
such that
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y]
, and this witnesses the extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{\infty},j}(x)=y
.

Fact 3.7
.

Take
f\colon X\to Y
to be a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Take
r\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},j\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose we have classes
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X),y\in Z_{r-k}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
that support an extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
.

1.

If
d_{k-a-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a},j+a}(z)=w
is a precrossing of this extension, then by definition we have
w\neq 0\in Z_{r-k+b}^{s+k-b,t+k-b}(Y)/B_{1+k+j-b}^{s+k-b,t+k-b}(Y)
, so it can be inessential only if there exists another extension
d_{k-a_{1}-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a_{1}},j+a_{1}}(z_{1})=w
with
a_{1}>a
, which is also a precrossing. After sufficiently many iterations, we would end up with an essential extension
d_{k-a_{n}-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a_{n}},j+a_{n}}(z_{n})=w
, which turns out to be a crossing. In particular, the original extension has no crossing if and only if it has no precrossing.

2.

As
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y]
implies
\tau^{j+c}f[x]=\tau^{k+j+c}[y]
for
c\in\mathbb{N}
, an extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
leads to extensions
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j+c}(x)=y
for all
c\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore, if
d_{k-a-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a},j+a+c}(z)=w
is a precrossing extension for
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j+c}(x)=y
witnessed by
\tau^{j+a+c}f[z]=\tau^{j+k+c-b}[w]\not\in\IIm(\tau^{j+k+c-b+1})
, then
\tau^{j+a}f[z]\not\in\IIm(\tau^{j+k-b+1})
, so it would also witness a precrossing extension for
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
. In particular, if the original extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
has full no crossing, then the induced extensions
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j+c}(x)=y
have full no crossing for all
c\in\mathbb{N}
.

To compute the extension of a certain class in the standard SS, it is necessary to choose one of its lifts in the bigraded homotopy group, while different choices of lifts could witness different extensions in principle. We interpret no crossing conditions as formulating the independence of such choices.

Lemma 3.8
.

Suppose we have a
j
-th layer extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
witnessed by
\tau^{j}f[x]^{\prime}=\tau^{k+j}[y]^{\prime}
. Then for each
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
lifting
x
, either
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y]
in which
[y]=[y]^{\prime}+\tau\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon\in\pi_{t-s,t+k+1}(Y/\tau^{r-k-1})
, or
[x]=[x]^{\prime}+\tau^{a}[z]
for some precrossing extension
d_{k-a-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a},j+a}(z)=w
witnessed by
\tau^{j+a}f[z]=\tau^{k+j-b}[w]
.

Proof.

Consider the difference
[x]-[x]^{\prime}
. If it lies in
\IIm(\tau^{k+1})
then the former case holds true. Otherwise,
[x]-[x]^{\prime}\in\IIm(\tau^{a})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{a+1})
for some
0<a\leqslant k
, so Theorem
2.32
(for
r<\infty
) or Theorem
2.34
(for
r=\infty
) implies that
[x]-[x]^{\prime}=\tau^{a}[z]
for
[z]\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{r-a})
lifting some nonzero
z\in Z^{s+a,t+a}_{r-a}/B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a}
. If the image
\tau^{j}f[z]
lies in
\IIm(\tau^{k+j+1})
then the former case also holds true. Otherwise,
\tau^{j}f[z]\in\IIm(\tau^{k+j-b})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{k+j-b+1})
for some
0\leqslant b\leqslant k-a
, so
\tau^{j}f[z]=\tau^{j+k-b}[w]
for
[w]
lifting some nonzero
w\in Z^{s+k-b,t+k-b}_{r-k+b}/B^{s+k-b,t+k-b}_{1+k+j-b}
, therefore we extract a precrossing extension
d_{k-a-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a},j+a}(z)=w
as described in the latter case.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

The extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
has no crossing if and only if for each
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
lifting
x
, there exists some
[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+k}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
lifting
y
so that
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y]
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose
f\colon X\to Y
is a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Take
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,k,j\in\mathbb{N}
so that
k<r
. Suppose we have classes
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
,
y\in Z^{s+k,t+k}_{r-k}
so that
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
. Then for each
0<a<r
and each
z\in Z^{s+a,t+a}_{r-a}(X)
, if
z
is not the source of a crossing extension for
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
, then there is a lift
[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{r-a})
such that
\tau^{j+a}f[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\IIm(\tau^{k+j+1})
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
3.8
, for any lift
[z]\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{r-a})
, either
\tau^{j+a}f[z]\in\IIm(\tau^{k+j+1})
, or
a\leqslant k
and
\tau^{j+a}f[z]=\tau^{k+j-b}[w]\in\IIm(\tau^{k+j-b})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{k+j-b+1})
witnesses some precrossing extension
d_{k-a-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a},j+a}(z)=w
. In the latter case, the precrossing extension has to be inessential, so there exists another precrossing extension
d_{k-a_{1}-b}^{f,E_{r+1-a_{1}},j+a_{1}}(z_{1})=w
with
a<a_{1}\leqslant k-b
witnessed by
\tau^{j+a_{1}}f[z_{1}]=\tau^{k+j-b}[w]_{1}
. Replacing
[z]
with
[z]-\tau^{a_{1}-a}[z_{1}]
, which is still a lift of
z
, it follows that
\tau^{j+a}f[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\IIm(\tau^{k+j-b+1})
. As this strictly increases the
\tau
-divisibility of the target, after sufficiently many iterations we obtain a lift
[z]\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{r-a})
with
\tau^{j+a}f[z]\in\IIm(\tau^{k+j+1})
as expected.
∎

We can translate
j
-th layer extensions for different
j
and/or stretch them across pages.

Lemma 3.11
.

Suppose
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
,
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
c,k,j\in\mathbb{N}
and
k+c\leqslant r\leqslant\infty
. The following are equivalent:

1.

Every
\alpha\in\IIm(\tau^{k})\subset\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
that is
\tau^{j}
-torsion also lies in
\IIm(\tau^{k+c})
.

2.

In the standard SS of
X
,
B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a+j}/B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a}=0
for each
k\leqslant a<k+c
.

Proof.

Suppose there exists
y\in B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a+j}\setminus B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a}
for some
k\leqslant a<k+c
, then by Theorem
2.38
we can find
[x]\in\pi_{t-s+1,t-a-j}(X/\tau^{a+j})
so that
\delta_{a+j}^{1}[x]=y
. It then follows that
\tau^{a}\delta_{a+j}^{r-a}[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
is
\tau^{j}
-torsion, while it lies in
\IIm(\tau^{a})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{a+1})
due to Theorem
2.32
(if
r<\infty
) or Theorem
2.34
(if
r=\infty
), so it is not in
\IIm(\tau^{k+c})
. Thus, item 1 implies item 2. Conversely, if there exists
\alpha\in\IIm(\tau^{a})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{a+1})\subset\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
for some
k\leqslant a<k+c
, then Theorem
2.32
(if
r<\infty
) or Theorem
2.34
(if
r=\infty
) implies that
\alpha=\tau^{a}[x]
where
[x]
is a lift of some nonzero
x\in Z_{r-a}^{s+a,t+a}/B_{1+a}^{s+a,t+a}
, and if furthermore
\tau^{j}\alpha=0
then
x\in B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a+j}/B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a}
. Therefore, item 2 also implies item 1.
∎

Theorem 3.12
.

Take
f\colon X\to Y
a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Fix
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
j,j^{\prime},k\in\mathbb{N}
subject to the relations
k<r^{\prime}\leqslant r
and
j^{\prime}\leqslant j
.

1.

If for
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
,
y\in Z_{r-k}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
we have
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j^{\prime}}(x)=y
, then
d_{k}^{f,E_{{r^{\prime}}+1},j}(x)=y
as well.

2.

If for
x\in Z_{r^{\prime}}^{s,t}(X)
,
y\in Z_{r^{\prime}-k}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
we have
d_{k}^{f,E_{r^{\prime}+1},j}(x)=y
and

•

The class
x
actually lies in
Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
.

•

This extension has no crossing
d_{k-a-b}^{f,E_{{r^{\prime}}+1-a},j+a}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}
with
x^{\prime}\in Z_{r^{\prime}-a}^{s+a,t+a}(X)\setminus Z_{r-a}^{s+a,t+a}(X)
.

Then
y
actually lies in
Z_{r-k}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
, and
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
as well.

3.

If for
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
,
y\in Z_{r-k}^{s+k,t+k}(Y)
we have
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
such that

•

The extension has a witness
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y]
in which
f[x]\in\IIm(\tau^{c})
for some
0\leqslant c\leqslant k
.

•

In the standard SS of
Y
,
B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a+j}(Y)/B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a+j^{\prime}}(Y)=0
for each
c\leqslant a\leqslant k
.

Then

d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j^{\prime}}(x)=y
as well.

Proof.

For part 1, suppose
\tau^{j^{\prime}}\!f[x]=\tau^{k+j^{\prime}}[y]
is a witness of the extension
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j^{\prime}}(x)=y
. Then by Lemma
2.18
we deduce
\tau^{j}f\rho^{r}_{r^{\prime}}[x]=\tau^{j-j^{\prime}}\rho^{r}_{r^{\prime}}f[x]=\tau^{k+j}\rho^{r-k}_{r^{\prime}-k}[y]
, which witnesses
d_{k}^{f,E_{{r^{\prime}}+1},j}(x)=y
.

For part 2, we assume
r^{\prime}<\infty
with no loss of generality. Take a pair of lifts
[x]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r^{\prime}})
and
[y]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s,t+k}(Y/\tau^{{r^{\prime}}-k})
so that
\tau^{j}f[x]^{\prime}=\tau^{k+j}[y]^{\prime}
. As
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
, we can choose another lift
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
. We then induct on the
\tau
-divisibility of
\rho^{r}_{r^{\prime}}[x]-[x]^{\prime}
. Suppose
\rho^{r}_{r^{\prime}}[x]-[x]^{\prime}\in\IIm(\tau^{a})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{a+1})
, then it is of the form
\tau^{a}[z]^{\prime}
for some nonzero
z\in Z_{r^{\prime}-a}^{s+a,t+a}(X)
, cf. Theorem
2.32
(for
r^{\prime}<\infty
) or Theorem
2.34
(for
r^{\prime}=\infty
). By Lemma
3.10
, either
z\in Z_{r-a}^{s+a,t+a}(X)
, or there exists a lift
[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{r^{\prime}-a})
so that
\tau^{j+a}f[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\IIm(\tau^{k+j+1})
. In the former case we take a certain
[z]_{1}\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{r-a})
lifting
z
and replace
[x]
by
[x]-\tau^{a}[z]_{1}
, while in the latter case we replace
[x]^{\prime}
by
[x]^{\prime}+\tau^{a}[z]_{\mathrm{std}}
. After this modification,
\rho^{r}_{r^{\prime}}[x]-[x]^{\prime}\in\IIm(\tau^{a+1})
, and we still have
\tau^{j}f[x]^{\prime}=\tau^{k+j}[y]^{\prime\prime}
for
[y]^{\prime\prime}=[y]+\tau\varepsilon
lifting
y
. Continuing this process until
a\geqslant k
, we obtain
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{r})
and
[y]^{\prime\prime}\in\pi_{t-s,t+k}(Y/\tau^{r^{\prime}-k})
lifting
x
and
y
, so that

\rho^{r+j}_{r^{\prime}+j}(\tau^{j}f[x])=\tau^{j}f\rho^{r}_{r^{\prime}}[x]=\tau^{k+j}[y]^{\prime\prime}.

Feeding this into the pullback square in Lemma
2.18
(via Lemma
2.37
), we obtain a stem
[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+k}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
so that
\tau^{k+j}[y]=\tau^{j}f[x]
and
\rho^{r-k}_{r^{\prime}-k}[y]=[y]^{\prime\prime}
. Thus,
[y]
is also a lift of
y
, and we conclude that
d_{k}^{f,E_{r+1},j}(x)=y
as expected.

For part 3, the difference
\tau^{j^{\prime}}\!f[x]-\tau^{k+j^{\prime}}[y]
lies in
\IIm(\tau^{j^{\prime}+c})
and it is
\tau^{j-j^{\prime}}
-torsion, so Lemma
3.11
suggests that
\tau^{j^{\prime}}\!f[x]-\tau^{k+j^{\prime}}[y]=\tau^{j^{\prime}+k+1}\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon\in\pi_{t-s,t+k+1}(Y/\tau^{r-k-1})
. Hence,
[y]^{\prime}=[y]+\tau\varepsilon
is another lift of
y
so that
\tau^{j^{\prime}}\!f[x]=\tau^{j^{\prime}+k}[y]^{\prime}
, which witnesses the extension we expect.
∎

One especially important family of arrows in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
consists of the “total differential” operators

\delta_{r}^{\infty}\colon X/\tau^{r}\to\Sigma^{1,-r}X,

whose mapping data determine the differentials in
{E_{r}^{s,t}(X)}
by Theorem
2.38
. These maps, however, do not directly fit into the framework of (hidden) extensions on specified pages, since that framework is tailored to the intuition of maps between spectral sequences rather than to the internal structure of a single spectral sequence. We will instead analyze their mapping data in a parallel, though not identical, manner via the following notion of crossings.

Definition 3.13

(crossing for differentials)
.

Suppose
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Fix
r,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leqslant k\leqslant r
. Take
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}
and
y\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r+1,t+r}
so that
d_{r+1}(x)=y
in the standard SS.

•

A
crossing on the
E_{k+1}
-page
for this
d_{r+1}
differential is a pair
x^{\prime}\in Z_{r-a-b}^{s+a,t+a}
,
y^{\prime}\neq 0\in E_{r-a-b+1}^{s+r-b+1,t+r-b}
with
0<a<k
and
0\leqslant b\leqslant r-k
, so that
d_{r-a-b+1}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.14
.

In the case of Adams SS, this recovers
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Definition 4.14]
.

Example 3.15
.

Take again the
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-Adams SS of
\mathbb{S}^{0}
, which is the standard SS of
X=\mathbb{S}^{0,0}_{\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}}
. Here the class
h_{5}Pe_{0}
of bidegree
(t-s,s)=(56,9)
supports a differential
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{d_{5}(h_{5}Pe_{0})=d_{0}\Delta h_{0}^{2}e_{0}}
. Its potential crossings are depicted in Figure
2
below.

Potential crossings for
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{d_{5}(h_{5}Pe_{0})=d_{0}\Delta h_{0}^{2}e_{0}}
on the
E_{3},E_{4}
and
E_{5}
-page.

•

Every differential has no crossing on the
E_{2}
-page.

•

A crossing for this
d_{5}
on the
E_{3}
-page is a pair
x^{\prime}\in Z_{4-a-b}^{9+a,56+9+a},y^{\prime}\neq 0\in E_{5-a-b}^{14-b,55+14-b}
with
1\leqslant a\leqslant 1
,
0\leqslant b\leqslant 2
so that
d_{5-a-b}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}
. In other words, this corresponds to an essential differential whose source and target lie in the shaded region for the figure
on the left
. As we can see, there is only one such crossing differential, namely
\color[rgb]{0,1,1}{d_{2}(\Delta^{2}h_{1}h_{3})=h_{1}^{2}MP}
.

•

A crossing for this
d_{5}
on the
E_{4}
-page is a pair
x^{\prime}\in Z_{4-a-b}^{9+a,56+9+a},y^{\prime}\neq 0\in E_{5-a-b}^{14-b,55+14-b}
with
1\leqslant a\leqslant 2
,
0\leqslant b\leqslant 1
so that
d_{5-a-b}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}
. In other words, this corresponds to an essential differential whose source and target lie in the shaded region for the figure
in the middle
. From the figure we see this
d_{5}
has no crossing on the
E_{4}
-page.

•

A crossing for this
d_{5}
on the
E_{5}
-page is a pair
x^{\prime}\in Z_{4-a-b}^{9+a,56+9+a},y^{\prime}\neq 0\in E_{5-a-b}^{14-b,55+14-b}
with
1\leqslant a\leqslant 3
,
0\leqslant b\leqslant 0
so that
d_{5-a-b}(x^{\prime})=y^{\prime}
. In other words, this corresponds to an essential differential whose source and target lie in the shaded region for the figure
on the right
. From the figure we see this
d_{5}
has no crossing on the
E_{5}
-page.

As in Lemma
3.8
, we also interpret no crossing conditions for differentials as formulating the independence of bigraded stem lifts that witness a certain differential.

Lemma 3.16
.

Suppose for
r,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leqslant k\leqslant r
,
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
we have classes
x\in Z^{s,t}_{r},y\in Z^{s+r+1,t+r}_{\infty}
in the standard SS of
X
such that
d_{r+1}(x)=y
, together with a pair of lifts
[x]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{k}),[y]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r}(X)
so that
\delta_{k}^{\infty}[x]^{\prime}=\tau^{r-k}[y]^{\prime}
(which always exists due to Theorem
2.38
). Then for each
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{k})
lifting
x
, either we have
\delta_{k}^{\infty}[x]=\tau^{r-k}[y]
in which
[y]=[y]^{\prime}+\tau\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r+1}(X)
, or we have
\delta_{k}^{\infty}([x]-[x]^{\prime})=\tau^{r-k-b}[w]
in which
[w]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r-b}(X)
lifts the target of some
E_{k+1}
page crossing differential
d_{r-a-b+1}(z)=w
.

Proof.

The difference
[x]-[x]^{\prime}
lies in
\IIm(\tau^{a})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{a+1})
for some
0<a<k
, so Theorem
2.32
implies that
[x]-[x]^{\prime}=\tau^{a}[z]
for
[z]\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{k-a})
lifting some nonzero
z\in Z^{s+a,t+a}_{k-a}/B^{s+a,t+a}_{1+a}
. If the image
\delta_{k}^{\infty}(\tau^{a}[z])=\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]
lies in
\IIm(\tau^{r-k+1})
then the former case in the statement holds true. Otherwise,
\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]\in\IIm(\tau^{r-k-b})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{r-k-b+1})
for some
0\leqslant b\leqslant r-k
, so Theorem
2.34
implies that
\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]=\tau^{r-k-b}[w]
for
[w]
lifting some nonzero
w\in Z^{s+r-b+1,t+r-b}_{\infty}/B^{s+r-b+1,t+r-b}_{r-k-b+1}
. If the corresponding differential
d_{r-a-b+1}(z)=w
(under Theorem
2.38
) is essential, then it is an
E_{k+1}
page crossing differential, so the latter case in the statement holds true. Otherwise, there is another lift
[z]_{\mathrm{std}}
of
z
so that
\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\IIm(\tau^{r-k-b+1})
. Thus, the difference
\tau^{a}([z]-[z]_{\mathrm{std}})=\tau^{a_{1}}[z_{1}]\in\IIm(\tau^{a_{1}})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{a_{1}+1})
for certain
a<a_{1}<k
, and
\delta_{k}^{\infty}(\tau^{a_{1}}[z_{1}])=\tau^{r-k-b}[w]_{1}
where
[w]_{1}=[w]+\tau\varepsilon
is another lift of
w
. If the corresponding differential
d_{r-a_{1}-b+1}(z_{1})=w
is essential then it is an
E_{k+1}
page crossing differential. Otherwise, we reiterate the process, and in the end we would obtain some
[z_{n}]\in\pi_{t-s,t+a_{n}}(X/\tau^{k-a_{n}})
with
0<a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}<k
whose corresponding differential
d_{r-a_{n}-b+1}(z_{n})=w
is essential, thus an
E_{k+1}
page crossing differential as expected.
∎

Corollary 3.17
.

The differential
d_{r+1}(x)=y
has no crossing on the
E_{k+1}
page if and only if for each
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{k})
lifting
x
, there exists some
[y]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r}(X)
lifting
y
so that
\delta_{k}^{\infty}[x]=\tau^{r-k}[y]
.

Remark 3.18
.

Under the setup of Lemma
3.16
, take
i\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
. The same argument there also shows that for each
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{k})
lifting
x
, either we have
\tau^{k-i}\delta_{k}^{\infty}[x]=\delta_{i}^{\infty}\rho^{k}_{i}[x]=\tau^{r-i}[y]
in which
[y]=[y]^{\prime}+\tau\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r+1}(X)
, or we have
\tau^{k-i}\delta_{k}^{\infty}([x]-[x]^{\prime})=\tau^{r-i-b}[w]
in which
[w]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r-b}(X)
lifts the target of an essential differential
d_{r-a-b+1}(z)=w
with
0<a<i,0\leqslant b\leqslant r-k
(namely
d_{r-a-b+1}(z)=w
is an
E_{c+1}
-page crossing of the original
d_{r+1}
for each
i\leqslant c\leqslant k
).

Lemma 3.19
.

Take
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Suppose for
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
1\leqslant k\leqslant r
we have classes
x\in Z^{s,t}_{r},y\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r+1,t+r}
in the standard SS of
X
so that
d_{r+1}(x)=y
. Then for each
0<a<r
and each
z\in Z_{k-a}^{s+a,t+a}
, if
z
does not support an
E_{k+1}
-page crossing differential for
d_{r+1}(x)=y
, then there is a lift
[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{k-a})
so that
\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\IIm(\tau^{r-k+1})
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
3.16
, for any lift
[z]\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{k-a})
, either
\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]\in\IIm(\tau^{r-k+1})
, or
\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]=\tau^{r-k-b}[w]\in\IIm(\tau^{r-k-b})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{r-k-b+1})
so that
[w]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r-b}(X)
lifts the target of some
E_{k+1}
-page crossing differential
d_{r-a_{1}-b+1}(z_{1})=w
.
In the latter case, we have
a<a_{1}<k
as
z
does not support any such crossing differential, so there exists
[z_{1}]\in\pi_{t-s,t+a_{1}}(X/\tau^{k-a_{1}})
for which
\delta_{k-a_{1}}^{\infty}[z_{1}]=[w]_{1}
is another lift of
w
. Replacing
[z]
with
[z]-\tau^{a_{1}-a}[z_{1}]
, which is still a lift of
z
, it follows that
\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]\in\IIm(\tau^{r-k-b+1})
.
As this strictly increases the
\tau
-divisibility of the target, after sufficiently many iterations we obtain a lift
[z]\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{k-a})
with
\delta_{k-a}^{\infty}[z]\in\IIm(\tau^{r-k+1})
as expected.
∎

3.2   Generalized Leibniz rule for SS

The “prototype” of the GLR is a simple consequence of the naturality for the total differentials.

Theorem 3.20

(Blueprint for GLR)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Fix
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,m,l,i,j,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
U\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
so that
m<n\leqslant r
,
i\geqslant r-n
and
U_{1}=U-r+n>l
. Suppose there are bigraded stems

[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{n}),[x_{\infty}]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r}(X/\tau^{U_{1}}),[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+m}(Y/\tau^{n-m}),[y_{\infty}]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r+l}(Y/\tau^{U_{1}-l})

subject to the relations
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{m+j}[y]
and
\delta_{n}^{U}[x]=\tau^{r-n}[x_{\infty}]
. Then
\tau^{i}f[x_{\infty}]=\tau^{i+l}[y_{\infty}]
if and only if
\tau^{i-r+n}\delta_{n-m}^{U}[y]=\tau^{i+l}[y_{\infty}]
.

Proof.

Naturality of
\delta_{n}^{U}
provides a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/\tau^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y/\tau^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{1,-n}X/\tau^{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{1,-n}Y/\tau^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{n}^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{n}^{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

thus
\delta_{n-m}^{U}[y]=\delta_{n+j}^{U}(\tau^{m+j}[y])=\delta_{n+j}^{U}(\tau^{j}f[x])=\delta_{n}^{U}f[x]=f(\delta_{n}^{U}[x])=\tau^{r-n}f[x_{\infty}]
due to Corollary
2.19
. The result then follows by multiplying
\tau^{i-r+n}
on both sides and comparing with
\tau^{i+l}[y_{\infty}]
.
∎

We then state the necessary no crossing conditions to translate Theorem
3.20
into a statement of standard spectral sequences. The following Definition
3.21
aims for maximal generality, while we list some sufficient conditions easy to verify in practice in Example
3.22
.

Definition 3.21

(Complementary crossings)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
.

•

Suppose for
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,n,m,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
m<n\leqslant r
, we have classes
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
,
x_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r+1,t+r}(X)
and
y\in Z_{n-m}^{s+m,t+m}(Y)
so that
d_{r+1}(x)=x_{\infty}
and
d_{m}^{f,E_{n+1},j}(x)=y
. Then the differential and the
j
-th layer extension are
complementary on the source
if for each
z\in Z^{s+a,t+a}_{n-a}(X)
with
0<a\leqslant m
, either
z
does not support an
E_{n+1}
-page crossing differential for
d_{r+1}(x)=x_{\infty}
, or
z
is not the source of a crossing extension for
d_{m}^{f,E_{n+1},j}(x)=y
.

•

Suppose for
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,l,k,i\in\mathbb{N}
,
U\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with
k>0
,
l<r
and
l<U
, we have classes
x_{\infty}\in Z_{U}^{s-l,t-l}(X)
,
y\in Z_{k}^{s-r-1,t-r}(Y)
and
y_{\infty}\in Z_{U-l}^{s,t}(Y)
so that
d_{l}^{f,E_{U+1},j}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty}
. Then the
i
-th layer extension and the class
y
are
complementary on the target
if each
w\in Z_{U-l}^{s,t}(Y)
is not the target of a crossing extension for
d_{l}^{f,E_{U+1},i}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty}
, and for each
w\in Z_{U-l+b}^{s-b,t-b}(Y)
with
0<b<l
, either
w
is not the target of a crossing extension for
d_{l}^{f,E_{U+1},i}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty}
, or in the standard SS of
Y
there is no essential differential of the form
d_{r+1-b}(y)=w
.

•

Suppose for
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,l,i\in\mathbb{N}
with

r>l+i
, we have
x_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s-l,t-l}(X)
,
y\in Z_{r}^{s-r-1,t-r}(Y)
and
y_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s,t}(Y)
so that
d_{r+1}(y)=y_{\infty}
. Then the differential and the permanent cycle
x_{\infty}
are
i
-complementary on the target
if each class
w\in Z_{\infty}^{s,t}(Y)
is not the target of an essential differential
d_{r-a+1}(z)=w
with
0<a<r-l-i
, and for each
w\in Z_{\infty}^{s-b,t-b}(Y)
with
0<b\leqslant l
, either
w
is not the target of an essential differential
d_{r-a-b+1}(z)=w
with
0<a<r-l-i
, or there is no nontrivial extension of the form
d_{l-b}^{f,E_{\infty},i}(x_{\infty})=w
.

Example 3.22
.

•

The differential
d_{r+1}(x)=x_{\infty}
and the extension
d_{m}^{f,E_{n+1},j}(x)=y
are complementary on the source if there exist subsets
S_{1},S_{2}\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
such that the differential has no crossing on the
E_{n+1}
-page with source range
S_{1}
, the extension has no crossing with source range
S_{2}
, and
\{1,\ldots,m\}\subset S_{1}\cup S_{2}
. In particular, this holds if either the differential
d_{r+1}(x)=x_{\infty}
has no crossing on the
E_{n+1}
-page, or the extension
d_{m}^{f,E_{n+1},j}(x)=y
has no crossing.

•

The extension
d_{l}^{f,E_{U+1},j}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty}
and the class
y
are complementary on the target if there exist subsets
T_{1},T_{2}\subset\{0,1,\ldots,l-1\}
such that the extension has no crossing with target range
T_{1}
, the class
y
does not support an essential
d_{r-m+l+1-b}
-differential for each
b\in T_{2}
, and
0\in T_{1}
,
\{1,\ldots,l-1\}\subset T_{1}\cup T_{2}
. In particular, this holds if the extension
d_{l}^{f,E_{U+1},j}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty}
has no crossing.

•

The differential
d_{r+1}(y)=y_{\infty}
and the class
x_{\infty}
are
i
-complementary on the target if there exist subsets
T_{1},T_{2}\subset\{0,1,\ldots,l\}
such that the differential has no crossing of the form
d_{r-a-b+1}(z)=w
with
0<a<r-l-i
and
b\in T_{1}
, the class
x_{\infty}
does not support a nontrivial
i
-th layer extension on the
E_{\infty}
-page with filtration jump
l-b
for each
b\in T_{2}
, and
0\in T_{1}
,
\{1,\ldots,l\}\subset T_{1}\cup T_{2}
. In particular, this holds if the differential
d_{r+1}(y)=y_{\infty}
has no crossing on the
E_{c+1}
-page for some
c\in\{r-l-i,\ldots,r-l\}
.

The intuition behind the complementary crossing condition is as follows. We use no-crossing hypotheses to choose bigraded stems that simultaneously realize two tasks, such as witnessing an extension and a differential, or two extensions. Corollary
3.9
and Corollary
3.17
guarantee this under full no crossing from either side, but these assumptions are stronger than necessary: complementary crossing conditions already suffice to produce the desired common lift.

Lemma 3.23
.

Suppose
f\colon X\to Y
is a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Fix
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,n,m,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
m<n\leqslant r
. Suppose we have classes
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X)
,
x_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r+1,t+r}(X)
and
y\in Z_{n-m}^{s+m,t+m}(Y)
which support a differential
d_{r+1}(x)=x_{\infty}
and an extension
d_{m}^{f,E_{n+1},j}(x)=y
complementary on the source.
Then there exist lifts
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{n}),[x_{\infty}]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r}(X),[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+m}(Y/\tau^{n-m})
of
x,x_{\infty}
and
y
, such that
\delta_{n}^{\infty}[x]=\tau^{r-n}[x_{\infty}]
and
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{m+j}[y]
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
3.10
, Lemma
3.19
and the “complementary on the source” assumption, any
z\in Z_{n-a}^{s+a,t+a}(X)/B_{a+1}^{s+a,t+a}(X)
with
0<a<n
admits a lift
[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\pi_{t-s,t+a}(X/\tau^{n-a})
for which either
\tau^{j+a}f[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\IIm(\tau^{m+j+1})
or
\delta_{n}^{\infty}\tau^{a}[z]_{\mathrm{std}}=\delta_{n-a}^{\infty}[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\IIm(\tau^{r-n+1})
. The differential and the extension in the assumption imply the existence of
[x],[x]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{n}),[x_{\infty}]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r}(X),[y]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s,t+m}(Y/\tau^{n-m})
lifting
x,x_{\infty},y
, so that
\delta_{n}^{\infty}[x]=\tau^{r-n}[x_{\infty}]^{\prime}
and
\tau^{j}f[x]^{\prime}=\tau^{m+j}[y]^{\prime}
. We induct on the
\tau
-divisibility of
[x]^{\prime}-[x]
. Suppose
[x]^{\prime}-[x]\in\IIm(\tau^{a})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{a+1})
, then it is detected by certain nonzero
z\in Z_{n-a}^{s+a,t+a}/B_{a+1}^{s+a,t+a}
, cf. Theorem
2.32
. If
\tau^{j+a}f[z]_{\mathrm{std}}\in\IIm(\tau^{m+j+1})
we replace
[x]
by
[x]+\tau^{a}[z]_{\mathrm{std}}
, otherwise we replace
[x]^{\prime}
by
[x]^{\prime}-\tau^{a}[z]_{\mathrm{std}}
. It follows that
\delta_{n}^{\infty}[x]=\tau^{r-n}[x_{\infty}]^{\prime\prime}
and
\tau^{j}f[x]^{\prime}=\tau^{m+j}[y]^{\prime\prime}
where
[x_{\infty}]^{\prime\prime},[y]^{\prime\prime}
are also lifts of
x_{\infty},y
, while
[x]^{\prime}-[x]\in\IIm(\tau^{a+1})
. Continuing this process until
a=n
, we obtain
[x]=[x]^{\prime}
, for which we have both
\delta_{n}^{\infty}[x]=\tau^{r-n}[x_{\infty}]
and
\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{m+j}[y]
with
[x_{\infty}],[y]
lifting
x_{\infty},y
.
∎

The Blueprint Theorem
3.20
organizes the information in two directions. On the one hand, if there is a differential
d(x)=x_{\infty}
in the domain of
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, together with two
f
-extensions
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y
and
x_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y_{\infty}
, then it suggests the corresponding differential
d(y)=y_{\infty}
in the codomain.

Theorem 3.24

(Generalized Leibniz rule, part 1)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Fix
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,m,l,j,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
U\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
so that
m<n\leqslant r
and
U_{1}=U-r+n>l
. Suppose there are classes
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X),x_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r+1,t+r}(X),y\in Z_{n-m}^{s+m,t+m}(Y),y_{\infty}\in Z_{U_{1}-l}^{s+r+l+1,t+r+l}(Y)
subject to the assumptions:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}d_{r+1}(x)=x_{\infty}}
.

2.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}d^{f,E_{n+1},j}_{m}(x)=y}
.

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}d^{f,E_{U_{1}+1},i}_{l}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty}}
, here
i=r-m-1
.

4.

The
differential
in item 1 and the
extension
in item 2 are complementary on the source.

5.

The
extension
in item 3 and the class
y
are complementary on the target.

Then
y\in Z_{r-m+l}^{s+m,t+m}(Y)
, and
d_{r-m+l+1}(y)=y_{\infty}
in the standard SS of
Y
(see Figure
3
).

s
s+r+1
x
\ \ x_{\infty}
d_{r+1}
s+m
s+r+l+1
y
\ \ y_{\infty}
d_{r+l-m+1}
d_{m}^{f}
d_{l}^{f}
X
Y
f

Figure 3:
Generalized Leibniz rule.

Proof.

Item 1,2 and 4 suggest the existence of
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{n}),[x_{\infty}]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r}(X/\tau^{U_{1}})
and
[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+m}(Y/\tau^{n-m})
lifting
x,x_{\infty}
and
y
, such that

\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{m+j}[y]\quad\text{ and }\quad\delta_{n}^{U}[x]=\tau^{r-n}[x_{\infty}]

due to Lemma
3.23
. Also, item 3 implies we can find
[x_{\infty}]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r}(X/\tau^{U_{1}})
and
[y_{\infty}]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r+l}(Y/\tau^{U_{1}-l})
lifting
x_{\infty}
and
y_{\infty}
, so that
\tau^{i}f[x_{\infty}]^{\prime}=\tau^{i+l}[y_{\infty}]^{\prime}
. Thus by Lemma
3.8
, either
\tau^{i}f[x_{\infty}]=\tau^{i+l}[y_{\infty}]
for some
[y_{\infty}]=[y_{\infty}]^{\prime}+\tau\varepsilon
,
or
[x_{\infty}]=[x_{\infty}]^{\prime}+\tau^{a}[z]
in which
[z]
witnesses some precrossing extension
d_{l-a-b}^{f,E_{U_{1}-a+1},i+a}(z)=w
. In the former case, the Blueprint Theorem
3.20
implies

\delta_{1}^{U+n-m-1}y=\delta_{1}^{U+n-m-1}\rho_{1}^{n-m}[y]=\tau^{n-m-1}\delta_{n-m}^{U}[y]=\tau^{i-r+n}\delta_{n-m}^{U}[y]=\tau^{i+l}[y_{\infty}].

Thus,
y\in Z_{r-m+l}^{s+m,t+m}(Y)
and
d_{r-m+l+1}(y)=y_{\infty}
in the standard SS of
Y
due to Theorem
2.38
. In the latter case, the class
w
is also the target of some crossing extension due to Fact
3.7
, so item 5 implies that
0<b\leqslant l-a
and there is no essential differential of the form
d_{r-m+l-b+1}(y)=w
. On the other hand, according to the Blueprint Theorem
3.20
,

\delta_{1}^{U+n-m-1}y=\tau^{i-r+n}\delta_{n-m}^{U}[y]=\tau^{i}f[x_{\infty}]=\tau^{i}f(\tau^{a}[z]+[x_{\infty}]^{\prime})=\tau^{i+l-b}([w]+\tau^{b}[y_{\infty}]^{\prime})

which leads to a shorter differential
d_{r-m+l-b+1}(y)=w
. As
w
is the target of a crossing extension, we have
w\not\in B^{s+r+l-b+1,t+r+l-b}_{r-m+l-b}(Y)
, therefore
d_{r-m+l-b+1}(y)=w
is essential. This contradicts our previous conclusion, so the latter case can never happen.
∎

Remark 3.25
.

We briefly discuss how to choose parameters when we apply Theorem
3.24
.

•

The parameters
r,m,l
are fixed by the desideratum.

•

The parameter
n
is fine-tuned towards two goals: the existence of the extension from
x
to
y
, and the “complementary on the source” condition. The smallest possible choice of
n
yields
n+1=m+2
, the earliest page on which extensions of filtration jump
m
make sense. The largest possible choice is
n=r
, which is also the default choice in practice.

•

The parameter
j
can be as large as possible to guarantee the extension from
x
to
y
is unobstructed by early boundaries (i.e.
\tau
-power torsion lifts). In practice, often
j=0
suffices.

•

The parameter
U
is fine-tuned towards two goals: the existence of the extension from
x_{\infty}
to
y_{\infty}
, and the “complementary on the target” condition. The smallest possible choice of
U
yields
U-r+n+1=l+2
, the earliest page on which extensions of filtration jump
l
make sense. The largest possible choice is
U=\infty
, which is also the default choice in practice.

Remark 3.26
.

The complementary crossing conditions can be simplified via Example
3.22
.

•

Assumption item 4 in Theorem
3.24
holds true if the differential in item 1 has no crossing on the
E_{n+1}
page or the extension in item 2 has no crossing.

•

Assumption item 5 holds true if the extension in item 4 has no crossing.

Under the translation in Remark
2.66
, Remark
3.3
and Remark
3.4
, we thus recover
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Theorem 6.1]
for Adams SS. Note that our formulation of complementary crossing conditions in item 4 and item 5 match the expected generalization of GLR in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Remark 6.5]
.

The Blueprint Theorem
3.20
can also be read from a complementary perspective: if
d(x)=x_{\infty}
and
d(y)=y_{\infty}
are two differentials lying respectively in the domain and the codomain of a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, together with an
f
-extension
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y
between the sources, then it suggests the corresponding
f
-extension
x_{\infty}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y_{\infty}
between the targets.

Theorem 3.27

(Generalized Leibniz rule, part 2)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Fix
s,t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
r,m,l,i,j,n\in\mathbb{N}
so that
m<r-i\leqslant n\leqslant r
. Suppose there are classes
x\in Z_{r}^{s,t}(X),x_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r+1,t+r}(X),y\in Z_{r-m+l}^{s+m,t+m}(Y),y_{\infty}\in Z_{\infty}^{s+r+l+1,t+r+l}(Y)
subject to the following assumptions:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d_{r+1}(x)=x_{\infty}}
.

2.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d^{f,E_{n+1},j}_{m}(x)=y}
.

3.

d_{r-m+l+1}(y)=y_{\infty}
.

4.

The
differential
in item 1 and the
extension
in item 2 are complementary on the source.

5.

The differential in item 3 and the class
x_{\infty}
are
i
-complementary on the target.

Then there is an
f
-extension
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d^{f,E_{\infty},i}_{l}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty}}
.

Proof.

Item 1,2 and 4 suggest the existence of
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(X/\tau^{n}),[x_{\infty}]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r}(X)
and
[y]\in\pi_{t-s,t+m}(Y/\tau^{n-m})
lifting
x,x_{\infty}
and
y
, such that

\tau^{j}f[x]=\tau^{m+j}[y]\quad\text{ and }\quad\delta_{n}^{\infty}[x]=\tau^{r-n}[x_{\infty}]

due to Lemma
3.23
. Also, item 3 implies via Theorem
2.38
that we can find
[y]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s,t+m}(Y/\tau^{n-m})
and
[y_{\infty}]^{\prime}\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r+l}(Y)
lifting
y
and
y_{\infty}
, so that
\delta_{n-m}^{\infty}[y]^{\prime}=\tau^{r-n+l}[y_{\infty}]^{\prime}
. Therefore, by Remark
3.18
, either
\tau^{i-r+n}\delta_{n-m}^{\infty}[y]=\tau^{i+l}[y_{\infty}]
for
[y_{\infty}]=[y_{\infty}]^{\prime}+\tau\epsilon
, or
\tau^{i-r+n}\delta_{n-m}^{\infty}([y]-[y]^{\prime})=\tau^{i+l-b}[w]
for certain essential differential
d_{r-m+l-a-b+1}(z)=w
with
0<a<r-m-i
,
0\leqslant b\leqslant r-n+l
. In the former case, the Blueprint Theorem
3.20
implies that

\tau^{i}f[x_{\infty}]=\tau^{i-r+n}\delta_{n-m}^{\infty}[y]=\tau^{i+l}[y_{\infty}]

which witnesses the extension
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d^{f,E_{\infty},i}_{l}(x_{\infty})=y_{\infty}}
. The latter case actually contradicts assumption item 5: from item 5 we know any such crossing satisfies
b>0
, and the same argument leads to

\tau^{i}f[x_{\infty}]=\tau^{i-r+n}\delta_{n-m}^{\infty}[y]=\tau^{i+l-b}([w]+\tau^{b}[y_{\infty}]^{\prime}).

Here
\tau^{i+l-b}[w]\in\IIm(\tau^{i+l-b})\setminus\IIm(\tau^{i+l-b+1})
since
w
receives an essential
d_{r-m+l-a-b+1}
-differential with
a<r-m-i
. Thus
b\leqslant l
, and we obtain a nontrivial extension
d_{l-b}^{f,E_{\infty},i}(x_{\infty})=w
which is forbidden by item 5.
∎

Remark 3.28
.

We briefly discuss how to choose parameters when we apply Theorem
3.27
.

•

The parameters
r,m,l
are fixed by the desideratum.

•

The choice of the parameters
n,j
follows from the same discussion as in Remark
3.25
.

•

The parameter
i
is fine-tuned towards the
i
-complementary crossing condition in assumption item 5. The largest possible choice of
i
is
i=r-m-1
, which matches the statement of Theorem
3.24
. The smallest possible choice of
i
is
i=r-n
, which is also the default choice. In practice, the most common situation is
n=r
,
i=0
.

Also, the complementary crossing conditions in Theorem
3.27
can be simplified via Example
3.22
.

•

Assumption item 4 holds true if the differential in item 1 has no crossing on the
E_{n+1}
page or the extension in item 2 has no crossing.

•

Assumption item 5 holds true if the differential in item 3 has no crossing on the
E_{c+1}
page for some
c\in\{r-m-i,\ldots,r-m\}
. In particular, if
i=r-m-1
, then it holds true automatically.

Remark 3.30
.

We end this section with some further historical comments and attributions concerning the generalized Leibniz rule. The relationship with Lin–Wang–Xu’s Adams SS version
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Theorem 6.1]
has already been discussed above.

•

Suppose
R
is a filtered ring spectrum and
[x]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(R)
is a lift of
x\in E_{\infty}^{s,t}(R)
. Applying the GLR to the map
[x]\colon\Sigma^{t-s,t}R\to R
recovers a special case of the classical Leibniz rule, namely
d_{r}(xy)=(-1)^{t-s}x\cdot d_{r}(y)
for any
y\in E_{r}^{*,*}(R)
. This principle is classical and has been used throughout the study of multiplicative SS.

•

The main innovation underlying the GLR is the naturality of
total
differentials
\delta
with respect to arbitrary maps
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. In practice, this naturality is used in two ways:

(a).

If
\delta[x]=\tau^{?}[x_{\infty}]
in
X
, and if there are extensions
f[x]=\tau^{?}[y]
and
f[x_{\infty}]=\tau^{?}[y_{\infty}]
, then one obtains
\delta[y]=\tau^{?}[y_{\infty}]
in
Y
.

(b).

If
\delta[x]=\tau^{?}[x_{\infty}]
in
X
,
\delta[y]=\tau^{?}[y_{\infty}]
in
Y
, and there is an extension between the sources,
f[x]=\tau^{?}[y]
, then one gets an extension between the targets,
f[x_{\infty}]=\tau^{?}[y_{\infty}]
.

•

Perspective (b) appears in several computations:

–

Burklund–Hahn–Senger
[
BHS1
, Theorem A.20(14)]
use this perspective to deduce the extension

2[d_{0}]=\tau[h_{0}][d_{0}]=\delta_{1}^{\infty}(h_{0}h_{4})=[h_{0}]\delta_{1}^{\infty}(h_{4})=[h_{0}]^{2}[h_{3}]^{2}

in the
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-synthetic stems.

–

Isaksen–Kong–Li–Ruan–Zou
[
IKLRZ
]
use this perspective to deduce hidden extensions in the (
\mathbb{C}
-motivic =
\mathrm{MU}
-synthetic) ANSS of (the synthetic lift of)
\mathrm{tmf}
.

–

Marek
[
Marek
]
uses this perspective to deduce hidden extensions in the (
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-synthetic) Adams SS of (the synthetic lift of)
\mathrm{tmf}
.

•

Perspective (a) also occurs in several places:

–

Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel
[
HHR-BPC4<1>
]
use a stem-level form of this perspective to deduce exotic transfer differentials in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 1\rangle
. Their argument also uses hidden extension input of the kind related to the generalized Mahowald trick; see Remark
4.29
for a more detailed discussion of this point.

–

Burklund–Xu
[
Burklund-Xu
, § 7]
use this perspective, applied to various
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{F}_{2}
-synthetic stems lifting
h_{j}^{2}
, to deduce the Adams differentials on the family
h_{j}^{3}
.

–

Carrick–Davies–van Nigtevecht describe this perspective in
[
vN25
, Remark 3.41]
, and use it systematically in
[
CDvN2
]
to deduce differentials in the descent SS of
\mathrm{Tmf}
.

•

The applications above operate at the level of bigraded stems. To crystallize the GLR into a statement that takes spectral sequence data as input, one has to translate carefully between bigraded stems and spectral sequence terms, and impose appropriate no-crossing conditions.

An earlier formulation in this direction appears in Chua
[
Chua
, Theorem 12.9]
for the Adams SS. As stated, however, this result cannot be correct without a no-crossing hypothesis; see
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Example 6.10]
for a counterexample.

4   Generalized Mahowald trick

In this section, we prove the generalized Mahowald trick, which translates between hidden extensions along a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
and differentials in the standard SS of
Z=\cofib(f)
. Roughly speaking, this is done in four steps:

1.

We construct from the two distinguished triangles

X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma^{1,0}X\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \mathbb{S}^{0,-k}/\tau^{r-k}\xrightarrow{\tau^{k}}\mathbb{S}^{0,0}/\tau^{r}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathbb{S}^{0,0}/\tau^{k}\xrightarrow{\delta_{k}}\mathbb{S}^{1,-k}/\tau^{r-k}

\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, i.e. a diagram

together with the coherence data witnessing the fact that every three horizontally (resp. vertically) consecutive arrows form a distinguished triangle.

2.

We then construct two families of isomorphisms between pullbacks of various
cospans
inside a “
2

, the “coherent Mahowald trick”.

3.

Using the previous steps, we extract a pair of isomorphisms from the above “
2
