X/\tau^{r}\times_{f,Y/\tau^{r},\tau^{k}}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}\times_{h,X/\tau^{k},\rho}X/\tau^{r}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\times_{g,\Sigma^{0,-k}Z/\tau^{r-k},\delta_{k}}\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}

compatible with all six projections to
X/\tau^{r}
,
\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}
and
\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}
, cf. Theorem
4.20
.

4.

Finally, we apply the Bockstein dictionary in §
2.2
to deduce the generalized Mahowald trick in terms of standard spectral sequences.

We organize this section as follows: In §
4.1
we define the notion of “
2
d triangles” and construct the internal isomorphisms. We also state the coherent Mahowald trick and defer its proof to Appendix
B
. In §
4.2
we specialize the discussions to
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
and prove the generalized Mahowald trick in Theorems
4.23
and
4.26
.

4.1
2
d triangles and the coherent Mahowald trick

To propose a definition for “
2
d distinguished triangles”, we start from a definition that coherently lifts a classical (
1
d) distinguished triangle, together with all its rotations, in a stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the sub-poset of
(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)\times(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)
consisting of pairs
(m,n)
such that
-1\leqslant m-n\leqslant 2
. For any stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, a
coherent unrolled distinguished triangle (cuDT)
in
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
F\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
such that
F(m,n)=0
if
m-n=2
or
-1
, and the image of each square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m+1,n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,n+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m+1,n+1)}

is cartesian in
\mathcal{C}
. We write
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
spanned by cuDTs.

Remark 4.2
.

One can draw a cuDT as follows: consider the “diagonal embedding” map of posets

t\colon(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)\to\mathcal{P},\qquad n\mapsto\left(\bigg\lfloor\frac{n+1}{2}\bigg\rfloor,\bigg\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\bigg\rceil\right)=\begin{cases}(\frac{n}{2},\frac{n}{2}),&2\mid n.\\
(\frac{n+1}{2},\frac{n-1}{2}),&2\nmid n.\end{cases}

and write
F\langle n\rangle\coloneq F(t(n))
. Then
F\in\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle-2\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle-1\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle 0\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle 1\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle 2\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle 3\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ddots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ddots}

in which each square is cartesian. Often for convenience we suppress null homotopies in the picture, and the resulting diagram of
F
(more precisely,
t^{*}F
) becomes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle-2\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle-1\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle 0\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle 1\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle 2\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle 3\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

We treat a cuDT as a coherent refinement of DT due to the following

Theorem 4.3
.

Consider the inclusions of posets

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}}

where
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\{(m,n)\in\mathcal{P}\;|\;(0,0)\leqslant(m,n)\leqslant(2,1)\}\cong[2]\times[1]
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}=\{(0,0)\leqslant(1,0)\}\cong[1]
. Restriction along these functors induces equivalences of
\infty
-categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DT}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{[1]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{1}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Here
\mathsf{DT}(\mathcal{C})
is the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Fun}([2]\times[1],\mathcal{C})
spanned by diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{3}}

(
\star
)

in which the upper-right corner and the lower-left corner are
0
, and each square is cartesian in
\mathcal{C}
.

Intuitively, this says a cuDT is freely generated by a map
X_{0}\to X_{1}
via taking cofibers and fibers recursively. It also explains the relation between a cuDT and a DT: according to
[
HA
, Definition 1.1.2.11 and Theorem 1.1.2.14]
, if
\mathcal{C}
is stable, then a triangle
X_{0}\to X_{1}\to X_{2}\to\Sigma X_{0}
in
h\mathcal{C}
is distinguished iff there is a diagram (

⋆

‣
4.3
) in
\mathcal{C}
, in which each square is cartesian, so that

•

The maps
X_{0}\to X_{1}
and
X_{1}\to X_{2}
in the triangle coincide with the two maps in the diagram.

•

The map
X_{2}\to\Sigma X_{0}
in the triangle is the composite of
X_{2}\to X_{3}
in the diagram and the inverse to the comparison map
\Sigma X_{0}\to X_{3}
induced by the outer rectangle.

Therefore, the theorem also says a cuDT contains the same information as a coherent refinement of a DT (and the latter is also freely generated by the map
X_{0}\to X_{1}
via taking cofibers twice).

Proof.

It suffices to show the composite
i_{1}^{*}i_{2}^{*}
is an equivalence, as the same proof would imply
i_{1}^{*}
is an equivalence, while
i_{2}^{*}
is an equivalence by
2
-out-of-
3
. Write
\partial\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}\setminus\mathrm{Im}(t)
, and write
\mathcal{P}^{\geqslant 0}=\{(m,n)\in\mathcal{P}\;|\;(m,n)\geqslant(0,0)\}
.
Note that
i_{1}^{*}i_{2}^{*}
is the composite of restriction functors

\displaystyle\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle=\left\{F\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &F(m,n)=0\text{ if }(m,n)\in\partial\mathcal{P}\text{ and the}\\
&\text{image of each square is cartesian in }\mathcal{C}\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle\to\left\{F\colon\mathcal{P}^{\geqslant 0}\cup\partial\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &F(m,n)=0\text{ if }(m,n)\in\partial\mathcal{P}\text{ and the}\\
&\text{image of each square is cartesian in }\mathcal{C}\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle\to\left\{F\colon\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}\cup\partial\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}\;\middle|\;F(m,n)=0\text{ if }(m,n)\in\partial\mathcal{P}\right\}

\displaystyle\to\left\{F\colon\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}\cup(\partial\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{P}^{\geqslant 0})\to\mathcal{C}\;\middle|\;F(m,n)=0\text{ if }(m,n)\in\partial\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{P}^{\geqslant 0}\right\}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Fun}({\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}},\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}^{[1]}.

We claim each functor here is an equivalence. Actually, the first functor has an inverse, which sends
F\colon\mathcal{P}^{\geqslant 0}\cup\partial\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
to its right Kan extension along the inclusion
\mathcal{P}^{\geqslant 0}\cup\partial\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
. Similarly, the fourth restriction functor also has an inverse given by right Kan extension, while the second functor and the third functor admit inverses given by left Kan extensions.
∎

Rotations of DTs correspond to certain autoequivalences of
\mathsf{DT}^{u}
.

Fact 4.4
.

There are isomorphisms of posets, inverse to each other:

+1\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P},\qquad(m,n)\mapsto(n+1,m)

-1\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P},\qquad(m,n)\mapsto(n,m-1)

for which precomposition functors induce autoequivalences
(+1)^{*}
and
(-1)^{*}
on
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
.

For
F\in\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
,
((+1)^{*}F)\langle n\rangle=F\langle n+1\rangle
and
((-1)^{*}F)\langle n\rangle=F\langle n-1\rangle
. Intuitively,
(+1)^{*}
rotates the DT once to the right, while
(-1)^{*}
rotates it once to the left.

In general, for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
there is a poset automorphism
+k\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{P}
given as
(+1)^{\circ k}
, and it induces an autoequivalence
(+k)^{*}
on
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
. This defines a
\mathbb{Z}
-action on
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
natural on
\mathcal{C}\in\mathsf{Cat}_{\mathrm{st}}
. For notational convenience, we will refer to
(+k)^{*}F
as
F[+k]
.

In a triangulated category, if we rotate a DT
(h,g,f)
three times, we see
(-\Sigma h,-\Sigma g,-\Sigma f)
is another DT. This suggests that for
F\in\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
, there is an isomorphism between
\Sigma F
and
F[+3]
.

Construction 4.5

(Alpha isomorphisms)
.

Consider the following map of posets

\Theta\colon\mathcal{P}\times[1]\times[1]\to\mathcal{P},\qquad(m,n,a,b)\mapsto\begin{cases}(m,n),&\text{ if }a=b=0.\\
(n+2,n),&\text{ if }a=1,b=0.\\
(m,m+1),&\text{ if }a=0,b=1.\\
(n+2,m+1),&\text{ if }a=b=1.\end{cases}

Precomposition with
\Theta
induces a functor
\Theta^{*}\colon\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})^{[1]\times[1]}
, so that
\Theta^{*}F|_{a=b=0}=F
,
\Theta^{*}F|_{a=b=1}=F[+3]
, while
\Theta^{*}F|_{a=1,b=0}
and
\Theta^{*}F|_{a=0,b=1}
are both constant functors
\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
taking value
0
. Post-composing
\Theta^{*}
with the pushout comparison functor

\mathrm{cp}\colon\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})^{[1]\times[1]}\to\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})^{[1]},\qquad\hbox to77.08pt{\vbox to49.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.54156pt\lower-24.91663pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

we get a family of isomorphisms
\alpha=\alpha_{F}\colon\Sigma F\to F[+3]
in
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
natural in
F
.

Remark 4.6
.

Suppose
F\in\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then the map
\alpha_{F[+1],x}\colon\Sigma F[+1]\langle x\rangle=\Sigma F\langle x+1\rangle\to F[+1]\langle x+3\rangle=F\langle x+4\rangle
comes from the transpose of the square defining
\alpha_{F,x+1}\colon\Sigma F\langle x+1\rangle\to F\langle x+4\rangle
. Therefore,
\alpha_{F[+1],x}\cong-\alpha_{F,x+1}
due to
[
HA
, Lemma 1.1.2.10]
.

Remark 4.7
.

The explicit Kan extensions in the proof of Theorem
4.3
imply that every distinguished triangle
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma X
in
\mathcal{C}
lifts to some
F\in\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
with

t^{*}F\colon\cdots\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{-1}f}X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma X\xrightarrow{\Sigma f}\Sigma Y\xrightarrow{\Sigma g}\Sigma Z\xrightarrow{\Sigma h}\Sigma^{2}X\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{2}f}\cdots

so that
\alpha_{F,x}\colon\Sigma F\langle x\rangle\to F\langle x+3\rangle
is
\mathrm{id}
for each
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore, the shape of
\mathcal{P}
(and
[
HA
, Lemma 1.1.2.13]
) suggests
F\langle x\rangle\to F\langle x+1\rangle\to F\langle x+2\rangle\to F\langle x+3\rangle=\Sigma F\langle x\rangle
is a DT if
x\equiv 0
mod
2
while it is an ADT otherwise. This matches the fact in a triangulated category that

X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma X\text{ is a DT}\Longleftrightarrow Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma X\xrightarrow{\Sigma f}\Sigma Y\text{ is an ADT}.

Up to this point, the theory seems to be a faithful translation of what happens in triangulated categories. The merit of this coherent reformulation is that it extends naturally to diagram
\infty
-categories.

Fact 4.8
.

•

The subcategory
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})\subset\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
is closed under limits and colimits. In particular, finite (co)limits exist in
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
and they can be computed entrywise.

•

For any small
\infty
-category
\mathcal{K}
, the equivalence
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P},\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{C}))\cong\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{K},\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C}))
restricts to an equivalence
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{C}))\cong\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{K},\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C}))
.

Proof.

The first part follows from the stability of
\mathcal{C}
and the fact that (co)limits commute with (co)limits. For the second part, after identifying the double functor categories with
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
, we see
\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{C}))
and
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{K},\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C}))
both correspond to the full subcategory spanned by functors
F\colon\mathcal{K}\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
such that the slices
F(k,-)\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
are cuDTs for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
.
∎

We can thus formulate a natural
2
d generalization:

Definition 4.9
.

A
2
-dimensional coherent unrolled distinguished triangle (
2
d cuDT)
in
\mathcal{C}
is a functor
F\colon\mathcal{P}^{2}\to\mathcal{C}
such that for each
(m,n)\in\mathcal{P}
, the slices
F(-,-,m,n)\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
and
F(m,n,-,-)\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{C}
are both cuDTs. We write
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P}^{2},\mathcal{C})
spanned by
2
d cuDTs. Under the identification in Fact
4.8
, we have
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})\cong\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C}))
.

Remark 4.10
.

For convenience, when drawing
F\in 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
we usually suppress all nullhomotopies. In other words, write
t\times t\colon(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)\times(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)\to\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
for the square of the diagonal embedding
t
in Remark
4.2
, and write
F\langle m,n\rangle\coloneq F(t(m),t(n))
, then
F
takes the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}

Proposition 4.11
.

Suppose
\odot\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{C}
preserves finite colimits separately in each variable. Then for
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
, the composite
F_{1}\odot F_{2}\colon\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{F_{1}\times F_{2}}\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{\odot}\mathcal{C}
lies in
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the definition.
∎

Theorem 4.12
.

Consider the map of posets

i\times i\colon[1]\times[1]\cong(\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime})^{2}\to\mathcal{P}^{2}
,
where

i=i_{2}i_{1}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}=\{(0,0)\leqslant(1,0)\}\to\mathcal{P}

is the poset inclusion in Theorem
4.3
. Restriction along
i\times i
yields an equivalence
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})\cong\mathcal{C}^{[1]\times[1]}
.

Proof.

As
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})\cong\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C}))
, this follows from applying Theorem
4.3
twice.
∎

Intuitively, this says a
2
d cuDT is freely generated by a square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{00}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{10}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{01}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{11}}

by taking iterated cofibers and fibers in both directions.

Remark 4.13
.

In general, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can define the
\infty
-category of
n
-dimensional cuDTs
in
\mathcal{C}
to be
n\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})=(\mathsf{DT}^{u})^{\circ n}(\mathcal{C})
, and realize it as a full subcategory of
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P}^{n},\mathcal{C})
. The same argument then provides an equivalence
n\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})\cong\mathsf{Fun}([1]^{n},\mathcal{C})
.

Remark 4.14
.

The
\mathbb{Z}
-action on
\mathcal{P}
leads to a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action on
\mathcal{P}^{2}
and thus a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action on
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
, which we denote by
F\mapsto F[+m,+n]
. The comparison map
\alpha
in Construction
4.5
gives rise to two natural isomorphisms here, the “horizontal” comparison
\alpha_{h}\colon\Sigma F\to F[+3,0]
and the “vertical” comparison
\alpha_{v}\colon\Sigma F\to F[0,+3]
. We write
\alpha_{vh}
for the composite
\alpha_{v}\alpha_{h}^{-1}\colon F[+3,0]\to F[0,+3]
. Taking specific values in
\mathcal{P}^{2}
, this gives rise to isomorphisms
F[+3,0]\langle 1,0\rangle=F\langle 4,0\rangle\to F[0,+3]\langle 1,0\rangle=F\langle 1,3\rangle
, etc. that are compatible with the functoriality of
F
. Therefore, it induces an isomorphism of pullbacks
\alpha_{vh}\colon F\langle 4,0\rangle\times_{F\langle 4,1\rangle}F\langle 3,1\rangle\to F\langle 1,3\rangle\times_{F\langle 1,4\rangle}F\langle 0,4\rangle
, etc.

Remark 4.15
.

For instance, if
\odot\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
preserves finite colimits separately in each variable and
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
, then for
F=F_{1}\odot F_{2}
,
\alpha_{h}\colon F\langle 0,0\rangle\to F\langle 3,0\rangle
is the composite
\Sigma(F_{1}\langle 0\rangle\odot F_{2}\langle 0\rangle)\cong(\Sigma F_{1}\langle 0\rangle)\odot F_{2}\langle 0\rangle\xrightarrow{\alpha\odot\mathrm{id}}F_{1}\langle 3\rangle\odot F_{2}\langle 0\rangle
. A similar discussion works for
\alpha_{v}
.

Construction 4.16

(Beta isomorphisms)
.

Write
F_{\partial}\langle x,y\rangle=F\langle x+1,y\rangle\times_{F\langle x+1,y+1\rangle}F\langle x,y+1\rangle
for any
F\in 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
. Under this notation, the above construction yields
\alpha_{vh}\colon F_{\partial}\langle x+3,y\rangle\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\partial}\langle x,y+3\rangle
. Below we will construct another family of natural isomorphisms
\beta\colon F_{\partial}\langle x+1,y\rangle\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\partial}\langle x,y+1\rangle
.

Consider the following posets and maps: Take
\mathcal{V}=\Lambda^{2}_{2}=\{a_{1}<b_{1}>c_{1}\}
and write
\mathcal{W}=\{a_{2}<b_{2}>c_{2}<d_{2}>e_{2}\}
. Write
\mu\colon\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}
for the map of posets given on elements by:

a_{2}

b_{2}

c_{2}

(a_{1},a_{1})

(a_{1},c_{1})

Also, for
(m,n,a,b)\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
with
0\leqslant m-n\leqslant 1
,
0\leqslant a-b\leqslant 1
, write
\psi=\psi_{mnab}\colon\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
for the map of posets given on elements by:

1
st component

2
nd component

\psi
-value

a_{1}

(m,n,a+1,b+1)

Write
m+n=x,a+b=y
. The composite functor

2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})\subset\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P}^{2},\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\psi^{*}}\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{V}^{2},\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\mu^{*}}\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{W},\mathcal{C})\cong\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{C}^{\mathcal{V}})\xrightarrow{\lim_{\mathcal{V}}}\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{C})

sends
F\in 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
to the following zig-zag diagram in
\mathcal{C}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\partial}\langle x+1,y\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fib(F\langle x+1,y+1\rangle\to F\langle x+2,y+2\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fib(F\langle x+1,y+1\rangle\to F\langle x+2,y+2\rangle)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\partial}\langle x,y+1\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\fib(F\langle x+1,y+1\rangle\to F\langle x+2,y+2\rangle)}

Here, the two vertical maps are both
\mathrm{id}
. Furthermore, part of the diagram
\psi^{*}F
takes the shape

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle x+2,y\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0=F(m+1,n+1,2a-b,2b-a+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle x+1,y+1\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle x+2,y+1\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\langle x+2,y+2\rangle}

in which the right square is already cartesian, so the upper horizontal map in the zig-zag

F_{\partial}\langle x+1,y\rangle=F\langle x+2,y\rangle\times_{F\langle x+2,y+1\rangle}F\langle x+1,y+1\rangle\to 0\times_{F\langle x+2,y+2\rangle}F\langle x+1,y+1\rangle

is an isomorphism, and the lower horizontal map is invertible for the same reason. We write
\beta\colon F_{\partial}\langle x+1,y\rangle\to F_{\partial}\langle x,y+1\rangle
for the composite map. By construction, this
\beta
is invertible, and it is compatible with projections to
F\langle x+1,y+1\rangle
on both sides.

Theorem 4.17

(coherent GMT for general
2
d cuDTs)
.

For any
F\in 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
and
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the two isomorphisms constructed above
(-1)^{x+y+1}\alpha_{vh}\colon F_{\partial}\langle x+3,y\rangle\to F_{\partial}\langle x,y+3\rangle
and
\beta^{3}\colon F_{\partial}\langle x+3,y\rangle\to F_{\partial}\langle x+2,y+1\rangle\to F_{\partial}\langle x+1,y+2\rangle\to F_{\partial}\langle x,y+3\rangle
are homotopic.

We defer its proof to Appendix
B
.

Remark 4.18
.

The same proof there also shows
(-1)^{x+y}\alpha_{hv}=(-1)^{x+y}\Sigma^{-1}(\alpha_{h}\alpha_{v}^{-1})\colon F_{\partial}\langle x+3,y\rangle\to\Sigma^{-1}F_{\partial}\langle x+3,y+3\rangle\to F_{\partial}\langle x,y+3\rangle
is homotopic to
\beta^{3}\colon F_{\partial}\langle x+3,y\rangle\to F_{\partial}\langle x,y+3\rangle
.

Remark 4.19
.

With more effort, for each stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
and each
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we can construct an invertible
2
-morphism
\beta^{3}\cong(-1)^{x+y+1}\alpha_{vh}
in
\mathsf{Fun}(2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{C})
. The proof sketch for
x=y=0
goes as follows: We first reduce to presentable
\mathcal{C}
by changing universe and taking
\mathsf{Ind}
. For presentable stable
\mathcal{C}
we have the Lurie tensor product, such that
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})\cong 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{Sp})\otimes\mathcal{C}
and
\alpha_{vh},\beta
both lie in the image of
-\otimes\mathcal{C}\colon\mathsf{Fun}(2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{Sp}),\mathsf{Sp})\to\mathsf{Fun}(2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C}),\mathcal{C})
, so it suffices to treat the case
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Sp}
. Under the equivalence
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{Sp})\cong\mathsf{Sp}^{[1]\times[1]}
, the composite
\alpha_{vh}^{-1}\beta^{3}
is an automorphism of the functor
U\colon\mathsf{Sp}^{[1]\times[1]}\to\mathsf{Sp}
,
T\mapsto\Sigma(T(1,0)\times_{T(1,1)}T(0,1))
. Its left adjoint
U^{L}
sends
A
to the square
\widehat{A}
with
\widehat{A}(0,0)=0
and
\widehat{A}(1,0)=\widehat{A}(0,1)=\widehat{A}(1,1)=\Sigma^{-1}A
. By
\mathsf{Sp}
-enriched Yoneda lemma, we have
\mathrm{map}_{\mathsf{Fun}(\mathsf{Sp}^{[1]\times[1]},\mathsf{Sp})}(U,U)\cong\mathrm{map}_{\mathsf{Fun}(\mathsf{Sp},\mathsf{Sp})}(\mathrm{id},UU^{L})\cong UU^{L}(\mathbb{S}^{0})=\mathbb{S}^{0}
as an isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-ring spectra, thus the automorphism
\alpha_{vh}^{-1}\beta^{3}
is homotopic to either
\mathrm{id}
or
-\mathrm{id}
. Following the proof of Theorem
4.17
in Appendix
B
, we can construct a specific object
\mathrm{H}\mathbb{Q}^{\phi}\in 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{Sp})
on which
\beta^{3}\cong-\alpha_{vh}
and
\alpha_{vh}\not\cong-\alpha_{vh}
(this is the tricky step). Therefore,
\beta^{3}\cong-\alpha_{vh}
globally in the functor category.

4.2   Generalized Mahowald trick for SS

We start by specializing the above discussion for
2
d cuDTs to
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. This leads to a “prototype” of GMT, which identifies hidden extensions along a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
with (total) differentials in its cofiber
Z
on the level of bigraded stems.

Theorem 4.20

(Blueprint for GMT)
.

Suppose
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma^{1,0}X
is a distinguished triangle in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Then for
1\leqslant k<r\leqslant\infty
, there are isomorphisms of pullbacks

X/\tau^{r}\times_{f,Y/\tau^{r},\tau^{k}}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\xrightarrow[\sim]{\beta}\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}\times_{h,X/\tau^{k},\rho}X/\tau^{r}\xrightarrow[\sim]{\beta}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\times_{g,\Sigma^{0,-k}Z/\tau^{r-k},\delta_{k}}\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}

that are compatible with projections to
X/\tau^{r},\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}
and
\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}
. In particular,

•

If
[a]\in\pi_{n,n+s}(X/\tau^{r})
and
[b]\in\pi_{n,n+s+k}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
satisfy
f[a]=\tau^{k}[b]
, then there exists
[c]\in\pi_{n+1,n+s}(Z/\tau^{k})
with
\delta_{k}[c]=g[b]
and
h[c]=\rho[a]
.

•

If
[b]\in\pi_{n,n+s}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
and
[c]\in\pi_{n+1,n+s-k}(Z/\tau^{k})
satisfy
g[b]=\delta_{k}[c]
, then there exists
[a]\in\pi_{n,n+s-k}(X/\tau^{r})
with
f[a]=\tau^{k}[b]
and
\rho[a]=h[c]
.

•

If
[c]\in\pi_{n,n+s}(Z/\tau^{k})
and
[a]\in\pi_{n-1,n+s}(X/\tau^{r})
satisfy
h[c]=\rho[a]
, then there exists
[b]\in\pi_{n-1,n+s+k}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
with
g[b]=\delta_{k}[c]
and
\tau^{k}[b]=f[a]
.

Proof.

For the first part (before “in particular”), we begin by lifting the distinguished triangles

X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma^{1,0}X\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \mathbb{S}^{0,-k}/\tau^{r-k}\xrightarrow{\tau^{k}}\mathbb{S}^{0,0}/\tau^{r}\xrightarrow{\rho}\mathbb{S}^{0,0}/\tau^{k}\xrightarrow{\delta_{k}}\mathbb{S}^{1,-k}/\tau^{r-k}

to
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp})
according to Theorem
4.3
and Remark
4.7
. Then we form
F=F_{1}\otimes F_{2}\in 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp})
following Proposition
4.11
. This
F
(more precisely,
(t\times t)^{*}F
) takes the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{0,-k}X/\tau^{r-k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X/\tau^{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Y/\tau^{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1,0}Y/\tau^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X/\tau^{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y/\tau^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Sigma^{0,-k}X/\tau^{r-k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Sigma^{0,-k}Z/\tau^{r-k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{1,-k}X/\tau^{r-k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}X/\tau^{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}Y/\tau^{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z/\tau^{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{k}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Here
F\langle 0,0\rangle={\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\Sigma^{0,-k}X/\tau^{r-k}}
is at the lower-left corner. Construction
4.16
supplies isomorphisms

\beta\colon F_{\partial}\langle 3,-3\rangle=X/\tau^{r}\times_{Y/\tau^{r}}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\partial}\langle 2,-2\rangle=\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}\times_{X/\tau^{k}}X/\tau^{r}

\beta\colon F_{\partial}\langle 2,-2\rangle=\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}\times_{X/\tau^{k}}X/\tau^{r}\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\partial}\langle 1,-1\rangle=\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\times_{\Sigma^{0,-k}Z/\tau^{r-k}}\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}

compatible with projections to
F\langle 3,-2\rangle=X/\tau^{r}
and
F\langle 2,-1\rangle=\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}
. It remains to show
\beta^{2}\colon F_{\partial}\langle 3,-3\rangle\to F_{\partial}\langle 1,-1\rangle
is compatible with projections to
\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}
on both sides. Note that

\beta\colon F_{\partial}\langle 1,-1\rangle=\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\times_{\Sigma^{0,-k}Z/\tau^{r-k}}\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{\partial}\langle 0,0\rangle=X/\tau^{r}\times_{Y/\tau^{r}}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}

is compatible with projections to
F\langle 1,0\rangle=\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}
and the identification
F\langle 4,-3\rangle=\Sigma^{1,0}Y\otimes\mathbb{S}^{-1,-k}/\tau^{r-k}\cong\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\to F\langle 1,0\rangle=\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}
is precisely
\alpha_{vh}
by Remark
4.15
. Thus, this result follows from the homotopy
\beta^{3}\cong\alpha_{vh}
, which is constructed in Theorem
4.17
with
(x,y)=(0,-3)
.

The second part follows directly from the first part together with Lemma
2.37
. For instance, say there are
[a]\in\pi_{**}(X/\tau^{r})
and
[b]\in\pi_{**}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
with
f[a]=\tau^{k}[b]
. Surjectivity in Lemma
2.37
yields a certain
[w]\in\pi_{**}(X/\tau^{r}\times_{Y/\tau^{r}}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k})
that projects to both
[a]
and
[b]
, and the projection
[c]
of
\beta[w]\in\pi_{**}(\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}\times_{X/\tau^{k}}X/\tau^{r})
to
\pi_{**}(Z/\tau^{k})
then satisfies
\delta_{k}[c]=g[b]
,
h[c]=\rho[a]
.
∎

Remark 4.21
.

•

We can treat the bigraded stems of
V_{f,r,k}\coloneq X/\tau^{r}\times_{f,Y/\tau^{r},\tau^{k}}\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}
as
coherent extensions
on the
E_{r+1}
-page with filtration jump
\geqslant k
along
f\colon X\to Y
. The first part of Theorem
4.20
identifies
V_{f,r,k}
with
\Sigma^{0,-k}Y/\tau^{r-k}\times_{g,\Sigma^{0,-k}Z/\tau^{r-k},\delta_{k}}\Sigma^{-1,0}Z/\tau^{k}
, of which the bigraded stems are
coherent (total) differentials
in
Z=\cofib(f)
with length
\in[k+1,r]
whose targets come from
Y
. Therefore, coherent extensions contain the exact same information as coherent differentials.

•

On the other hand,
actual
finite page extensions of bigraded stems (e.g.
[a]\in\pi_{**}(X/\tau^{r})
,
[b]\in\pi_{**}(Y/\tau^{r-k})
with
f[a]=\tau^{k}[b]
) yield total differentials in
Z
by the second part of Theorem
4.20
, and vice versa, but the correspondence is not one-to-one. This is because the kernel in Lemma
2.37
is usually nonzero, namely there are multiple ways to enhance an extension (or a total differential) to a coherent one, resulting in the indeterminacy along the correspondence.

•

In practice, the input of GMT will come from SS. This suffers from another source of indeterminacy, namely nonuniqueness of the lift from SS to bigraded stems. In some cases, we have “no-crossing conditions” at hand to eliminate this indeterminacy, resulting in Theorem
4.23
and Theorem
4.26
below. For delicate situations where “crossings” are inevitable, we need to carry out further analysis with a certain explicit choice of lift via Theorem
4.20
.

We also record a variant of Theorem
4.20
for the “limit case” triangle in Construction
2.40
.

Theorem 4.22

(Blueprint for the “abutment type” GMT)
.

Suppose
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma^{1,0}X
is a distinguished triangle in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Then there are natural isomorphisms of pullbacks

X[\tau^{-1}]\times_{f,Y[\tau^{-1}],\iota}Y\xrightarrow[\sim]{\beta}Z^{\tau\textup{-tors}}\times_{h,\Sigma^{1,0}X^{\tau\textup{-tors}},\rho^{\infty}_{\infty}}X[\tau^{-1}]\xrightarrow[\sim]{\beta}Y\times_{g,Z,\delta_{\infty}^{\infty}}Z^{\tau\textup{-tors}}

that are compatible with projections to
X[\tau^{-1}],Y
and
Z^{\tau\textup{-tors}}
.

Proof.

This follows from the proof of Theorem
4.20
upon replacing the second input DT by

\mathbb{1}\xrightarrow{\iota\,=\,\langle\tau^{\infty}\rangle}\mathbb{1}[\tau^{-1}]\xrightarrow{\rho_{\infty}^{\infty}}\Sigma^{1,0}\mathbb{1}^{\tau\textup{-tors}}=\colim_{k\to\infty}\Sigma^{0,k}\mathbb{1}/\tau^{k}\xrightarrow{\delta_{\infty}^{\infty}}\Sigma^{1,0}\mathbb{1}

coming from Construction
2.40
.
∎

As in the GLR case, The Blueprint Theorem
4.20
for GMT also organizes the information in two directions. On the one hand, given
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, if there is a differential
d(\overline{x})=\overline{y}
in its cofiber
Z
together with two extensions
\overline{x}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}x
and
y\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}\overline{y}
, then it suggests an
f
-extension
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y
.

Theorem 4.23

(Generalized Mahowald trick, part 1)
.

Suppose
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma^{1,0}X
is a distinguished triangle in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Take
n,l,m,i,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
i<n
and write
r=n+m+l
. Suppose there are classes
x\in Z^{s+l+1,t+l}_{n}(X)
,
y\in Z^{s+n+l+1,t+n+l}_{m+1}(Y)
,
\overline{x}\in Z^{s,t}_{r}(Z)
,
\overline{y}\in Z^{s+r+1,t+r}_{\infty}(Z)
subject to the following assumptions:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}d_{r+1}(\overline{x})=\overline{y}}
.

2.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}d^{h,E_{r^{\prime}+1},j}_{l}(\overline{x})=x}
, where
r^{\prime}=n+l-i
.

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.75,0.5,0.25}d^{g,E_{m+2},i}_{m}(y)=\overline{y}}
.

4.

The
differential
in item 1 and the
extension
in item 2 are complementary on the source.

Then
x
lies in
Z^{s+l+1,t+l}_{n+m+1}(X)
,
and there is a
(j+l)
-th extension
d_{n}^{f,E_{n+m+2},j+l}(x)=y
(see Figure
5
).

s+l+1
x
s+l+n+1
y
s
s+r+1
\overline{x}
\overline{y}
d_{r+1}
s+l
x
d_{n}^{f}
d_{m}^{g}
d_{l}^{h}
X

Z
\Sigma^{1,0}X

Figure 5:
Generalized Mahowald trick.

Proof.

Items 1, 2, and 4 provide two bigraded stems
[\overline{x}]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(Z/\tau^{r^{\prime}})
and
[x]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+l}(X/\tau^{n-i})
lifting
\overline{x}
and
x
, so that

\tau^{j}h[\overline{x}]=\tau^{j+l}[x]\ \text{ in }\ \pi_{t-s-1,t-j}(X/\tau^{r^{\prime}+j})\quad\text{ and }\quad\delta_{r^{\prime}}^{m+i+1}[\overline{x}]=\tau^{m+i}\overline{y}\ \text{ in }\ \pi_{t-s-1,t+r^{\prime}}(Z/\tau^{m+i+1}),

cf. Lemma
3.23
. On the other hand, by item 3 we can find
[y]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+r^{\prime}}(Y/\tau^{m+1})
lifting
y
, so that
\tau^{i}g[y]=\tau^{m+i}\overline{y}
in
\pi_{t-s-1,t+r^{\prime}}(Z/\tau^{m+i+1})
. Apply Theorem
4.20
with
1\leqslant r^{\prime}<r+1
to the stems

g(\tau^{i}[y])=\tau^{m+i}\overline{y}=\delta_{r^{\prime}}^{m+i+1}[\overline{x}],

we obtain
[a]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t}(X/\tau^{r+1})

such that
\rho^{r+1}_{r^{\prime}}[a]=h[\overline{x}]
and
f[a]=\tau^{r^{\prime}}\!(\tau^{i}[y])=\tau^{n+l}[y]
. Feeding

\rho^{r+1+j}_{r^{\prime}+j}(\tau^{j}[a])=\tau^{j}\rho^{r+1}_{r^{\prime}}[a]=\tau^{j}h[\overline{x}]=\tau^{l+j}[x]

into the pullback in Lemma
2.18
(via Lemma
2.37
) produces a refined lift
[x]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+l}(X/\tau^{n+m+1})
of
x
with
\tau^{l+j}[x]=\tau^{j}[a]
. Thus, we have
\tau^{j+l}f[x]=\tau^{j}f[a]=\tau^{j+l+n}[y]
which witnesses the desired extension.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

We briefly discuss how to choose parameters when we apply Theorem
4.23
.

•

The parameters
n,m,l
are fixed by the desideratum.

•

The parameter
j
can be as large as possible to guarantee the extension from
\overline{x}
to
x
is unobstructed by early boundaries (i.e.
\tau
-power torsion lifts). In practice, often
j=0
suffices.

•

The parameter
i
is fine-tuned towards two goals: the existence of the extension from
\overline{x}
to
x
, and the “complementary on the source” condition in item 4. The largest possible choice of
i
yields
r^{\prime}+1=n-i+l+1=l+2
, the earliest page on which extensions of filtration jump
l
make sense. It can be as small as
0
, and we usually take
i=0
in practice.

Remark 4.25
.

The conclusion of Theorem
4.23
can be refined using Theorem
3.12
or Lemma
3.11
.

•

In the setup of Theorem
4.23
, suppose furthermore
f(x)=0
in
E_{2}^{s+l+1,t+l}(Y)
, and there exists
0\leqslant v\leqslant j+l
so that
B_{1+j+l+a}^{s+a+l+1,t+a+l}(Y)/B_{1+v+a}^{s+a+l+1,t+a+l}(Y)=0
for
1\leqslant a\leqslant n
. Then
d_{n}^{f,E_{n+m+2},v}(x)=y
according to Theorem
3.12
.

•

Alternatively, apart from
f(x)=0
in
E_{2}^{s+l+1,t+l}(Y)
, suppose we only have certain
0\leqslant v\leqslant j+l
with
B_{1+j+l+a}^{s+a+l+1,t+a+l}(Y)/B_{1+v+a}^{s+a+l+1,t+a+l}(Y)=0
for
1\leqslant a\leqslant n-1
. Then
d_{n}^{f,E_{n+m+2},v}(x)=y
has a witness
\tau^{v}[x]=\tau^{v+n}[y]
, and the difference
[x]-\tau^{n}[y]
is in
\IIm(\tau^{n})\cap\ker(\tau^{v})
according to Lemma
3.11
. In other words, the conclusion becomes
d^{f,E_{n+m+2},v}_{n}(x)=y^{\prime}
for some
y^{\prime}\in Z^{s+n+l+1,t+n+l}_{m+1}(Y)
such that
y-y^{\prime}\in B^{s+n+l+1,t+n+l}_{1+v+n}(Y)
.

On the other hand, the complementary crossing condition in assumption item 4 there can be simplified via Example
3.22
. For instance, it holds true if the differential in item 1 has no crossing on the
E_{{r^{\prime}}+1}
-page or the extension in item 2 has no crossing. Consequently, under the translation in Remark
2.66
, Remark
3.3
and Remark
3.4
, we recover
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
, Theorem 6.12]
for Adams SS.

There is also a dual perspective extracted from the Blueprint Theorem
4.20
: Given a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, if there is an
f
-extension
x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}y
together with two other extensions
\overline{x}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}x
and
y\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\textstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.82}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightsquigarrow$}}}}}\overline{y}
out of/into the cofiber
Z
, then it suggests a differential
d(\overline{x})=\overline{y}
in
Z
.

Theorem 4.26

(Generalized Mahowald trick, part 2)
.

Suppose
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\xrightarrow{h}\Sigma^{1,0}X
is a distinguished triangle in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Take
n,l,m,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geqslant 1
, and write
r=n+m+l
. Suppose there are classes
x\in Z^{s+l+1,t+l}_{n+m+1}(X)
,
y\in Z^{s+n+l+1,t+n+l}_{m+1}(Y)
,
\overline{x}\in Z^{s,t}_{l+1}(Z)
,
\overline{y}\in E^{s+r+1,t+r}_{2}(Z)
subject to the following assumptions:

1.

d_{n}^{f,E_{n+m+2},j+l}(x)=y
.

2.

d^{h,E_{l+2},j}_{l}(\overline{x})=x
.

3.

d^{g,E_{m+2},n+l-1}_{m}(y)=\overline{y}
.

4.

Item 3 has no crossing.

5.

B_{a+l+j+2}^{s+a+l+2,t+a+l+1}(Y)/B_{a+l+1}^{s+a+l+2,t+a+l+1}(Y)=0
in the standard SS of
Y
for each
0\leqslant a<n
.

Then
\overline{x},\overline{y}
are actually
r
-cycles in the standard SS of
Z
, and
d_{r+1}(\overline{x})=\overline{y}
.

Proof.

Item 1 supplies
[x]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+l}(X/\tau^{n+m+1}),[y]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+n+l}(Y/\tau^{m+1})
lifting
x,y
so that
\tau^{j+l}f[x]=\tau^{j+n+l}[y]
, and item 2 yields a lift
[\overline{x}]\in\pi_{t-s,t}(Z/\tau^{l+1})
of
\overline{x}
so that
\tau^{j}h[\overline{x}]=\tau^{j+l}x
. Apply Theorem
4.20
with parameters
1\leqslant j+l+1<r+j+1
to the stems

h(\tau^{j}[\overline{x}])=\tau^{j+l}\rho^{n+m+1}_{1}[x]=\rho^{r+j+1}_{j+l+1}(\tau^{j+l}[x]),

we obtain
[b]\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+l+1}(Y/\tau^{n+m})
so that
f(\tau^{j+l}[x])=\tau^{j+l+1}[b],g[b]=\delta_{j+l+1}^{n+m}(\tau^{j}[\overline{x}])=\delta_{l+1}^{n+m}[\overline{x}]
. Thus, the class
\tau^{l}([b]-\tau^{n-1}[y])\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+1}(Y/\tau^{r})
is
\tau^{j+1}
-torsion and
\tau^{l}
-divisible, and item 5 implies (through Lemma
3.11
) that all such stems in this bidegree are also
\tau^{n+l}
-divisible. Therefore,
\tau^{l}[b]=\tau^{n+l-1}[y]^{\prime}
where
[y]^{\prime}=[y]+\tau\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon\in\pi_{t-s-1,t+n+l+1}(Y/\tau^{m})
. Item 3 and item 4 imply that
\tau^{n+l-1}g[y]^{\prime}=\tau^{r-1}\overline{y}
due to Corollary
3.9
, so
\delta_{1}^{r}\overline{x}=\tau^{l}\delta_{l+1}^{n+m}[\overline{x}]=\tau^{l}g[b]=\tau^{r-1}\overline{y}
. By Theorem
2.38
,
\overline{x},\overline{y}
are actually
r
-cycles in the standard SS of
Z
and
d_{r+1}(\overline{x})=\overline{y}
.
∎

Remark 4.27
.

We briefly discuss how to choose parameters when we apply Theorem
4.26
.

•

The parameters
n,m,l
are fixed by the desideratum.

•

The parameter
j
can be as large as possible to guarantee the extension from
\overline{x}
to
x
is unobstructed by early boundaries (i.e.
\tau
-power torsion lifts). In practice, often
j=0
suffices.

in

d_{1}^{[h_{1}],E_{3}}(h_{1}\Delta_{1}h_{1}^{2})=h_{5}Pe_{0}\qquad\text{and}\qquad d_{1}^{[h_{1}],E_{3}}(d_{0}g^{2})=d_{0}\Delta h_{0}^{2}e_{0}.

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d_{5}(h_{5}Pe_{0})=d_{0}\Delta h_{0}^{2}e_{0}
in the Adams SS of
