.

In Table
2
, “restriction” stands for a direct comparison with the
C_{2}
-slice SS along the map
\mathrm{res}\colon Y\to Y[C_{4}/C_{2}]
, “transfer” stands for Remark
5.24
(i.e. a direct comparison with the
C_{2}
-slice SS along the map
\mathrm{tr}\colon Y[C_{4}/C_{2}]\to Y
), “transchromatic isomorphism” stands for Theorem
5.27
, “exotic transfer” stands for Theorem
5.29
(more precisely, here it comes from Example
5.31
), and “HHR norm” stands for the norm differential theorem (cf.
[
HHR-BPC4<1>
, Theorem 4.7]
). The remaining one,
d_{7}(u_{4\lambda})
, can be proved via the Leibniz rule for total differentials (i.e. Theorem
2.50
) as follows:

Proposition 5.34
.

d_{7}(u_{4\lambda})=\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{1}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{t}_{1}a_{\sigma_{2}})u_{2\lambda}u_{\sigma}a_{3\lambda}
.

Proof.

Write
X=P_{\bullet}\mathrm{MU}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}
and
Y=P_{\bullet}\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 1\rangle
. As
u_{2\lambda}
is a
d_{4}
-cycle, there exists
[u_{2\lambda}]^{\prime}\in\pi_{4-2\lambda,0}(Y/\tau^{4})
lifting
u_{2\lambda}
, which further lifts
25
25

25

Strictly speaking, we should also check that
u_{2\lambda}
is a
d_{4}
-cycle in the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{MU}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}
. But this is automatic: For degree reasons the only other possibility is that
u_{2\lambda}
supports an essential
d_{3}
here, which does not happen because the potential target bidegree
(3-2\lambda,3)
of the
C_{4}
-slice SS of
\mathrm{MU}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}
is isomorphic to that of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 1\rangle
. In fact, both of them are
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-vector spaces of dimension
1
generated by the class
\mathrm{tr}(\bar{t}_{1}u_{2\sigma_{2}}a_{3\sigma_{2}})
.
to a bigraded stem
[u_{2\lambda}]\in\pi_{4-2\lambda,0}(X/\tau^{4})
. Applying the total Leibniz rule (i.e. Theorem
2.50
) to the map in
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}

\psi_{0}(\Sigma^{-V,0}X)\otimes\psi_{0}(\Sigma^{-V,0}X)\to\psi_{0}(\Sigma^{-2V,0}X)

induced from the
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-ring structure on
X
and the lax symmetric monoidality of
\psi_{0}=\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(\mathbb{S}^{0,0},-)
(here
V=4-2\lambda
), we compute that

\delta_{4}^{4}([u_{2\lambda}]^{2})=2[u_{2\lambda}]\delta_{4}^{4}([u_{2\lambda}])=[u_{2\lambda}]\delta_{4}^{4}(2[u_{2\lambda}])

in the slice SS of
X
. Therefore, by projecting along the map
X\to Y
, we find a bigraded stem
[u_{4\lambda}]\in\pi_{8-4\lambda,0}(Y/\tau^{4})
lifting
u_{4\lambda}
whose
\delta_{4}^{4}
is equal to
[u_{2\lambda}]\delta_{4}^{4}(2[u_{2\lambda}]^{\prime})
. Note that there is a transfer differential
d_{7}(2u_{2\lambda})=\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{1}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{t}_{1}a_{\sigma_{2}})u_{\sigma}a_{3\lambda}
, for which the only possible crossing is a
d_{3}
-differential into the same target bidegree
(3-2\lambda,7)
. As this transfer
d_{7}
has no crossing on the
E_{5}
-page, Corollary
3.17
implies that
\delta_{4}^{4}(2[u_{2\lambda}]^{\prime})=\tau^{2}[y]
where
[y]\in\pi_{3-2\lambda,6}(Y/\tau^{4})
is a lift of
y=\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{1}\mathrm{tr}(\bar{t}_{1}a_{\sigma_{2}})u_{\sigma}a_{3\lambda}
. Thus,
\delta_{4}^{4}([u_{4\lambda}])=\tau^{2}[u_{2\lambda}][y]
, so
d_{7}(u_{4\lambda})=u_{2\lambda}y
due to Theorem
2.38
.
∎

In summary, using Burklund–Lin–Wang–Xu methods, we obtain a satisfactory interpretation for the
C_{4}
-slice SS computation of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 1\rangle
: each generating differential in the
C_{4}
-slice SS can be deduced from a systematic construction that also produces new families of differentials for all
m\geqslant 1
(and potentially all groups
G
), which replaces all ad-hoc case-by-case arguments in previous computations. In ongoing work, we will try to develop a similar understanding for the
C_{4}
-slice SS computation of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(C_{4})\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle 2\rangle
using Burklund–Lin–Wang–Xu methods, together with the norm structure on the slice filtration. This will be our second step towards a systematic computation of the slice SS of
\mathrm{BP}^{(\mkern-4.22mu(G)\mkern-3.5mu)}\langle m\rangle
.

Appendix A
Appendix · Rigidity of the synthetic Adams SS

Let
E
be a homotopy ring spectrum of Adams type (cf.
[
Pst23
, Definition 3.14]
). Write
\mathsf{Syn}_{E}
for the
\infty
-category of
E
-synthetic spectra, and write
\nu\colon\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Syn}_{E}
for the synthetic analogue functor. In this appendix, for any reasonable spectrum
X
, we identify the synthetic
\nu E
-Adams SS of
\nu X
and
\nu X/\tau^{r}
(as well as their abutments) with trigraded versions of the
\tau
-Bockstein SS, cf. Theorem
A.9
. We thus obtain a complete description of these synthetic
\nu E
-Adams SS in terms of the
E
-Adams SS of
X
due to “rigidity” of the trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS, cf. Theorem
A.6
and Remark
A.8
.

Remark A.1
.

The results of this appendix are used only in Remark
3.3
to compare Definition
3.1
with the extension spectral sequence approach in
[
Lin-Wang-Xu-kervaire
]
; the remainder of the paper is independent of Appendix
A
.

Definition A.2
.

We write
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp})
for the
\infty
-category of
bifiltered spectra
.

•

The
\infty
-category
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
is presentable and stable.

•

We equip
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp})
with the Day convolution symmetric monoidal structure, which makes it presentably symmetric monoidal. This is the same as the Day convolution symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Sp})
. We denote by
\mathbb{1}
its tensor unit.

•

There are three families of invertible objects in
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
:

–

By construction, the spectral Yoneda embedding functor

\gamma\colon\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\times\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\to\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp},\quad(a,b)\mapsto\gamma_{(a,b)}=\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\mathrm{Map}(-,(a,b))

is symmetric monoidal, thus each
\gamma_{(a,b)}
is invertible in
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
with inverse
\gamma_{(-a,-b)}
. We write
\mathbb{S}^{0,a,b}=\gamma_{(a,b)}
. Concretely, the image of
(x,y)\in\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}
under
\mathbb{S}^{0,a,b}
is
\mathbb{S}^{0}
if
x\leqslant a,y\leqslant b
or
0
otherwise. In particular,
\mathbb{S}^{0,0,0}=\mathbb{1}
.

–

On the other hand, each suspension
\Sigma^{n}\mathbb{1}
is also invertible. We write
\mathbb{S}^{n,0,0}=\Sigma^{n}\mathbb{1}
.

In general, we write
\mathbb{S}^{n,a,b}=\mathbb{S}^{n,0,0}\otimes\mathbb{S}^{0,a,b}
, and we write
\Sigma^{n,a,b}X=X\otimes\mathbb{S}^{n,a,b}
.

•

We write
\tau^{r}_{h}\colon\mathbb{S}^{0,-r,0}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0,0}
(resp.
\tau^{r}_{v}\colon\mathbb{S}^{0,0,-r}\to\mathbb{S}^{0,0,0}
) for the image of
(-r,0)\to(0,0)
(resp.
(0,-r)\to(0,0)
) under
\gamma
, and refer to it as the
horizontal
(resp.
vertical
)
deformation parameter
in
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
. For general
Y\in\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
, we also write
\tau^{r}_{h}
for
Y\otimes\tau^{r}_{h}\colon\Sigma^{0,-r,0}Y\to Y
, and similarly for
\tau^{r}_{v}
. It follows from the discussion in Construction
2.43
that both
\mathbb{1}/\tau^{r}_{h}
and
\mathbb{1}/\tau^{r}_{v}
acquire the structure of an
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebra in
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
.

•

Also,
\mathbb{1}[\tau_{h}^{-1}]
and
\mathbb{1}[\tau_{v}^{-1}]
are both idempotent
\mathbb{E}_{\infty}
-algebras in
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
, so that
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}[\tau_{h}^{-1}]}\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
and
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{1}[\tau_{v}^{-1}]}\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
are both equivalent to
\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. The functor
-[\tau_{h}^{-1}]\colon\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
sends
\mathbb{S}^{n,a,b}
to
\mathbb{S}^{n,b}
,
\tau_{h}
to
\mathrm{id}
and
\tau_{v}
to
\tau
. Similar results hold true for the functor
-[\tau_{v}^{-1}]
.

•

In practice, we write
Y=\{Y_{w}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
for an arbitrary object in
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp})
. Here we treat
w
as the vertical coordinate, while each
Y_{w}\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
occupies a horizontal row. Under this convention,
\tau_{h}
is the map
\{\tau\colon\Sigma^{0,-1}Y_{w}\to Y_{w}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
, while
\tau_{v}
is the map
\{Y_{w+1}\to Y_{w}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

Construction A.3
.

We construct below the trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS.

•

We denote by
\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{SS})=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{SS})
the
1
-category of
filtered SS
. Concretely, a filtered SS
E=\{E_{r}^{s,t,w}\}
consists of the following data:

–

A family of abelian groups
\{E_{r}^{s,t,w}\}_{r\geqslant 2,s,t,w\in\mathbb{Z}}
.

–

A family of maps
d_{r}\colon E_{r}^{s,t,w}\to E_{r}^{s+r,t+r-1,w}
, together with isomorphisms to witness that
\{(E_{r}^{s,t,w},d_{r})\}_{r\geqslant 2,s,t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a SS in the sense of Definition
2.21
for each fixed
w
.

–

A family of maps
\tau\colon E_{r}^{s,t,w}\to E_{r}^{s,t,w-1}
such that
d_{r}\tau=\tau d_{r}
.

By combining Theorem
and
[
HA
, Example 2.2.6.9]
, we equip
\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{SS})
with the structure of a
1
-truncated
\infty
-operad under Day convolution.

•

The functor
E_{*}^{*,*}\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{SS}
in Theorem
2.26
induces a
trigraded standard SS
functor

E_{*}^{*,*,*}\colon\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp})\to\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{SS}),\quad Y=\{Y_{w}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}\mapsto\{E_{r}^{s,t,w}(Y)=E_{r}^{s,t}(Y_{w})\}_{r\geqslant 2,s,t,w\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Theorem
implies
E_{*}^{*,*,*}
is also lax symmetric monoidal under Day convolutions. In particular, a multiplicative bifiltration gives rise to a multiplicative trigraded standard SS.

•

For
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
and
w\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
\kappa_{\geqslant w}X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
for the filtration

\cdots\to X(w+2)\to X(w+1)\to X(w)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}X(w)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\cdots

where the first
X(w)
sits in filtration
w
. The assignments
X\mapsto\{\kappa_{\geqslant w}X\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
assemble into a functor
\kappa\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
. Formally, write
\Delta\colon\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\hookrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\times\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}
for the diagonal inclusion, then
\kappa\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Sp})\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathsf{Sp})=\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
computes the left Kan extension along
\Delta
. Since
\Delta
is a map of commutative monoids,
\kappa
is symmetric monoidal under Day convolutions. Additionally, for each
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
,
\kappa X[\tau_{h}^{-1}]
and
\kappa X[\tau_{v}^{-1}]
can both be identified with
X
, while
(\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{r})[\tau_{h}^{-1}]\cong X/\tau^{r}
. We call
\kappa X
the
\tau
-Bockstein bifiltration
of
X
.

•

For
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, we refer to
E_{*}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X)
as the
trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS
of
X
. Also, for
1\leqslant r<\infty
, we refer to
E_{*}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{r})
as the
(refined) trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS
of
X/\tau^{r}
. The two functors
E_{*}^{*,*,*}(\kappa(-)),E_{*}^{*,*,*}(\kappa(-)/\tau_{v}^{r})\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{SS})
are both lax symmetric monoidal, so multiplicative filtrations lead to multiplicative (refined) trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS.

Remark A.4
.

The functor
\kappa\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
admits a right adjoint
\kappa^{R}
given by restriction along
\Delta\colon\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\hookrightarrow\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}\times\mathchoice{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{$\scriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.54247pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}{\mathord{\accentset{\smash{\raisebox{-0.45206pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\rightharpoonup$}}}{\mathbb{Z}}}}
. The unit
\mathrm{id}\to\kappa^{R}\kappa
is an isomorphism, so
\kappa
is fully faithful, and it induces an equivalence

\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\cong\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\mathrm{de}}
where
\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\mathrm{de}}\subset\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
consists of bifiltrations
Y=\{Y_{w}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
such that for each
w\in\mathbb{Z},k\in\mathbb{N}
the maps
Y_{w}(w)\to Y_{w}(w-k)
and
Y_{w}(w)\to Y_{w-k}(w)
are isomorphisms
26
26

26

In other words, such bifiltrations are “diagonally extended”, i.e. left Kan extended from the diagonal filtration
.
According to Remark
2.12
part 2, if
Y=\{Y_{w}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
is
horizontally complete
(i.e. each
Y_{w}
is complete), then
Y\in\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\mathrm{de}}
iff
E_{2}^{s,t,w}(Y)=0
for
w>t
and
\tau\colon E_{2}^{s,t,w}(Y)\to E_{2}^{s,t,w-1}(Y)
is an isomorphism for
w\leqslant t
.

Remark A.5
.

Some literature reserves the name “trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS” for the trigraded SS
E^{\prime}=\{(E^{\prime})_{r}^{s,t,w}\}
whose weight-
w
part comes from
\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},-)
image of

\cdots\xrightarrow{\tau}\Sigma^{0,-3}X\xrightarrow{\tau}\Sigma^{0,-2}X\xrightarrow{\tau}\Sigma^{0,-1}X\xrightarrow{\tau}X\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}X\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\cdots\in\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}).

In other words,
(E^{\prime})_{r}^{s,t,w}=E_{r}^{s,t}(\Sigma^{0,-w}\kappa_{\geqslant w}X)
. This is not very different from the construction above, since for each fixed weight
w
there is an isomorphism of SS
(E^{\prime})_{r}^{s,t,w}\cong E_{r}^{s+w,t+w,w}(\kappa X)
. We prefer our construction for the following reasons:

•

The natural maps
(E^{\prime})_{r}^{s,t,w}\to(E^{\prime})_{r}^{s,t,w-1}
induced by
\Sigma^{0,-w}\kappa_{\geqslant w}X\to\Sigma^{0,1-w}\kappa_{\geqslant w-1}X
are zero for each
r
. To recover the
\tau
-multiplication in
E_{r}^{s,t,w}(\kappa X)
, one has to set up another operator “
\tau
” on
E^{\prime}
with tridegree
(1,1,-1)
, which leads to unnecessary complications.

•

In Theorem
A.9
we identify synthetic Adams SS with the trigraded Bockstein SS as in Construction
A.3
. If we use the
E^{\prime}
-convention instead, they are no longer isomorphic on the nose, but only after an appropriate reindexing.

Theorem A.6

(Rigidity)
.

Take
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
. The trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS
E_{*}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X)
and
E_{*}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{n})
are completely determined by the standard SS
E_{*}^{*,*}=E_{*}^{*,*}(X)
(together with
Z_{*}^{*,*},B_{*}^{*,*}
in Remark
2.23
).

1.

E_{2}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X)\cong E_{2}^{*,*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\tau]
, where
x\in E_{2}^{s,t}
sits in tridegree
(s,t,t)
and
|\tau|=(0,0,-1)
. More generally, for
1\leqslant r\leqslant\infty
,

E_{r+1}^{s,t,w}(\kappa X)\cong\begin{cases}Z_{r}^{s,t}/B_{\min\{t-w+1,r\}}^{s,t},&t\geqslant w.\\
0,&t<w.\end{cases}

For differentials, there is a differential
d_{r+1}(x)=y
in the standard SS iff there is a differential
d_{r+1}(x)=\tau^{r}y
in the trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS. Moreover, in this case we also have
d_{r+1}(\tau^{k}x)=\tau^{r+k}y
for
k\geqslant 0
, and all nontrivial differentials in the trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS are of this form.

2.

If
X
is complete, then each
\kappa_{\geqslant w}X
is complete. If furthermore all weak obstructions
RE_{\infty}^{s,t}(X)=\lim^{1}_{r}Z_{r}^{s,t}
vanish, then
\{E_{r}^{s,t,w}(\kappa X)\}
converges strongly to
\pi_{t-s,w}(X)
equipped with the
\IIm(\tau^{a})
filtration in Theorem
2.34
, where
\IIm(\tau^{a})\subset\pi_{m,w}(X)
sits in filtration
w+a
.

3.

E_{2}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{n})\cong E_{2}^{*,*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\tau]/\tau^{n}
where
x\in E_{2}^{s,t}
sits in tridegree
(s,t,t)
and
|\tau|=(0,0,-1)
. More generally, for
1\leqslant r\leqslant\infty
,

E_{r+1}^{s,t,w}(\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{n})\cong\begin{cases}0,&t\geqslant w+n.\\
Z_{\min\{w+n-t,r\}}^{s,t}/B_{\min\{t-w+1,r\}}^{s,t},&w\leqslant t<w+n.\\
0,&t<w.\end{cases}

For differentials, there is a differential
d_{r+1}(x)=y
in the standard SS with
r<n
iff there is a differential
d_{r+1}(x)=\tau^{r}y
in the refined trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS. Moreover, in this case we also have
d_{r+1}(\tau^{k}x)=\tau^{r+k}y
for
0\leqslant k<n-r
, and all nontrivial differentials in the refined trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS are of this form. In particular, there is no nontrivial differential starting from the
E_{n+1}
-page. Also,
\{E_{r+1}^{s,t,w}(\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{n})\}
always converges strongly to
\pi_{t-s,w}(X/\tau^{n})
equipped with the
\IIm(\tau^{a})
-filtration in Theorem
2.32
, where
\IIm(\tau^{a})\subset\pi_{m,w}(X/\tau^{n})
sits in filtration
w+a
.

Proof.

For part 1, note that
(\kappa_{\geqslant w}X)(t)=X(\max\{t,w\})
. For fixed
w
,
E_{r+1}^{s,t,w}(\kappa X)\cong E_{r+1}^{s,t}(\kappa_{\geqslant w}X)\cong Z_{r}^{s,t}(\kappa_{\geqslant w}X)/B_{r}^{s,t}(\kappa_{\geqslant w}X)
. Expanding the construction in Theorem
2.26
,
Z_{r}^{s,t}(\kappa_{\geqslant w}X)
is the image of
\rho\colon\pi_{t-s}(X(\max\{t,w\})/X(\max\{t+r,w\}))\to\pi_{t-s}(X(\max\{t,w\})/X(\max\{t+1,w\}))
. Thus,
Z_{r}^{s,t}(\kappa_{\geqslant w}X)
is isomorphic to
Z_{r}^{s,t}
if
t\geqslant w
, and it vanishes otherwise. Furthermore, for
t\geqslant w
,
B_{r}^{s,t}(\kappa_{\geqslant w}X)
is the image of
\delta_{r}\colon\pi_{t-s-1}(X(\max\{t-r+1,w\})/X(t))\to\pi_{t-s}(X(t))\to\pi_{t-s}(X(t)/X(t+1))
, which is precisely
B_{t-\max\{w,t-r+1\}+1}^{s,t}=B_{\min\{t-w+1,r\}}^{s,t}
. We have thus computed
E_{r+1}^{s,t,w}(\kappa X)
for each
1\leqslant r<\infty
(the result for
r=\infty
also follows by taking limits), which in particular recovers the expression for
E_{2}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X)
. As for differentials, the natural map
\kappa_{\geqslant w}X\to\kappa X[\tau_{v}^{-1}]
defines a map of SS
E_{*}^{**w}(\kappa X)\to E_{*}^{*,*}(X)
which is injective on each
E_{r+1}
entry that could receive a nontrivial
d_{r+1}
and surjective on each
E_{r+1}
entry that could support a nontrivial
d_{r+1}
, so it determines the pattern of
d_{r+1}
for each
r\geqslant 1
as stated above.

Part 2 follows from applying the result on strong convergence in Theorem
2.34
item 2 to each filtration
\kappa_{\geqslant w}X
. Here the whole plane obstructions vanish due to Remark
2.35
and the fact that each
\kappa_{\geqslant w}X
gives rise to a half-plane SS.

For part 3, one computes
E_{r+1}^{s,t,w}(\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{n})\cong E_{r+1}^{s,t}(\kappa_{\geqslant w}X/\kappa_{\geqslant w+n}X)
using the same method as in the proof of part 1. For differentials, the map
\kappa X\to\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{n}
induces a map of filtered SS
E_{*}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X)\to E_{*}^{*,*,*}(\kappa X/\tau_{v}^{n})
that is injective on each
E_{r+1}
entry that could receive a nontrivial
d_{r+1}
and surjective on each
E_{r+1}
entry that could support a nontrivial
d_{r+1}
, so it determines the pattern of
d_{r+1}
for each
r\geqslant 1
as stated above. The strong convergence here also follows from Theorem
2.34
item 2 applied to
\kappa_{\geqslant w}X/\kappa_{\geqslant w+n}X
(these are complete filtrations with vanishing obstructions for degree reasons).
∎

Remark A.7
.

If one chooses to avoid
\tau,\rho,\delta
when setting up the theory of standard SS and strong convergence while recovering the relation to these operators afterwards, then one can establish Theorem
A.6
first (by combining the argument above and an appropriate paraphrase of
[
Boa99
, § 7]
) and use it to prove Theorem
2.34
and Theorem
2.32
, cf.
[
vN25
, §3]
.

Remark A.8
.

In this remark we go through the story of
rigidity
for spectral sequences.

1.

Since filtered colimits are exact in
\mathsf{Ab}
, there is a functor
-[\tau^{-1}]\colon\mathsf{Fil}(\mathsf{SS})\to\mathsf{SS}
sending
E=\{E_{r}^{s,t,w}\}_{r\geqslant 2,s,t,w\in\mathbb{Z}}
to
E[\tau^{-1}]=\{\colim_{w\to-\infty}E_{r}^{s,t,w}\}_{r\geqslant 2,s,t\in\mathbb{Z}}
. We say a filtered SS
E
is
rigid
(over
E[\tau^{-1}]
) if
E
is determined by
E[\tau^{-1}]
in the sense of Theorem
A.6
part 1.

2.

Suppose
E=\{E_{r}^{s,t,w}\}
is a filtered SS,
E^{\prime}=\{(E^{\prime})_{r}^{s,t}\}
is a SS,
\{f_{w}\colon E_{*}^{**w}\to(E^{\prime})_{*}^{*,*}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a family of maps of SS so that
f_{w}\tau=f_{w+1}
for each
w\in\mathbb{Z}
, and these maps induce an isomorphism
E_{2}^{*,*,*}\cong(E^{\prime}_{2})^{*,*}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\tau]
. Then by induction on
1\leqslant r<\infty
, the map
\{f_{w}\colon E_{r+1}^{**w}\to(E^{\prime})_{r+1}^{*,*}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
is injective on each
E_{r+1}
entry that could receive a nontrivial
d_{r+1}
and surjective on each
E_{r+1}
entry that could support a nontrivial
d_{r+1}
, so the
d_{r+1}
-patterns in
E
and
E^{\prime}
determine each other. Thus, a page-by-page computation shows
E
is rigid over
E[\tau^{-1}]\cong E^{\prime}
.

3.

According to Theorem
A.6
part 1, if
Y\in\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
is isomorphic to
\kappa X
for some
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
, then
E_{*}^{*,*,*}(Y)
is rigid over
E_{*}^{*,*,*}(Y)[\tau^{-1}]\cong E_{*}^{*,*}(X)
.

4.

Conversely, suppose
Y=\{Y_{w}\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}
is horizontally complete (cf. Remark
A.4
),
X\in\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is complete, and there is a family of compatible maps
\{Y_{w}\to X\}_{w\in\mathbb{Z}}
which induce an isomorphism
E_{2}^{*,*,*}(Y)\cong E_{2}^{*,*}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\tau]
. Then
Y[\tau_{v}^{-1}]\cong X
due to Remark
2.12
part 2. Moreover, Remark
A.4
shows
Y\in\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\mathrm{de}}
, and
-[\tau_{v}^{-1}]\colon\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\mathrm{de}}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
is an equivalence since it is a left inverse to
\kappa\colon\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}\to\mathsf{biFil}\mathsf{Sp}_{\mathrm{de}}
. Therefore,
Y\cong\kappa X
.

Consequently, trigraded spectral sequences with the rigidity property are precisely the shadows of
\tau
-Bockstein bifiltrations. Examples of this rigidity property include the motivic Adams–Novikov SS (cf.
[
Isaksen-stable-stems
, § 6.1 and § 6.2]
) which is a special case of Remark
A.10
, and the motivic Cartan–Eilenberg SS (cf.
[
Burklund-Xu
, Theorem 2.8]
) which follows directly from item 2 (and item 4) above.

We now compare these with the synthetic Adams SS. Let
E
be a homotopy ring spectrum of Adams type. Recall the forgetful functor
U=\mathrm{map}^{\mathsf{Fil}}(\mathbb{S}^{0,0}_{E},-)\colon\mathsf{Syn}_{E}\to\mathsf{Fil}\mathsf{Sp}
as in Remark
2.66
.

Theorem A.9
.

Suppose
X\in\mathsf{Sp}
is
E
-nilpotent complete.

1.

The
E
-Adams SS of
X
, starting from its
E_{2}
-page, coincides with the standard SS of
U(\nu X)
.

2.

The synthetic
\nu E
-Adams SS of
\nu X
, starting from its
E_{2}
-page, coincides with the trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS of
U(\nu X)
.

3.

For each
1\leqslant r<\infty
, the synthetic
\nu E
-Adams SS of
\nu X/\tau^{r}
, starting from its
E_{2}
-page, coincides with the refined trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS of
U(\nu X)/\tau^{r}
.

The identifications above are also compatible with their abutments, so the
\IIm(\tau^{a})
-filtrations on
\pi_{**}(\nu X)
and
\pi_{**}(\nu X/\tau^{r})
, as in Theorems
2.34
and
2.32
, coincide with their synthetic
\nu E
-Adams filtrations.

Proof.

To identify the SS and the abutment altogether, it suffices to identify the filtered spectra behind them. The result of part 1 is standard: we have

\displaystyle U(\nu X)(w)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},\nu X)\cong\lim_{[k]\in\Delta}\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},\nu X\otimes\nu E^{\otimes k})

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{[k]\in\Delta}\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},\nu(X\otimes E^{\otimes k}))\cong\lim_{[k]\in\Delta}\tau_{\geqslant w}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k}).

Here the first
\cong
follows from
[
BHS1
, Proposition A.13]
, the second
\cong
follows from
[
Pst23
, Lemma 4.24]
, and the third
\cong
follows from the fact that
\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},\nu M)\cong\tau_{\geqslant w}M
for each
M\in\mathsf{Sp}
with a homotopy
E
-module structure (which boils down to
[
Pst23
, Proposition 4.60]
). On the other hand, the
E
-Adams SS of
X
is constructed using the
E
-Adams tower coming from the fiber of
\mathbb{S}^{0}\to E
. According to
[
MNN17
, Proposition 2.14]
and
[
Lev15
, Proposition 6.3]
, the filtered spectrum for this SS, starting from its
E_{2}
-page, is also given by
\lim_{[k]\in\Delta}\tau_{\geqslant\bullet}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k})
. We thus conclude part 1.

For part 2, the weight
=w
part of the trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS of
U(\nu X)
comes from the filtration
\kappa_{\geqslant w}U(\nu X)
.
On the other hand, the synthetic
\nu E
-Adams SS of
\nu X
is constructed using the
\nu E
-Adams tower coming from the fiber of
\nu\mathbb{S}^{0}\to\nu E
. Thus, the same argument shows the filtered spectrum for the weight
=w
part of this SS, starting from its
E_{2}
-page,
is given by
\lim_{[k]\in\Delta}\tau_{\geqslant\bullet}(\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},\nu X\otimes\nu E^{\otimes k}))
. Note that
\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},\nu X\otimes\nu E^{\otimes k})\cong\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},\nu(X\otimes E^{\otimes k}))\cong\tau_{\geqslant w}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k})
, and
\tau_{\geqslant\bullet}(\tau_{\geqslant w}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k}))\cong\kappa_{\geqslant w}(\tau_{\geqslant\bullet}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k}))
.
Since
\kappa_{\geqslant w}
preserves limits, the totalization yields
\kappa_{\geqslant w}U(\nu X)
as well.

For part 3, the weight
=w
part of the refined trigraded
\tau
-Bockstein SS for
U(\nu X)/\tau^{r}
comes from the filtration
\kappa_{\geqslant w}U(\nu X)/\kappa_{\geqslant w+r}U(\nu X)
.
Also, the identical argument as before shows the filtered spectrum for the weight
=w
part of the synthetic
\nu E
-Adams SS of
\nu X/\tau^{r}
, starting from its
E_{2}
-page,
is given by
\lim_{[k]\in\Delta}\tau_{\geqslant\bullet}(\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},(\nu X/\tau^{r})\otimes\nu E^{\otimes k}))
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},(\nu X/\tau^{r})\otimes\nu E^{\otimes k})

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{map}(\mathbb{S}^{0,w},\nu(X\otimes E^{\otimes k})/\tau^{r})

\displaystyle\cong\tau_{\geqslant w}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k})/\tau_{\geqslant w+r}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k})

and
\tau_{\geqslant\bullet}(\tau_{\geqslant w}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k})/\tau_{\geqslant w+r}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k}))\cong\kappa_{\geqslant w}(\tau_{\geqslant\bullet}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k}))/\kappa_{\geqslant w+r}(\tau_{\geqslant\bullet}(X\otimes E^{\otimes k}))
.
Thus, the totalization also gives rise to
\kappa_{\geqslant w}(U(\nu X))/\kappa_{\geqslant w+r}(U(\nu X))
.
∎

Remark A.10
.

In terms of Remark
A.8
, the above theorem implies that the synthetic
\nu E
-Adams SS of
\nu X
is rigid (over the
E
-Adams SS of
X
).

Appendix B
Appendix · Proof of the coherent Mahowald trick

In this appendix we prove Theorem
4.17
. Our strategy goes as follows:
For any stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
and any
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we construct
an “auxiliary”
2
d cuDT of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

for which we can pin down all data (objects, maps, squares,
\alpha
-isomorphisms,
\beta
-isomorphisms, etc.) explicitly. We then reduce Theorem
4.17
to the full subcategory
\IIm(\phi^{*}\colon\mathsf{Aux}(\mathcal{C})\hookrightarrow 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C}))
spanned by such
2
d cuDTs, and prove it in these cases through a simple diagram chase.

Remark B.1
.

Actually, according to Remark
4.19
, we can further reduce to just one concrete example, namely the auxiliary
2
d cuDT for
X=\mathrm{H}\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Sp}
(or
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{D}(\mathbb{Q})
).

We will continue to use the notations from §
4.1
. The crucial step for our strategy is the following construction.

Construction B.2
.

Write
\mathcal{Q}
for the sub-poset
(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)\times(\mathbb{Z},\leqslant)
consisting of pairs
(i,j)
such that
-1\leqslant i-j\leqslant 1
. There is a well-defined map of sets

\phi\colon\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q},\qquad(m,n,a,b)\mapsto(k,k)+\epsilon(m,n,a,b),

where
s=m+n+a+b
,
k=\lfloor s/3\rfloor
and the correction term
\epsilon=\epsilon(m,n,a,b)
takes values in the five-element set
\{(0,0),(1,0),(0,1),(-1,0),(0,-1)\}
. More precisely, the value of
\epsilon(m,n,a,b)
is determined by the table below:

s\mod 3

Table 3:
Values of the correction term
\epsilon=\epsilon(m,n,a,b)
.

The specific formula for
\phi
is not essential in itself; what matters is that it gives an explicit map
\phi\colon\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
with the following properties.

Lemma B.3
.

1.

The map
\phi\colon\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is an order-preserving surjection. Also, if
(m,n,a,b)
lies in the
\phi
-preimage of some
(i,i)
, then
0\leqslant m-n\leqslant 1
and
0\leqslant a-b\leqslant 1
.

2.

Furthermore, if a square in
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
is of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,n,a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m+1,n,a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,n+1,a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m+1,n+1,a,b)}

or

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,n,a,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,n,a+1,b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,n,a,b+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(m,n,a+1,b+1)}

with
0\leqslant m-n\leqslant 1
,
0\leqslant a-b\leqslant 1
, then its
\phi
-image in
\mathcal{Q}
has one of the following shapes (up to transpose):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i+1,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i+1,j+1)}
or
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i+1,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i+1,j)}
or
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j+1)}

Proof.

We start by introducing some notation:

•

We say
(m,n)\in\mathcal{P}
is
of type T
if
0\leqslant m-n\leqslant 1
, and it is
of type F
otherwise.

•

For
(m,n,a,b)\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
, we say it is of type TT if
(m,n)
and
(a,b)
are both of type T. Likewise, in
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
we can make sense of type TF, FT and FF.

•

We say
(m,n)\leqslant(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{P}
is an
increment
if
m^{\prime}=m+1,n^{\prime}=n
or
m^{\prime}=m,n^{\prime}=n+1
. The order relation on
\mathcal{P}
is generated by increments, i.e. each nontrivial
(m,n)\leqslant(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{P}
factors as a finite chain of increments. Note that there are only three types of increments in
\mathcal{P}
: T to T, T to F, F to T. For degree reasons, there is no increment of type F to F.

•

The order relation on
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
is generated by increments in two directions. We say increments
(m,n,a,b)\leqslant(m^{\prime},n^{\prime},a,b)
are
horizontal
and increments
(m,n,a,b)\leqslant(m,n,a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
are
vertical
.

•

The poset
\mathcal{Q}
admits a
\mathbb{Z}/2
action generated by the
swap
map
\rho\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q},(i,j)\mapsto(j,i)
.

We now prove part 1. The map
\phi
is surjective since each element of
\mathcal{Q}
takes one of the forms
(i,i)
,
(i,i)+(0,-1)
, or
(i,i)+(-1,0)
, which are hit respectively by
(i+1,i,\lfloor(i+1)/2\rfloor,\lceil(i-1)/2\rceil)
,
(i,i,\lfloor(i+1)/2\rfloor,\lceil(i-1)/2\rceil)
, or
(i+1,i-1,\lfloor(i+1)/2\rfloor,\lceil(i-1)/2\rceil)
. Also, if
\phi(m,n,a,b)=(i,i)
, then
\epsilon(m,n,a,b)=0
, which forces
(m,n,a,b)
to be of type TT by definition.

To show
\phi(m,n,a,b)\leqslant\phi(m^{\prime},n^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
whenever
(m,n,a,b)\leqslant(m^{\prime},n^{\prime},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
, it suffices to consider increments. We first fix
(a,b)
and treat the horizontal increments
(m,n)\leqslant(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. It is safe to assume
a+b-k\equiv 0
mod
2
, as
\epsilon
-images for the case
a+b-k\equiv 1
mod
2
would only differ by a swap. If
(a,b)
is of type T, then the
\epsilon
-image of this horizontal increment is given by

TT to TT

TT to FT

FT to TT

0

(0,-1)
to
(0,0)

(0,-1)
to
(0,-1)

(-1,0)
to
(0,0)

1

(0,0)
to
(0,0)

(0,0)
to
(0,1)

(0,-1)
to
(0,0)

2

(0,0)
to
(-1,0)

(0,0)
to
(0,-1)

(0,1)
to
(-1,0)

We access the table as follows: the top left slot reads “
(0,-1)
to
(0,0)
”, which means if
(m,n,a,b)
satisfies
s=m+n+a+b\equiv 0
mod
3
and
(m,n),(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
are both of type T, then

\phi(m,n,a,b)=(k,k)+(0,-1)=(k,k-1)\leqslant\phi(m^{\prime},n^{\prime},a,b)=(k,k)+(0,0)=(k,k)

so
\phi
is order-preserving in this case. Using the first two rows above, this argument shows that
\phi
is order preserving for
s\equiv 0,1
mod
3
. If
s\equiv 2
mod
3
, then
k^{\prime}=\lfloor(m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}+a+b)/3\rfloor=k+1
. Thus, for instance the bottom right slot reads “
(0,1)
to
(-1,0)
”, which means if
(m,n,a,b)
satisfies
s=m+n+a+b\equiv 2
mod
3
,
(m,n)
is of type F and
(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})
is of type T, then

\phi(m,n,a,b)=(k,k)+(0,1)=(k,k+1)\leqslant\phi(m^{\prime},n^{\prime},a,b)=(k+1,k+1)+(-1,0)=(k,k+1).

Using the third row, this argument shows that
\phi
is also order preserving for
s\equiv 2
mod
3
.

If
(a,b)
is of type F, then the
\epsilon
-image of this increment is given by another table:

TF to TF

TF to FF

FF to TF

0

to
(-1,0)

to
(1,0)

to
(-1,0)

to
(1,0)

(-1,0)
to
(-1,0)

to
(1,0)

2

(1,0)

(0,-1)

(1,0)
to
(0,-1)

(-1,0)
to
(0,-1)

A similar argument applied to this table shows that
\phi
is order preserving in all listed instances, so this concludes the case of horizontal increments. Next we fix
(m,n)
and treat vertical increments
(a,b)\leqslant(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
. Again we can assume
(m,n,a,b)
satisfies
a+b-k\equiv 0
mod
2
. We can summarize the
\epsilon
-image of this vertical increment in two tables, for
(m,n)
of type T or F respectively:

The argument for the first table applies equally well here, which explains that
\phi
is order-preserving on all vertical increments. Thus
\phi
is order-preserving, which finishes part 1.

For part 2, take
(m,n,a,b)\in\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
of type TT. Here we can also assume
a+b-k\equiv 0
mod
2
, since
\phi
-images for the case
a+b-k\equiv 1
mod
2
would only differ by a swap. We first consider the horizontal bi-increment square “
(m,n,a,b)
to
(m+1,n,a,b)
&
(m,n+1,a,b)
to
(m+1,n+1,a,b)
” in
\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}
. The type of this square is always “TT to TT & FT to TT” up to transpose, and the possible
\phi
-values are listed in the following table:

s\mod 3

-value of the square (up to transpose)

On the other hand, for the vertical bi-increment square “
(m,n,a,b)
to
(m,n,a+1,b)
&
(m,n,a,b+1)
to
(m,n,a+1,b+1)
”, its type should be “TT to TT & TF to TT” up to transpose, and the possible

From these we can conclude that part 2 holds true in full generality.
∎

Proposition B.4
.

Write
\mathsf{Aux}(\mathcal{C})\subset\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})
for the full subcategory spanned by functors
F\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{C}
so that
F(i,j)=0
if
i\neq j
, and the image of each square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i+1,j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i,j+1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i+1,j+1)}

is cartesian in
\mathcal{C}
. Then restrictions along the maps

\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime}=\{(0,0)\}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\{(0,-1)\leqslant(0,0)\}\to\mathcal{Q}
induce equivalences
\mathsf{Aux}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathsf{Aux}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})=\{F\colon\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}\mid F(0,-1)=0\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{Q}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Apply the same argument as in Theorem
4.3
.
∎

This
\mathsf{Aux}(\mathcal{C})
describes an interesting portion of
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proposition B.5
.

The precomposition functor
\phi^{*}\colon\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
restricts to a fully faithful functor
\phi^{*}\colon\mathsf{Aux}(\mathcal{C})\to 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
, and its essential image consists of
F\in 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
such that

1.

If
m+n+a+b\equiv 0

mod

3
, then
F(m,n,a,b)=0
.

2.

If
0\leqslant m-n\leqslant 1
,
0\leqslant a-b\leqslant 1
and
m+n+a+b\equiv 1

mod

3
, then
F(m,n,b+1,a)\leftarrow F(m,n,a,b)\to F(n+1,m,a,b)
are isomorphisms.

Also,
\phi^{*}\colon\mathsf{Aux}(\mathcal{C})\to 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
admits a left adjoint
\phi_{!}
and a right adjoint
\phi_{*}
.

Proof.

The image of
\mathsf{Aux}(\mathcal{C})\subset\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\phi^{*}}\mathsf{Fun}(\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C})
lies in
2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})
due to Lemma
B.3
. Furthermore, for
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}=\{(0,0),(1,0)\}\subset\mathcal{P}
, the restriction of
\phi\colon\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}\times\mathcal{P}^{\prime\prime}
factors through
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\{(0,-1),(0,0)\}\subset\mathcal{Q}
. This induces a commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Aux}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2\mathsf{DT}^{u}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Aux}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{[1]\times[1]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{*}}

in which the two vertical functors are equivalences by Theorem
4.12
and Proposition
B.4
. The lower horizontal functor is explicitly given as

\displaystyle\phi^{*}\colon\mathsf{Aux}^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})\quad\

\displaystyle\to\ \quad\mathcal{C}^{[1]\times[1]}
